### Brick overlays (round 321)

**Delivered:** the eight semi-transparent overlays for a plain Normal brick - Spinning,
Flashing, Breathing, Moving, Gravity, Fixed, Exploding and Spawner - imported as
`BrickNormal<Style>Overlay`, and worn in the field and on the bricks page in place of every
tint and glyph those styles had. A Tiny brick shares the Normal picture, being the same shape.
**Redrawn simpler in round 322** and dropped in under the same names at the same 56 by 28,
so no code changed. James: "other brick variants incoming once I'm happy with this set" - so
the 63 below wait on his sign-off of these eight.

**Still to draw, if wanted: 63 pictures**, listed by `PerStyleArtCostTests.testWhatBrickOverlaysAreStillToDraw`
from the game's own rules rather than typed here, so it moves when they do. Every silhouette one
of the eight can actually be carried by: Square and Big for the six styles that take any size,
Rounded and Rounded Square, the three Normal-only faces (Convex, Concave, Wedge), and Diamond and
Diamond Square. The names follow the portal glow's order - base, shape, size, then what it is -
so `BrickNormalSquareGravityOverlay` and `BrickNormalWedgeFixedOverlay` are the names the game
already looks for, and a picture dropped in under one is worn with no code change.

**Until one exists, a silhouette wears no overlay** - never the Normal picture stretched over a
shape it was not drawn for, which is round 274's Directional-panel lesson. Fixed, Exploding and
Spawner keep their old glyph there, because they look like any other brick until struck; the
five motions keep nothing, because the movement says it.

**One thing to decide before drawing the shaped ones:** a shaped brick is turned by flipping its
node, and its overlay is flipped with it, the way the portal glow is. That is fine for a pattern
with no direction and wrong for Gravity's downward V or Moving's chevrons, which would point up
or backwards on a flipped wedge or dome. Either those are drawn symmetrical, or drawn once per
orientation the way the wedge bricks themselves are (`Wedge0`, `Wedge90` and so on).

### One pixel, and only if you are in the file anyway

`PowerUpPortal` is **249x250** rather than 250x250, consistently at all three scales, so it
scales an honest 1:2:3 and round 271's check passed it. Every other power-up badge is square
to the pixel. At the 30-odd points a badge is actually drawn at this is invisible, and it is
recorded here only because `testTheArtworkIsSquare` had to be loosened from an equality to a
one-per-cent ratio to accommodate it (round 319a) - so if the file is ever open for another
reason, one pixel of width would let that assertion go back to being exact.

**All twenty-two redrawn** (James, round 314), bolder glyphs filling more of each tile.
Twenty-one went in at once; **`DroughtTwistIcon` took three deliveries** and is in as of round

`ArtStillToDrawTests.testEveryTwistBadgeHasTransparentCorners` measures the four corner
pixels of every badge at 3x from now on, so this cannot arrive unnoticed again. The bar is 40,
which clears both the anti-aliasing below it (Vanilla's 14, which looked like a hairline on a
contact sheet and is not one) and Drought's 177 above.

### Sound

**Wired ahead of the audio** (round 314, James: "for the sound, I will work on this. Are you
able to put in some place holder sounds. Are you able to also produce a list of new items
that need sounds"). Every event below now calls `playMayhemSound("<name>")`, and
`GameScene.mayhemSound` asks the bundle before building an `SKAction` - so a name with no
file behind it is **nil**, the event is silence and a haptic exactly as it is today, and a
recording dropped into `Megaball/Sounds` under that name plays with no code change and
nothing to ask for. The file names are the list:

| Sound | Fires when | File |
|---|---|---|
| Explosion | an Exploding brick goes off | `explosion.mp3` |
| Cull | half the field is destroyed at once | `cull.mp3` |
| Aura | the glow around a ball eats a brick | `aura.mp3` |
| Infill | six bricks arrive in empty cells | `infill.mp3` |
| Brick portal | the ball enters a Portal brick | `brickPortal.mp3` |
| Fixed brick locking | a Fixed brick anchors itself | `brickLocked.mp3` |
| Paddle portal | the ball leaves a paddle edge - also the mirror's and an aimed launch's | `paddlePortal.mp3` |
| Paddle halo | the halo destroys a brick | `paddleHalo.mp3` |
| Wrap-around | the paddle crosses a wall | `wrapAround.mp3` |
| Multi-Ball | an extra ball appears | `multiBall.mp3` |
| Safety paddle | the bar appears under the field | `safetyPaddle.mp3` |
| Mirror paddle | the second paddle appears | `mirrorPaddle.mp3` |
| ~~Drift~~ | the field steps sideways | **Settled, round 322:** `endlessRowDown.mp3`, the descent's own sound (James: "use the endlessRowDown sound for the drift power-up when bricks are moving to the side"). No `drift.mp3` is wanted |

Thirteen files for fifteen call sites: the three portal jumps share one sound, because they
are one event the player sees in three places. `MayhemSoundTests` holds the wiring safe while
the files do not exist, including a control that a sound which *does* ship is still found -
without it the whole set would pass just as well if the lookup were broken.

**No placeholder audio was generated.** Synthesised blips in a game this finished read worse
than silence, and each file also needs a hand-edited project entry; the plumbing is the half
that unblocks James, and the offer stands if he wants something audible before he records.

The original table, kept for what it says about where each one fires:

| Event | Today | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Explosion / Cull / Laser Beam / Infill | haptics only | `EndlessIIBehaviourBricks.endlessIIExplode`, `EndlessIIFieldPowerUps` |
| Portal jump (brick, paddle, wrap) | haptic only | `endlessIIEnterPortal`, `applyEndlessIIPaddlePortals`, `applyEndlessIIWraps` |
| Spawner refilling | none | `endlessIISpawn` |
| Gravity brick landing | none | the fallers tick in `EndlessIIBehaviourBricks` |
| Flashing brick turning solid | none | the flashing tick |
| Fixed brick anchoring | heavy haptic | `endlessIIAnchorIfNeeded` |
| Build-in rain / Clear And Retreat | `endlessRowDownSound` (borrowed) | `runEndlessIIBuildIn`, `endlessIICollectClearAndRetreat` |
| Power-up brick going off | `powerUpSound` (borrowed) | `endlessIITriggerPowerUpBrick` |
| Aimed catch / aimed launch | sticky catch / release sounds (borrowed) | `EndlessIIAimedSticky` |

The borrowed ones may be fine borrowed - the new ones from silence are the priority.

## 8.6 Constraints the implementation has to respect

Things that are not obvious from reading the code, each of which has already caused a bug.
Written down because they are the expensive kind of knowledge — every one cost a debugging
session, and none of them announces itself.

**A pinned sequence only guards the dates it contains.** The daily's history pin is the
enforcement of §2.1, and for a hundred rounds it held thirty August days while the twists most
able to rewrite history - the layout flips, which filter the *pool a roll indexes into* - could
not be drawn until September. It passed the whole time and guarded nothing about them. When a
feature activates on a date, the pin has to reach past that date or it is testing the era before
the feature existed. The same applies to anything whose range is derived rather than frozen:
`classicLevelCount` is computed from the pack tables, so it is the modulus of a roll that a
future level pack silently changes.

**A key-value store's key is a string, and a wrong one is silent.** `CloudKitHandler` reads
each array out of `NSUbiquitousKeyValueStore` by a string literal, and round 302 found one
naming a *different* array: the achievement percentages were read from the unlocked flags' key
and cast to `[String]`, which that key can never satisfy, so the merge branch had never run in
the app's life and every sync overwrote the cloud instead of merging with it. Nothing crashed
and nothing warned, because the code reads correctly - the variable is named after the array it
wants and only the literal disagrees. The convention that saves this is that **the key is the
variable's own name**, and `testEveryICloudArrayIsReadFromTheKeyNamedAfterIt` now asserts it
rather than trusting it. Two lessons beyond the fix: a dead branch's `!` is a crash waiting for
the day the branch comes alive, so fixing a key means auditing what the key was hiding; and the
*load* path deserves the same guard as the save path, because a wrong key there reads another
array straight into a player's stats.

**A correction has to run after the thing it corrects, and `SKAction`s run after `update`.**
SpriteKit's frame order is `update`, then action evaluation, then physics, then
`didSimulatePhysics`. Anything animated by an action - and Expand, Shrink and every paddle
resize are - has *not* moved yet when `update` runs, so a clamp written there is answering for
the node as it was one frame ago. Round 293 chased a laser turret that drifted a pixel or two
at the wall, diagnosed the cause exactly right, put the fix in `update`, and shipped a bug that
looked identical; round 300 moved the same code to `didEvaluateActions` and it went away. The
symptom of getting this wrong is always small and always persistent - one frame of an
animation, every frame of it - which is precisely why it survives two rounds of being looked at.

**One writer, one rule.** When a rule has to hold in more than one place, the second place calls
the first; it does not restate it. Round 293 added a per-frame wall clamp that carried its own
copy of the clamp arithmetic, and Wrap-Around - a power-up whose entire purpose is to *suspend*
that clamp - silently stopped working for seven rounds. Neither piece of code was wrong on its
own, which is what made it hard to see: the bug was that there were two of them. The same shape
has now bitten the queue entries (§12.0, a note is a claim about code that nothing re-checks)
and the two contradictory comments about `SKSpriteNode.size`. If a fact is worth writing twice,
write it once and call it twice.

**`SKScene` is an `SKEffectNode`.** So a whole-scene filter costs `filter` and
`shouldEnableEffects` and nothing else - no wrapper node, no reparenting. This is worth knowing
because the obvious implementation of a full-screen effect is to wrap the world in an
`SKEffectNode`, and that moves every child into a new coordinate space: a brick's `position.y`
is its row, and the descent and the bottom-row check both read it. Round 300 built Blackout in
two lines on the strength of this, having first scoped the reparenting version and found it
touching the one invariant this document exists to protect.

**A brick wearing a face is not the size it looks.** `makeRounded` and `makeFace` shrink the
sprite so it disappears behind the face they build, and from then on `brick.size` is the
*hiding rectangle* rather than the cell. Two things read it and were wrong: `endlessIISizeOf`,
which had a rounded Square brick measuring 1.56 cells against a threshold of 1.5 - right by a
twentieth of a cell and right by accident; and the save, which wrote the shrunk size while the
restore applied the style again from it, so **a rounded brick came back 22% smaller on every
resume**, compounding, and the shaped faces did the same. The face's *path* is the thing that
still knows the cell, which is what `endlessIIFieldSize` reads. And the anchor moves with the
size, because a sprite shrinks towards its anchor point and a Square brick's is on its top edge
- `(0.5 - anchorPoint) * size` is the invariant every one of these has to hold, because it is
where the drawing sits, and the face, the multi-hit bar and the resumed body all read it.

**A node built in code to stand in for a node built in the scene file inherits nothing
from it.** The scene file anchors `paddleSticky` and `paddleLaser` at (0.5, 0) - they
stand on the line they are placed on and grow upward over the paddle - and every sprite
made in code is anchored in its middle. Round 313 made the same slip three times in one
round: the safety bar's sticky strip, and both of the strips a split paddle's overlay
rebuilds, each sitting half its own height too low. Copying texture, size, zPosition and
cap rect reads as thorough and is not, because the anchor is the one property that
decides *where* the copy is. **Copy the anchor, or place the copy by an edge rather than
by a centre.** Two more reasons this hides: nothing is clipped or missing, so it looks
like a small art mistake rather than a bug; and a `GameScene` built in a test rather
than loaded from the .sks has the code default for these, so the property under test is
already wrong and a test that does not set it deliberately can neither see the fault nor
prove the fix. Every one of these was found by rendering the scene and looking at it.

**A snapshot for one contact has to be cleared by that contact.** `endlessIISpendPaddleTurns`
takes three - the portal paddle's, Auto-Aim's and Aimed Sticky's - so a clock expired by its own
last turn still delivers what that turn bought. Aimed Sticky's is also read by
`endlessIIFieldIsHeld`, and it was only ever cleared on the catch that found the clock already
stopped. So the *first* landing of the power-up set it, nothing put it back, and the field
stopped descending for the rest of the run (play-test round 275: "the bricks stopped descending
down, even with the bottom row empty. I had to pause and unpause"). It read as a pause bug for
the same reason round 169's did - resuming resets the ball, and the reset clears the flag. The
hold that *should* stop the field while the player aims is `endlessIIAimHold`, which begins at
the catch and ends at the launch.

**There is one paddle top and one paddle underside, and `paddleHeight` is neither.** It is the
*plain* paddle's height; a shaped paddle is up to half as tall again with its node lifted so the
underside stays put, so `paddle.position.y + paddleHeight/2` names a line inside the dome rather
than the surface the ball meets. Eleven places worked it out by hand. Round 259 fixed the laser
and sticky overlays, round 275 the retro theme's three layers, and round 278 found a third copy
of both in the *resume* path - a run resumed wearing a shape came back with its overlays and its
retro dress misplaced, then quietly corrected itself the first time the player moved, which is
why nobody ever reported it. `paddleTopY` and `paddleUndersideY` are the answer now.

**The placement sites use them; the judgement sites deliberately do not.** Four expressions of
that shape are not placements but *decisions* - whether a ball has landed on top, whether a
sticky catch has arrived, the square paddle's branch - and moving those lines up would make a
shaped paddle catch fewer balls off the flank of its own dome. That is a change to how the
power-up plays rather than to where a picture sits, and it belongs in a play test rather than in
a sweep. They are listed in `PaddleSurfaceTests`.

**A layer that follows the paddle has to follow its underside.** A shaped paddle is taller and
its node rises by half the growth so the underside stays on the line it was on - so anything
tracking `paddle.position.y` rises with it. Round 259 found this for the laser and sticky
overlays; round 275 found the retro theme's three extra layers doing it too, which read as "2
paddle graphics at the same time with one laid over another". They are drawn at one size for
every shape, so pinning them to the underside is the whole fix.

**The paddle's top overlay is shared between Sticky and Grip.** Since round 261 it is one node
wearing one of two pictures, and retro hides only the *sticky* one - "its regular sticky texture
is quite different from the other paddles, but its grip texture is in the same style" (James).
Three places still hid the node whenever the theme was retro, written when it could only ever
wear the sticky picture, and one of them runs on the power-up HUD's own tick - so a retro paddle
wearing the grip had it taken away and put back several times a second.

**`FadingLine`'s `thickness` and `blur` are not independent.** They add together before the
picture is sized, so a thin core and a wide blur gets a *thick* core inside a very wide picture.
That is right for the trajectory, which is a core layer plus a deliberately larger glow layer
beneath it, and it is the trap for anything else: round 276 handed an `SKShapeNode`'s `lineWidth`
and `glowWidth` straight across and drew a line three times too wide. For a plain soft line,
pass the core as `thickness` and leave `blur` near zero - the picture is already about three
times the core tall with the outer two thirds falling off, which is a glowing line for free.

**And a number that matched arithmetically did not match visually.** The aim line's alpha floor
was 0.3 as an `SKShapeNode`, whose glow adds brightness on top of its stroke; the same 0.3 on a
sprite with no such bonus washed out to olive where the old line stayed green. It is 0.6 now,
chosen by drawing the old line beside four candidates and looking at them. The arithmetic answer
and the one that looks the same were different answers, and only the render could say so.

**An `SKShapeNode` handed a path re-tessellates, and a glowing one costs an offscreen pass.**
Round 258 found the trajectory line doing both 46 times a frame per ball and fixed it there. The
power-up **timer rings** were doing the same thing and were not looked at: two shape nodes per
*running* power-up, one of them glowing, both handed a freshly built path on every frame for the
whole of its life. Which is the likeliest reading of James's stutter list - "Drift, Shaped
paddles, Trajectory line, Quicksand" is not four unrelated mechanics, it is four **timed**
power-ups, and the cost is per ring rather than per power-up. `PowerUpRingHUD.hasTurned` now
skips the redraws that land in the same place: 75% of them over ten seconds, 92% over thirty,
and 99% for a segmented ring, which only ever shows whole segments.

Neither this nor anything else measured is *proven* to be the stutter - what is proven is that a
shaped paddle's own per-frame tick is indistinguishable from a plain paddle's (median 0.36ms
against 0.35ms, identical 99th percentiles, and the worst single frame belonging to the plain
one). The trace cache from round 258 holds: zero retraces in 120 frames of a moving shaped
paddle.

~~**One thing measured and not acted on**~~ - **built in round 280**, on James's word. The
report that settled it was "ball is still stuttering with shaped paddles active, **even if it's
the only active power-up**", which rules out everything that scales with how many are running.

A shaped paddle's body was traced from the picture's alpha at the size it is drawn, so the
surface the ball met was a staircase: 20 steps for the dome, 26 for the dish, 32 for the wave,
each up to two points high, against a plain paddle's flat top. `PaddleOutline` reads the same
silhouette from the *full-resolution* artwork to sub-pixel precision - where the alpha crosses a
half between two rows - smooths it, and cuts it into twenty convex strips. Measured against the
trace on the same picture at the same twenty places, the computed surface scores 4.2 against
12.0 for the dome, 11.4 against 16.0 for the dish and 24.5 against 25.0 for the wave, whose
roughness is mostly its own two bumps.

**The plain paddle keeps its traced body.** A flat top has no staircase to remove, and it has
bounced Classic and Endless for years; changing it to prove a point about a Mayhem power-up
would be the wrong trade.

Three things this cost, all worth writing down. The first attempt sampled a window exactly one
strip wide, so neighbouring boundaries shared no pixels - not smoothing but sampling in blocks,
and it measured *rougher* than the trace it was meant to beat. The second used a flat average,
which smooths by flattening, and what it flattened hardest were the extremes: the dish's
shoulders came out nearly two points below the picture, which is the body sitting inside the
art. A triangular kernel does both jobs. And the bar itself was wrong three times - "much
smoother" was tried at 0.6 and failed for a dome that rises fast at its ends, a dish that turns
hard into its corners, and a wave whose bumps are the shape rather than the sampling. What is
asserted now is that it beats the trace on every shape and lands on the picture; the ratios are
printed rather than fixed, because bending the reading of a picture to hit an invented number is
how a body stops matching its art.

**A face's hiding rectangle has to be inside the *picture*, not just inside the geometry.** The
sprite behind a shaped face is shrunk into a rectangle chosen to sit within the silhouette, and
those rectangles were worked out against `EndlessIIFaceGeometry` - whose notch is a deliberately
shallow tenth of the height, because "a deeper notch stops reading as a dish and starts reading
as two bricks with a gap". James's drawn concave cuts a V nearly to the floor. So the sprite was
tucked under the silhouette's notch and standing in the open under the painted one, and it read
as a bar floating in the middle of the brick (play-test, round 280, with a screenshot). Both the
notch's and the tent's rectangles sit on the brick's floor now, which is the one place every one
of these faces is solid in both.

~~**The disagreement underneath it is still open.**~~ **Settled in round 282**, and James chose
the art: "match the concave body to the graphic of the concave brick I supplied, not the body you
created prior. The geometry needs to deepen to match the art."

So `concaveNotch` is a *measurement* now rather than a judgement. `PaddleOutline.edges` - the
sub-pixel edge finder built for the paddle in round 280 - reads the top of `BrickNormalConcave`
and `BrickMultiHit1Concave` bottoming out at 0.260 of the picture's height, which is 0.240 below
its middle. It was `+0.10`; it is `-0.24`.

Both of the old objections were answered rather than overruled. "A notch cut past the centre
would leave the sprite nowhere to hide" stopped being true in round 280, when the hiding
rectangle moved to the brick's floor - below the notch the silhouette is full width. And whether
a deep notch "stops reading as a dish and starts reading as two bricks with a gap" was a
judgement about a drawing, which is now James's drawing.

*A note on the measurement:* two of the four pictures read as having no notch at all, and both
are ones whose art carries a border around its own square canvas - which makes every column
opaque at the very top and defeats an alpha edge finder. That is the same border that draws as a
hairline around every diamond brick, which James has said he will crop later.

**`SKPhysicsBody.area` is in square metres, and SpriteKit is 150 points to the metre.** A
75 x 18 point paddle reports `0.06`, not `1350`. Round 277 read it as points, saw `0.000` for
every shape, concluded a traced body reports no area at all, and wrote that down as the reason
it could not settle what resolution the tracer samples at. The number was there the whole time -
multiplied out it is 1274 square points, 94% of the paddle's box, which is exactly a rounded
rectangle. It is a good check that a polygon body was *accepted*: a piece wound the wrong way
still returns an object, and area is what tells the difference. Round 280 uses it to say the
five computed outlines come to 94, 82, 76, 63 and 58 per cent of their cells, which are the
right fractions for a rounded rectangle, a dome, a wave, a wedge and a dish.

**`didBegin` reports once per *fixture pair*, and a bounce is one event.** This has now bitten
three times in three different places: the power-up HUD taking two segments off a shaped paddle
hit (round 259), Randomised Bounce drawing a fresh angle two or three times for one strike
(round 283), and it is why every concave brick - two convex halves - and every brick seam is a
double report waiting to happen. The fix each time is the same shape: a per-ball, per-frame
guard keyed on `frameNumber`, held at the *one gate* everything already asks rather than at each
call site. `paddleLandingFrame` and `randomisedBounceFrame` are the two that exist.

**A tint at full blend replaces the picture, it does not shade it.** `colorBlendFactor = 1`
is not "colour this in", it is "draw this colour instead", and the texture underneath stops
being visible at all. Directional bricks were painted grey that way from the day they were
built, so a directional Multi-hit and a directional Indestructible were the same grey oblong
and the side deliberately left open showed grey along with everything else - which is round
284's "the open side should show the brick underneath". Anything that wants to *mark* a brick
rather than replace it needs a child node over it, which is what the panel art is.

**An exponential approach cannot overshoot, at any tuning.** A follow written as "close a fixed
share of the gap each frame" comes in from one side and slows down for ever - it is a first-order
system and the shape of its response is fixed. So a play-test note asking for momentum past the
target and a swing back cannot be answered by changing the share, however long the number is
argued over; it needs a second-order system, which means the thing being moved has to carry a
velocity of its own. Ball Control is the one that has been rebuilt this way (round 284), and the
same reasoning applies to anything else here that follows something: Magnetism, the aim, the
steering lead. **And a spring has to be integrated in fixed slices**: taken once per frame it
gains energy when the frame is long, which is not "feels different at 120fps" but "ball leaves
the field".

**A `GameScene` is an expensive thing to make, and a test that makes a hundred will fail
strangely.** The scene holds on the order of two hundred `SKTexture` stored properties, all
loaded from the asset catalogue at init. Building one per level to walk the hundred and ten
levels exhausted CoreUI and threw `-[CUINamedRenditionInfo bufferId]: unrecognized selector`,
which names nothing to do with the cause and looks like a fault in the level being built when it
happens. The same shape of error - `-[__NSArrayI_Transfer bufferId]` - has appeared twice before
in this project when a test class ran alone. **Make one scene and reuse it**, clearing whatever
the last iteration added; round 296's flip measurement went from a crash to 13 seconds that way.

**`SKSpriteNode.size` carries the node's scale.** A sprite built 100 wide and set to `xScale`
1.5 reports a size of 150, and its `frame` agrees. So Expand and Shrink, which animate the scale
and never assign a size, change what `paddle.size.width` reports - and anything that clamps,
measures or divides by it is already correct. Round 293 believed the opposite, on the strength of
a file comment, and broke the wall clamp by multiplying the scale in a second time. **Two
comments in the same file contradicted each other about it**, which is the tell: where two notes
disagree, neither is evidence, and the sprite is three lines away in a test.

**A durable value a test writes is a value a killed test leaves behind** (round 319c). The
daily's test-day offset is a `UserDefaults` integer that only a test ever sets, and the test
that sets it saved the old value and restored it in a `defer`. That is correct code and it is
not enough: a killed process runs no `defer`, the relaunch trap above kills one often enough
that this file calls one per full run normal, and the value it dies holding is durable. Then
the leak feeds itself, because the next run reads the leaked value as "the old value" and
dutifully restores it. This machine was carrying a 1, so every date-dependent test in the suite
had been running a day ahead of the calendar for an unknown number of rounds without failing
anything - and the app installed from the same build read the same default, which is how it
surfaced: the daily on the phone and the daily in a render disagreed, and a daily that
disagrees with itself is the one fault §2.1 exists to prevent. Half an hour went into proving
it was not that.

The general form: **a test may not write anything that outlives the process.** Where it has to
write, give the code an injectable store and point the test at an in-memory one, which is what
`DailyChallengeSession.clockStore` is for. And note the contrast the same round provides: the
launch argument rig from round 199 writes nothing at all, so winding the clock by hand leaves
nothing to forget - the durable path was the one built for convenience.

**A test that has never finished a full run is a claim about the code too** (round 319a).
Rounds 315 to 318 each ran their own classes, passed, and were committed; the last full suite
was round 316's and its result was never read. It was holding 27 assertion failures, and two
of them were faults in round 318 that **round 318's own new test had already caught** - the
test was correct, it was written for exactly that fault, and it had never been part of a run
that reached the end. Targeted runs are the right tool while working and they cannot see
across rounds: three of the six failures were tests pinning a look James changed two rounds
later, which by definition no single round's own classes would run. So a round is not finished
when its own tests pass, and the suite's result has to be **read**, not merely started.

**A note about what is left is a claim about the code, and nothing fails when it rots.** Five
"still owed" entries in §12.0 were found built while chasing them, across rounds 286 to 292: the
Wipe's half of the Lock, the twin's portal, three of the workbook's four leftovers, the Endless
2.0 leaderboards and the grip artwork. Every one described work that a later round had done
without striking the note. Tests catch a stale *decision*; nothing at all catches a stale
sentence about the future, and the document is the queue. **And before writing a test to close
one, look for the test that is already there** - round 292 wrote a duplicate of a class forty
lines further down the same file, and reported it passing off a run that had never executed it
(the neighbouring class runs the same number of tests on its own). So: **the round that closes an item
strikes the entry in the same commit**, and an item picked up off the queue is checked against
the code before any work starts on it - which is how three of these five were found.

**Round 313 found four more, and then swept for them instead of waiting.** Three turned up by
accident in two rounds - §12.2 still calling the ring HUD blocked, three rounds committed with
no row at all, and a taste call never written down - which is a pattern rather than bad luck.
The sweep that found the fourth is mechanical and takes seconds, so run it rather than reading:
**every backticked identifier in every row of this table, checked against the whole Swift source
and the asset catalogue.** 783 distinct tokens, 22 without a match, and most of those 22 are the
table working correctly - a row saying "X became Y" has to name X. What is worth looking at is a
row claiming in the *present tense* that a symbol exists. That is how `pendingLegendSupport` was
found: a row saying Millrace was deliberately held out of the generator's pool awaiting builder
support that had, in fact, been built, while Millrace had been shipping to players for dozens of
rounds. **A note that describes shipped behaviour as withheld is worse than one that describes
finished work as owed**, because the second only wastes a round and the first hides a feature.

**"Speeding up and slowing down" means uneven frames, not slow ones.** The ball's speed is
held exactly constant every frame (`holdBallSpeeds`), so what varies when frames vary is how far
it travels in each one - which reads as acceleration and braking rather than as lag. A report
phrased that way is pointing at something *lumpy*: an allocation, a render, a texture upload
happening on the frame rather than once. Round 291 spent two earlier rounds on a shaped paddle
that was never the cause, because the shape was what happened to be running.

**Build a texture once.** `SKTexture(image:)` measures at **9.8ms** - it is a GPU upload, and
the innocent-looking line that does it was inside a HUD rebuilt sixty times a second. Anything
per-frame that reaches for an image, a `UIGraphicsImageRenderer` or a `CGPath` wants a cache
above it, and the cache wants an `@autoclosure` so the thing it is caching is never built on the
frames it is not needed. `PowerUpIcon.ringTexture` is the pattern.

**And a cache is not a warm-up** (round 321). Round 291 cached the ring HUD's pictures and took
the per-frame cost from 30.8ms to nothing - and every *first* build stayed exactly where it had
been: 10.68ms measured, in the frame of the catch, once for each kind a run had not caught yet.
From the player's side that is indistinguishable from the original stutter arriving only once
per power-up, which is why it came back as "the first power up of a game". A cache answers
"how much does this cost every frame"; it says nothing about *which* frame pays the first time,
and the first time is always the busiest frame there is - a drop landing, a catch, a new row.
Anything built lazily on a gameplay event wants building before play instead, off the main thread
where it is more than a few milliseconds, and the list it is built from wants to be the same list
the lazy path reads (`PowerUpIcon.ringArt`), or the two will drift the first time a picture is
renamed. `SKTexture(imageNamed:)` is the same trap in its quietest form: it decodes on first
*draw*, so round 84's preload of what falls did nothing for the tray icon a catch shows.

**A number nobody has measured drifts, and the drift outlives its reason.** Two power-ups read
as doing nothing in round 283 and both were correctly wired; what was wrong was scale. Ball
Spin's full grip needed 1400 points a second on a field 400 points across - three and a half
crossings in one second, a speed no thumb produces - so an ordinary swipe earned a quarter of
the intended curve. Randomised Bounce's spread had been narrowed twice while chasing a vibrating
ball that turned out to be round 258's sign bug, and once that was fixed nobody put it back: a
tenth of the room moved a 45-degree bounce by 3.5 degrees, which is *less* than the player
already gets from hitting the paddle nearer one end. Neither had ever been made to state its
effect in degrees. Both do now, in `testWhatBallSpinAndRandomBounceActuallyDo`.

**And the fourth time the number was measured in the wrong place** (round 319). The daily's twist
mix was tuned against `DailyChallengeGenerator.rawChallenge(forKey:)`, which is the draw, and
players are handed `challenge(forKey:)`, which is the draw with the no-repeats rule applied to
it. `stepped` re-rolls a whole day that reads like the day before, so the two distributions are
genuinely different and only the second one exists as far as anybody playing is concerned. Every
new test passed, every printed number was real, and all of them described a calendar nobody would
ever see. What caught it was an *old* test failing on a number that did not reconcile with the
new ones, which is the argument for not deleting a test whose thresholds a change has moved.

The general form: **when tuning against a measurement, measure the function the caller actually
calls.** A generator with anything layered on top of it - a filter, a correction, a retry, a
clamp - has an inner distribution that is not the delivered one, and the inner one is usually the
easier to reach from a test.

**And the third time it was not the number but the quantity** (round 313). Ball Spin was
reported a third time as doing nothing without a moving paddle - after round 305 had answered
exactly that report. Its still-paddle curve was taken from *where the ball struck*: nothing dead
centre, a third of a flick's turn at the very edge. Measured for the first time, that is 0
degrees in the middle and 5.4 over a whole flight a quarter of the way out, and a player holding
the paddle still is a player who has put it under the ball. The scale was fine; the thing being
measured was not the thing being modelled. The slide a ball makes across a surface is its own
horizontal speed, not its landing spot - and once that was said out loud the old term turned out
to have the *sign* wrong too, curving along the slide where friction acts against it. So: when a
power-up is reported as doing nothing for a second time, suspect the quantity before the
constant, and make it state its effect in the units the player sees before changing either.

**A brick's `position.y` is its row.** The descent moves by it and the bottom-row check that
gates new-row generation reads it. A brick whose position is anywhere but its row centre is
cleared away at the wrong moment, or sits in the last row blocking generation for ever. Big
bricks keep their node on a row centre and express their size through an anchor point and an
offset physics body for exactly this reason.

**The descent moves by `brickHeight`, not by each brick's own height.** Those were the same
number while every brick was one cell. A brick of any other size drifts out of step with the
field it belongs to.

**Nothing runs a repeating `SKAction` on a brick.** `countBricks()` decides whether a row move
is underway by asking every brick `hasActions()`, and a new row is only generated once nothing
is moving. A brick with a permanent action answers yes for ever and the field stops descending.
Spinning, flashing, falling and wandering are all driven from `update` instead.

**`SKSpriteNode.size` is backed by floats.** A width assigned straight from `brickWidth` does
not read back as `brickWidth`, so `size.width == brickWidth` is always false. Compare with a
tolerance. This silently disabled two whole features once.

**A velocity written before `addChild` is lost.** A body attached to a node that is not yet
in a physics world does not keep the velocity it is given - the write silently vanishes when
the node arrives in the world. Cluster's burst hung motionless in the air until the release
set velocities *after* `addChild` (round 179). Position survives; velocity does not.

**One collision can be reported as more than one contact.** The block and the frame both
answer to a ball, and a manifold can carry several points - so a handler that does something
*unidempotent* per `didBegin` does it twice for one hit. Two wrap teleports are a round trip
that looks exactly like a bounce (round 177). Note the subject once per frame, and make the
response ask whether it still applies.

**A physics body cannot be moved from `didBegin`.** SpriteKit calls contact handlers in the
middle of simulating the step, and a position written there is overwritten as the step
resolves. Record the intent and apply it in `didSimulatePhysics`.

**`colorBlendFactor` is modulated by the texture.** Colourising a bright texture gives the
colour; colourising a dark one gives a dark, muddy version of it. Tinting only works on the
plain brick artwork.

**`CGMutablePath.addArc` joins to the current point.** Drawing a segmented ring without a
`move(to:)` before each arc fills every gap with a connecting line and the ring reads solid.

**The side blocks are the walls.** They fill whatever the play area leaves over, and a wall of
zero width gets no physics body — which the next line force-unwraps. They take a minimum
thickness that extends off-screen, so the play area can reach the edge.

**Cells can be part full.** Four Tiny bricks share one cell, so "is this cell occupied" is the
wrong question — a cell with one quarter left in it is not a wall. Ask how full it is.

**A style has to be in a pool to exist.** `EndlessIIStyle` is the list of styles, and
`applyEndlessIIStyles` is offered a *pool* per call site — the appearance ones and the
field-changing ones. Fixed was added to the enum, to the compatibility grid, to the reference
page and to the progression, and to neither pool, so no brick could ever be given it. From the
outside that is indistinguishable from a style that is simply very rare, which is why it went
unnoticed for so long.

**Adding a power-up lengthens arrays in two places, not one.** Every per-power-up array exists
twice: in the stats file on disk and in `NSUbiquitousKeyValueStore`. Both hold whatever the last
version to write them had. `TotalStats.padded` covers the file; the cloud copy was missed, and
the twenty-ninth power-up crashed on the first launch after the update — every merge loop in
`CloudKitHandler` walked the local array's length while indexing the stored one. The same is
true of achievements, levels, packs and themes. Loops there are now bounded by the shorter of
the two in both directions, and a test reads the source to check no new one slips in.

**A contact reports the velocity *after* the bounce.** SpriteKit calls the contact handler
partway through resolving the step, so a ball's velocity read there is the one it is leaving
with, not the one it arrived with. Anything that needs the approach — which face was struck,
which way a Portal should send the ball on — has to sample it in `update`, before the physics
runs. `ballStateBeforeStep` is that sample.

**Reflecting a reported velocity bounces it twice.** Following from the note above: the wall
and ceiling handlers negated a velocity the engine had already turned round, so the ball was
sent back into the surface it had just left. At the ceiling that is a ball running along the
top of the screen; at a side wall it is the sideways part of a shallow approach being lost.
Both now reflect the *approach*, sampled before the step.

**Adjacent bricks have a seam.** Every brick is its own body, so a ball landing exactly on the
join between two of them is resolved against both at once and leaves off a corner rather than
off the flat face. `BrickSeamBounce` catches it: two bricks struck in one step are treated as
one surface. Anything else that gives one ball several brick contacts in a step needs to think
about the same thing.

---

## 9. Out of scope for the first version

- **A separate descent-pressure mode** — where the field reaching the paddle is the core
  threat. A different game; parked as a future mode.
- **iPad-specific layout** beyond the fixed ratio.

### 9.3 Pause and resume
**In scope.** A run cannot be abandoned and returned to from within the app, but it must
survive pausing *and* the app being quit — the same guarantee Classic has. The save format needs the ball array (§5.5), active power-up state including Lock, the
generated field as it stands, and the phase and schedule position so generation continues
coherently. Generation does not need to be reproducible from a seed - the field is stored
as it is today, and only one phase is planned ahead.

**Built.** Every ball beyond the first is written as four values — x, y, dx, dy — in one flat
array beside the first ball's five. The fifth value there is the paddle's x, which belongs to
the game rather than to a ball and is written once.

- The field is **optional**, so every save written before Multi-Ball existed still decodes.
  Those restore with no extras, which is what they had.
- A save is a file on disk that an older build or a bad write may have left in any state, and
  it is read at launch, where a trap is a crash on opening the app. So a ragged array — one
  not a whole number of balls — fails the consistency check and the save is discarded rather
  than read past the end, and extras claiming no first ball to be extra to are rejected the
  same way.
- No more balls come back than §5.5 allows, capped both when writing and when reading.
- Velocities are restored the way the first ball's are: into the paused-velocity store, so
  the countdown runs with the field still, and every ball starts moving at the moment play
  does. Pausing is what takes the velocities off the field, so each ball needs somewhere of
  its own to keep one — without that the extras came back stationary and dropped straight
  down, which is three balls lost to opening the pause menu.

---

## 10. Decisions

Settled in review, recorded so they are not re-argued.

| Question | Decision |
|---|---|
| Lives | **One**, exactly as Endless |
| Score | **Height alone** |
| Name | **Endless 2.0** for now |
| Ring HUD | **Endless II only** initially; may extend to other modes later |
| Multi-Ball | **In** |
| Explosion chaining | **Yes**, chains |
| Introduction schedule length | Tuned by play-testing. **Must not show everything in one run** |
| Channels | **Replaced** by narrow conflict groups (§5.1) |
| Level data refactor | **After** Endless II |
| Complete Level, Extra Ball | **Excluded** from Endless II's drop table |
| Multi-Ball's name | **Kept** — the collision went with Extra Ball |
| Laser Beam with several balls | **One beam per ball** |

## 11. Still open

1. **Does Tiny survive?** Kept unless sub-cell sizing turns out to be a rewrite rather
   than an addition — the call comes when §8.1 is built, and Big alone still gives the size
   axis if it goes.
2. **Rarity tuning.** How, rather than whether: §6.4.

---

## 12. Build phases

Each phase ends in something playable, so the mode can be judged as it grows rather than
only at the end. Nothing in a later phase is a prerequisite for testing an earlier one.

| Phase | What lands | What you can test |
|---|---|---|
| **1. The mode exists** ✅ | Endless 2.0 on the main menu, playing exactly as Endless does today, with its own stats and leaderboard | That it launches, plays and scores - and that Endless and Classic are untouched |
| **2. The ring HUD** ✅ | Only-active power-ups, ring timers, in Endless 2.0 only | Whether the ring reads better than the tray, side by side with the old one |
| **3. Simple bricks** ✅ | Spinning, Flashing, Rounded - no grid changes needed | Whether they read clearly and whether Flashing is fair |
| **4. Sizes** ✅ | Big and Tiny, on the brick grid | Whether Tiny is worth its cost, which is the open question in §11 |
| **5. Behavioural bricks** ✅ | Gravity, Moving, Directional, Exploding, Spawner, Portal | Whether explosions and cascades feel good or chaotic |
| **6. Generation** ✅ | Phases, gentle opening, the **style progression** below, and the power-up introduction schedule | The heart of it: whether a run feels varied and whether the pacing works |
| **7. Multi-Ball** | The collection of balls, and the run continuing while one survives | Performance with four balls, and whether it is as fun as it sounds |
| **8. New power-ups** | In batches, simplest first: vision, then paddle, then rules-changing | Each batch on its own, which is the only way to tune rarity |
| **9. Presentation** (code side ✅) | Scrolling backgrounds, icons, the information page | The finish. The backdrop machinery, the info pages and the reference content are built and current; what remains is §8.5's asset list - real icons, brick artwork, the mode's own menu mark, the backdrop tile, sounds - and the Quick Start Guide |

Phases 3 to 5 can be reordered freely - they are independent. Phase 6 is where the mode
stops being Endless with extra bricks and starts being its own thing, so it is worth
reaching before judging whether the whole idea works.

### 12.0 Where this has got to

Phases 1 to 6 are built. Everything below is what remains, in the order it is worth doing —
all of it presentation and reference material rather than mechanics.

**Built since:** the background selection screen and the brick types page. Both are new UI
rather than mechanics.

- **Background selection.** The settings row opened nothing and cycled a name; three of the
  four backgrounds are shades of the same purple, so the name said nothing about what had been
  chosen. It now opens a screen that swipes between scale models of the game scene, one per
  background, drawn at the device's own proportions. What a background *is* moved to
  `GameBackground` and the playfield's proportions to `GameSceneLayout`, so the model and the
  scene cannot disagree — the layout maths the scene has always used now has a home that can
  be tested, and the 1.8236 ratio is asserted across a spread of screens.
- **Brick types page.** A reference page in the shape of the power-ups one, with sections for
  the five behaviours, the ten styles and the three sizes. The compatibility lines are derived
  from `EndlessIIStyle` rather than written out, so the page cannot fall behind the game; the
  icons reproduce the placeholder look described in §8.5 deliberately, and this is where the
  page will start using the real artwork when it exists.

**Phase 7 is built.** Multi-Ball drops, adds a ball up to four, and the run continues while any
of them is in play.

- `EndlessIIBalls` holds the rules; `EndlessIIMultiBall` holds the collection. `ball` never
  changes identity — when it is the one lost, a survivor hands over its position and velocity,
  so nothing else in the scene has to know a swap happened. That is what keeps Classic and
  Endless untouched.
- Every ball gets its own contact handling: its own wall bounces, paddle angles, brick
  corrections and portal jumps. Speed, size and texture are shared across the set (§5.5).
- The drop weight falls to zero while four are in play, so it stops being offered rather than
  being collected for nothing.
- **Sticky Paddle holds every ball**, in the order they were caught: one leaves per tap, oldest
  first, and the first ball waits its turn in the same queue - so a first ball caught last goes
  last. That is the per-ball launch state §5.5 asked for, for the power-up that exists today;
  Aimed Sticky will inherit the same queue.
- The save format carries every ball, so a run paused with four resumes with four (§9.3).

Everything a power-up does reaches every ball. Giga-Ball reached all four textures and one
physics body, so three of them looked the part and bounced like ordinary balls; the same is
true of ball size, which is a scale rather than a size and has to be copied as one.

Phase 7 is complete.

**What play-testing found, and what it was.** Recorded because each of these was written when
there was only ever one ball, and the next thing built on top of the collection will meet the
same class of bug:

- A bounce off nothing in the gap below the field was the paddle being handed back to a ball
  still overlapping it. The paddle is taken out of a ball's way while the ball is underneath
  (so a Backstop can work), and a contact reported in that moment is not a landing.
- The resume countdown left the other balls running: pausing zeroed the first ball's velocity
  once per ball in play and left the rest travelling. Every ball now keeps its own heading
  across the pause and shows its own direction marker while the countdown runs.
- Losing several balls within a frame or two spent balls that were never in play, and handed
  the first ball the position of another ball that was itself about to be lost. The handover
  now takes the highest survivor, and a ball already retired is not counted twice.

**Since the last round of play-testing:** the power-up brick draws from the same eligibility
rules as the falling ones (`powerUpCanAppear`, which the drop path now asks too rather than
answering one case at a time inside its own switch); Tiny bricks ramp with height rather than
appearing at a flat rate from the first metre; and the opening field arrives the way the field
moves in play - each row entering at the top and stepping down, in lockstep, rather than each
brick fading into its own place.

**Built in play-test rounds 16 and 17**, all presentation:

- **One pop-up style.** The app had two: its own dark blurred sheet, and `UIAlertController`,
  which newer screens reached for because it is one line. `GigaBallAlert` is the app's own,
  presented as a *child view controller* so it sits inside the pause menu's blur instead of
  sliding a white card over it, and the storyboard's warning sheet was restyled to match
  rather than replaced - everything it does hangs off actions and notifications that work.
- **The daily's twists explain themselves on the pause screen**, not the briefing card: the
  briefing prints each blurb under its twist already, and the pause screen shows only an icon
  and a name, which is exactly where "what does Fog of War do again" gets asked.
- **The daily card is two containers**, details and posted score, in a column - so the result
  is its own block and still scrolls with the day.
- **A signed-out player is told, quietly.** `GameCenterHandler.notSignedInNote` is one line in
  one place, shown under the daily's title and at the foot of a finished run's score block.
  Not an alert: nothing is broken and nothing is lost, so it wears the same grey as the rest
  of the small print.
- **The lives line follows the score in the daily**, where there is no high score printed - a
  label with no text still holds its place, which had left "Last ball" stranded a third of a
  screen below the number it belongs to.
- **Classic's menu icon is the app icon, round-masked**, drawn from the icon preview art at
  runtime so it cannot fall out of step with the icon itself.

The tints are gone, including the anchored Fixed colour, and a brick wearing an overlay has its colour blend cleared, which also cleans up older saves that recorded Flashing's green before the resume re-applies the styles. The helper now clears anything else setting the icon's top edge; `MenuHeaderSwapTests` builds that arrangement and checks one top and a sane layout. **(d) The rest of the log, read rather than acted on.** The phantom-brick line ("dying for 4.9s") is the safety net working: the sweep runs every frame with a half-second threshold measured in `CACurrentMediaTime()`, which keeps counting through a pause, so the figure is the pause and not 4.9 seconds of a solid invisible brick in play. The "unexplained" crooked-ball bends to exactly 180 degrees sit at the ceiling and against brick faces, which fits SpriteKit's inelastic threshold zeroing a grazing contact's normal velocity below about 150 pt/s on a ball already touching - no fresh `didBegin`, so no contact noted - and the horizontal escape repairs each one the next frame; the two 67 pt/s speed wobbles fit the same mechanism. A gap in the diagnostic's attribution, not a gameplay fault. **(e) Also:** `regularPaddleGlow` imported (James: the stray `Copy` should have been this), the power-up brick on the bricks page grown to the full canvas height while the Square brick stays as it is, the brick sizes row left alone on his word, and the Egg queued for a later release in its own row. |
| **Later release: Egg, a power-up that makes the ball egg-shaped** | James, round 320, with a delivered `ballEgg` picture: "the ball becomes egg-shaped, which makes its bounces much less predictable. This would require the ball to spin about its own axis." Round 321: "forget the egg for now. Maybe something to add in a later release." **What it would cost, so a future round starts from the answer:** the body swap and the spin are small (`SKPhysicsBody(texture:size:)`, `allowsRotation` true on the ball, which is false today at `GameScene.swift` ~1641). The cost is everything that assumes a circle: the resting-height correction round 313z4 fixed reads the ball's width as a radius and an egg has two; the seam correction likewise; `holdBallSpeeds` normalises linear velocity only, so collision-induced spin would be unmanaged; and the paddle's leave angle comes from *where* the ball struck the bar rather than the contact normal, so the unpredictability would only show off bricks and walls unless that model changes too. Every ball in Multi-Ball needs the same, and it lengthens the dozen parallel arrays. **The design risk is the larger one:** rounds 258 and 307 both found that unpredictable ball behaviour reads as broken rather than fun, and the drawn egg is what would let a player attribute it to the power-up. Recommended shape: a spike first, one ball, body and rotation only, played for five minutes before any of it is wired into the arrays. `ballEgg` (43x55, 1:2:3) was delivered to File Sharing and not imported. |
| **Round 319f: a scoring bug that is not there, and the trail left behind** | Reading round 319e's mapping test raised a question worth chasing. A save writes its `totalScore` as `totalScore + levelScore`, because a level in progress has not banked and the resuming screen needs a number to show; `Playing.reloadUI` assigns both straight back, which puts the level's running score inside `totalScore` **and** in `levelScore`, and everything that draws the score adds the two. That should make the HUD jump by a level's worth on the first brick after a resume and bank it twice at the level end. **It does not**, because thirty lines further down `scene.totalScore = scene.totalScore - scene.levelScore` takes the overlap back out - inside a branch otherwise about whether the ball animates in, under a four-word comment. Both ends now say what the other end does, because three places have to agree for a resumed score to be right and none of them said so. |
| **Open: nothing in the suite can drive a level being built, which is why the resume path is 2% covered** | Found by trying, in round 319f. The restore only runs on a `PreGame` or `InbetweenLevels` transition into `Playing`, and driving `PreGame` to `Playing` on a bare `GameScene` traps in level building (`Range requires lowerBound <= upperBound`) because the scene has no pack tables, level indices or field behind it. **No test in over 2,300 drives that transition**, which is exactly why round 311's coverage pass put `resumeGame` and `reloadUI` near the top of its risk list at 2%, and why round 313c's iPad resume bug went unnoticed until James met it on a device. A fixture thin enough to compile is a fixture that skips the code: round 319f's first attempt entered `Playing` from nothing and read back two zeros, which is the vacuous test this project has been caught by twice. **What is wanted is a real scene fixture** - a `GameScene` set up enough to build a level from the catalogue and take the two transitions - and it is worth building once rather than per test, because everything in the resume path is behind it. **Round 319g got two thirds of the way and is worth starting from rather than repeating.** Three things it established: (1) `PreGame.resetGame` force-unwraps `gameViewControllerDelegate`, so the fixture needs a stub, and the protocol is four methods and four properties (watch the two long signatures, which have grown parameters since the obvious guess); (2) the transition traps in `powerUpIconReset` on `1...iconArray.count` because the tray is filled from the scene file and an in-code scene has none - **fixed in round 319g**, and the fixture gets further for it; (3) with those two dealt with, `PreGame` to `Playing` completes and lands in `Playing`, but the save's scores do not arrive: `savedGame` is nil and `resumeGameToLoad` is false by the end, so something in the sequence clears them before or after `loadNextLevel` reads them, and `numberOfLives` coming back as 3 is *not* evidence either way because that is also `classicStartingRack`. **The next step is to find where the real resume sequences through `resumeGame()`**, which ends by entering `Paused` rather than `Playing`, so the launch path and the transition path may not be the same path at all. That is the question, and it is a piece of work rather than a side quest. |
| **Round 319b to 319e: the App Store readiness pass, and four things it turned over** | The §12.0 item asks for "a deliberate pre-release review against the guidelines" plus an anti-cheat sweep. Worked as a pass rather than a checklist, and what it found was mostly not about guidelines at all. **(a) The About screen would have shipped saying 1.2.** The version line was typed into the storyboard as "Giga-Ball 1.2 (3) - August 2026" and nothing set it at runtime, while the project is at 1.3 build 83. Nothing could have failed on it: a label with the wrong words in it is not a bug, it is a fact about the app that stopped being true, which is the documentation rot of rounds 313 and 314 wearing a different coat. It reads `CFBundleShortVersionString` and `CFBundleVersion` now. **The month is gone rather than corrected**, because nothing in the bundle can supply it and a typed date is a second copy of a decision that goes stale on a schedule of its own - which is exactly what had happened. If James wants it back it needs a source. **(b) The daily briefing never got round 314's iPad cap** (round 319b, its own row below). **(c) A test had left the day clock wound forward** (round 319c, also below), found while verifying (b). **(d) The save's forty arguments are pinned** (round 319e), because the readiness pass reached "saved progress and leaderboards stay valid", which is one of the two constraints that never bend, and the function that writes a save was second on round 311's risk list at 3% covered. **What the pass cleared:** the `#if DEBUG` blocks are all console diagnostics with no visible UI, so nothing developer-facing ships; the Quick Start Guide row opens the intro rather than nothing, so there is no dead control; `ITSAppUsesNonExemptEncryption` is declared false, which avoids the export-compliance prompt; the storyboard holds no placeholder copy beyond design-time labels the code overwrites; and round 301's deprecated `SKStoreReviewController` path is gone, so no deprecated StoreKit API sits in the binary. |
| ~~**Open: the plist and the code allow iPad landscape, and the decision on record says portrait**~~ | **Answered, round 320. James: "iPad landscape is allowed."** So the code is right and the recorded decision is the thing that moved: round 301's "no landscape" was made when meeting the four-orientation requirement would have meant designing landscape menus, and the layout work since has made landscape hold on its own - the menus cap and centre, the play zone letterboxes, and `WindowSizeTests` covers an 11-inch, a 13-inch and a 900x420 window with nothing falling outside any of them. FUTURE-RELEASES.md updated. *The note as it stood:* | Found in round 319d's readiness pass, and it is a question rather than a fault. `UISupportedInterfaceOrientations~ipad` lists all four orientations and `GameViewController.supportedInterfaceOrientations` returns `.all` on iPad, so the app rotates. FUTURE-RELEASES.md records the opposite as a decision, in James's words (round 301: "no landscape"), with the reasoning that Slide Over and Split View need only that the app resize, that the four-orientation requirement belongs to older iPadOS, and that meeting it would mean designing landscape *menus* while the play zone simply letterboxes. **So the decision was made and the code was never changed to match.** What is not clear is which way that should now be resolved, because the layout work since has made landscape survivable: `WindowSizeTests` covers an 11-inch and a 13-inch landscape window and a 900x420 one, nothing falls outside any of them, the menus cap to a phone's width and centre, and the play zone letterboxes as predicted. Restricting the mask would implement his decision and would take rotation away from iPad players who hold the device that way; leaving it allows a shape nobody designed. **It wants his word rather than my taste**, which is why this is a row and not a commit. |
| **The anti-cheat sweep, done: the in-app paths are tight and the two that are not are not closeable from inside the app** | The other half of the readiness item. **Every path that posts** was walked: the standing bests (`packHighScores`, both endless modes' heights and totals) are resubmitted from local stats on every save, which is idempotent because a leaderboard keeps the best; the daily board takes one submission per attempt, with `isScoringAttempt` set false the moment it is consumed and the post gated on `challenge.dateKey == session.todayKey`; the overall total is always submitted whole, so it is safe to resubmit and self-heals a day that lands late. **A finished game is never saved** - `runIsOver` covers game over and the last level once the scene has moved past playing it, and `testAGameOverIsNeverSaved` holds the play test's own words for why ("when a game is over it mustn't be saved - this could allow users to cheat"). **A practice run never posts**, a forfeited No Pausing run never posts, and the attempt is spent at the play press rather than at the result, so a force quit cannot buy a second one. **Two hold-outs, both outside the app.** A player who winds the *device* clock reaches a future day's challenge and can post it into the current Game Center window, which only a server-side clock could close. And a player with filesystem access to the container, through a jailbreak or a manipulated backup, can restore a save repeatedly and post the best of several attempts, because a recurring board keeps the best score submitted in the window. Both are inherent to a client-scored daily and neither is worth a mitigation that a determined player would walk around; recorded here so the answer is "known and accepted" rather than "never looked at". |
| **Round 319a: the full suite read, and six things it was holding** | The last full suite was round 316's and **its result was never read**; rounds 315 to 318 went in on targeted runs. Waiting in it were 27 assertion failures across five classes, every one real, and two of them faults in round 318's own work that **round 318's own new test had already caught**. **(a) The achievement indices, wrong in both directions.** `dailyOnly` was `Set(87...96)` and the daily's own are 85 to 94, two out at both ends: Butter Fingers and Blur were filed as facts about a history and so listed under no play mode, while First Daily Challenge and Serial Daily Challenger stayed in the bucket meaning "earnable everywhere". And round 318 moved `endlessOnly`'s 95 to 97 on the reading that 95 was Top Of The Charts - 95 is Butter Fingers, 93 is Top Of The Charts, 97 is Pokey, so the original number was right and the correction broke it. Butter Fingers now sits in a new `bothEndlessModes` set, which is the distinction round 318 needed and did not have: `ballLost` asks `endlessMode`, true in both, so one achievement serves the pair and belongs on both tabs. `testTheModeSetsNameWhatTheyThinkTheyName` found all of it. **A test written and never run to completion is a test that has not been run.** **(b) A brick asked to move before it has a size does not move.** Round 317's motions are attached when a cell is configured, which is before its icon is laid out, and Moving and Gravity are measured in *points*: built against a zero size, both travel nowhere. A reused cell already has a size, so the brick would have sat still on its first appearance and started moving once the row had been scrolled away and come back - intermittent stillness on the two bricks whose whole point is that they move, and nothing in a render test would have caught it because a render test lays its views out first. `reanimateIfNeeded` rebuilds the motion at the real size from `layoutSubviews`, and only when the size has actually moved, so scrolling does not restart a patrol several times a second. **(c) Three tests that pinned a look James has since changed.** A shaped Portal wears Portal art and no rings, because round 315 delivered every shape - and the old test guarded itself on `BrickPortalWedge`, a name nothing looks up, so it went on passing while the behaviour under it changed completely. The Portal Paddle is lit rather than tinted. Magnetism, now the only paddle power-up that paints, gets a test of its own so a round that removes the last tint by accident cannot take its only signal with it. **(d) A pin on artwork that a redraw moved.** The wrecking ball theme map was checked through three textures with distinctive sizes; round 315's redraw made eleven of the twelve 64pt, so a delivery of new pictures failed a test about the *order* of a lookup table. `wreckingThemeNames` is exposed and pinned by name instead - identity rather than a drawing decision - and the Square theme keeps a test of its own, since its 50pt spikes sit inside the ball's own footprint and "the power-up did nothing" is exactly what that looks like. **(e) One pixel**: `PowerUpPortal` is 249x250 at all three scales, so it scales honestly and round 271's check passed it; `testTheArtworkIsSquare` is a one-per-cent ratio now rather than an equality, and §8.5 records the pixel. |
| **Round 319: the daily's twist mix, which was cycling the same few ideas because most days offered nothing** | James: "the daily challenge twists seem to cycle through the same few options quite frequently. There should be different ones more often, more often days with multiple twists per day, a theme or B&W pretty much every day or very frequently paired with another twist. Vanilla should be quite rare, like once every 2 weeks." **Three of those four were one cause.** The draw spent 30% of days on no twist and 50% on exactly one, so a third of days offered nothing and half offered a single idea, and the look category (Monochromatic, Daily Theme) then had to win a blind category draw on top of that. The thing the player sees before reading anything was the thing they saw least. **The new draw** rolls a plain day first at 5%, draws the look deliberately at 90%, then takes a second twist at 45% from the categories still open. **Five rather than the seven that one-day-in-fourteen works out at**, because a handful of days a year have no legal twist at all and land plain however the roll went: the constant compensates for a measured effect and says so where it is declared. **The zero-guard is the other half of it.** A day the rolls said was not plain now redraws across every still-open category rather than falling through to Vanilla when its drawn category refuses everything, so a day is plain because the roll said so and never because a pool was empty. **All of it sits behind `twistMixKey` = 2026-10-01**, which is the §3 promise that a day already played replays identically, pinned by `testTheDaysAlreadyPlayedStillReadExactlyTheSame`. **And the measurement had to be corrected before the constants could be trusted.** The first version of `DailyTwistMixTests` measured `rawChallenge(forKey:)`, which is the draw and not the day: `stepped`, the no-repeats rule, sits between them and re-rolls a day that reads like the one before it. Tuning against the raw draw is tuning against a distribution nobody is ever handed, and it was only caught because `testTwistCountsFollowTheDistribution` failed on the delivered numbers while the new tests were passing on the raw ones. Measured properly, over the year from 2026-10-01: **7.7% plain (one every 13 days), 81.9% carrying a theme or B&W** once that category opens, 83.2% of those paired, 64.9% with two or more twists, 20 distinct twists met inside the year. That old distribution test was measuring nothing useful either and was rewritten in the same round: it ran a thousand days from 2026-01-01 and asserted one set of thresholds across three regimes, 212 days of which pre-date every activation key and are plain because there is nothing to draw. It asks each regime in its own window now, the old split included, because those are days people have played. |
| **Round 318: three faults in which achievements a tab lists, and only one was visible** | James: "remove the endless mode achievements from showing up in the endless mayhem section - check the other sections to make sure they only show achievements available in those game modes." **The one he saw**: `endlessOnly` answered both endless modes, which was true when it was written and stopped being true in round 309, the round that gave Mayhem its own eleven milestones *because* it had been sharing Endless's. Sharing them on the tab outlived sharing them in the game, so the Mayhem tab listed Endless's eleven above Mayhem's eleven, each pair saying the same thing twice. Whether a Mayhem run still *earns* the Endless ones is a separate question and deliberately unchanged: the checks do not ask which endless mode it is, and rewriting that would take achievements off players who hold them. **The one that checking the others found**: the daily's ten fell through to the default bucket, "every play mode", so Week Long Streak, First Daily Challenge and Top 10 Finish were listed under Classic, Endless and Mayhem. They are facts about a history rather than about a rally, awarded from a menu with no scene to ask, and no amount of Classic play earns one. They belong to no play mode at all, which is what an empty set says; the Daily tab reaches them through `earnableInDaily` and is unaffected. **The one nobody could have seen by reading**: `endlessOnly` held 95 with the comment "Butter Fingers", and 95 is Top Of The Charts. Two off, so a daily leaderboard placing was filed as an endless achievement and Butter Fingers sat in the bucket meaning "earnable everywhere". Found by printing each set beside the names it indexes, which is the only way a number typed into a `Set<Int>` can be checked at all, and that is a test now: the sets are asked against what the strings say rather than against a count. |
| **Round 317: the bricks page shows bricks doing what they do** | Four of James's items, all on the same page. **"Can we make those bricks actually move in the table view and in the brick info pages to demonstrate how they actually behave?"** Five of the nine actions are motions and a still picture could only ever half say so: a spinning brick drawn at an angle, a flashing one at half opacity, a breathing one shrunk inside its cell, each a single frame standing in for a movement and each needing its caption read to be understood. They perform now, on the grid and on their own page. **Core Animation rather than `UIView.animate`**, because these run on cells that are laid out, reused and scrolled: a layer animation does not fight Auto Layout, needs no undoing before the next layout pass, and is cleared when the cell is handed to a different brick. **Gravity is the one that does not autoreverse.** A spin, a flash, a breath and a patrol all return the way they came; a gravity brick does not rise again, so it waits, falls with the easing of something accelerating, and starts over. Reversing it would draw a brick floating upward. The shapes and the on-hit actions get no motion at all, an exploding brick being something that happens once when struck rather than a brick permanently detonating. **"Make the power-up brick and square brick larger."** Both were small for the same reason: every other picture is an oblong 104 by 52 and a square one cannot be, so at the oblong's scale a Square brick is 52 by 104, taller than the canvas, and it had been shrunk until it fitted with room to spare. They take the canvas height now. **"Remove the dotted line around the tiny brick."** Gone, and gone from the Square one too, which is the same decision rather than a second one: the cell was the comparison device for a row that is no longer a comparison chart, and beside a Square brick drawn to its own scale it would be a measurement that is not true. Normal and Big keep theirs, where it still is. The Breathing brick's outline goes for the same reason, an outline breathing with the brick saying the *cell* was changing size. **"Move the power-up and portal bricks to the on-hit actions section."** Done, which empties "Endless Mayhem Brick Types" and leaves five headings; they belong there on the section's own test, bricks that cannot be told apart until they are struck. Two catalogue tests pinned the old arrangement and one caught a real slip on the way: `styleOrder` was `shapeOrder + actionOrder + [.portal]`, and with Portal in the on-hit list that named it twice. |
| **Rounds 315 and 316: the graphics delivery, and the corrections that followed it** | James: "there's lots of new and updated graphics in File Sharing." **113 imagesets at three scales, 339 files**, every one complete at all three and scaling an honest 1:2:3, which is the check rounds 271 and 288 wrote down. 76 updated, 36 created: the Portal bricks' missing shapes and orientations, their glows, the paddle glows and the retro Portal paddles. The mechanisms for the last three did not exist yet, so the assets landed first and the code was written against real pictures rather than placeholders. **Two names placed on what the picture is** (round 271's rule): fourteen HUD icons arrived as `IconAimedSticky` where the code says `AimedStickyIcon`, prefix and suffix swapped, and `ballWreckingUndestruciCandy` is missing its "t" the same way round 183's `ballWreckingGigaCandy Copy` was. **The neutral power-up rule** (315a): "the yellow ones are considered neither good nor bad... these shouldn't show up at all for either." Each news twist now keeps one side and nothing else, written as "keep one side" rather than "ban the other two" so a fourth category invented later is excluded by default. This reverses round 229 on his word, and that round is worth reading first: what changed is that the badges are colour-coded now, so neutrality is something the player can see, and a day announcing "Bad power-ups only" dropping a yellow one reads as the twist failing. **The glows, and the correction that matters most** (315b, 316): round 315b read "20 points larger" as twenty points added to the cell. That is right on exactly one device, the one whose cell happens to be the size the art was drawn at, because a brick is scaled to its cell and a halo added in absolute points is not scaled at all. James: "do not scale them down." It is a **ratio** now, asked of the pair that matches the brick's own size, and it has to be per size rather than one number: the oblong pair is 1.357 by 1.714, the square 1.357 both ways, the Big 1.179 by 1.357, the same thickness of halo around three different shapes. The paddle's is per *shape* for the same reason. The test that would have caught it is in: the same brick in three different cells has to keep the same proportion between halo and brick, which a fixed margin cannot. **Rounded is not a face**, found by looking at a render: a dome, a notch, a wedge and a diamond are `EndlessIIFace` values, Rounded is a style, so a rounded Portal asked for `BrickPortalGlow` and wore the plain oblong's halo behind a capsule. **The paddle tint is gone** ("I don't think the paddles require a tint any more. The glow effect is enough"), leaving Magnetism as the only tinted paddle power-up, which is right since it has no glow drawn for it. **Two tests were passing without asserting anything**: `SKTexture` compares by identity and `SKTexture(imageNamed:)` hands back a fresh instance every call, so "the texture changed" was true whether or not it had. They compare asset names now. **And the info screens followed** ("make sure the info screens are updated with the new graphics"): most of the page derives its pictures and updated itself, but the Portal could not, because the delivery moved its identity out of the brick and into the glow and the page was showing a lime oblong that said nothing.
