It draws the halo now, from the same arithmetic the field uses. **Per-style brick artwork, costed rather than estimated** (315d, James: "if I were to do this, what would I need to create?"): nine styles still tint the brick underneath, and a tint lands on whatever brick is there, so drawn replacements mean 137 pictures per theme, 274 across classic and retro, **822 files at three scales**. `PerStyleArtCostTests` asks the compatibility rules for that number rather than listing it. The overlay design is nine pictures instead, is what four of the nine already do since round 271, and is §8.5's own stated rule. James: "let's leave the per-style bricks as they currently are." |
| **Round 314: James's answers worked through, and five documentation claims that had rotted** | A list of answers rather than a play-test list. **(a) The endless run lists merge** ("yes, merge the lists"), which closed its own row below. **(b) The iPad gets a phone's width, not a phone stretched across it** ("it should really just look like the phone app with everything centred on the larger background. The gameview should be as tall as possible within the view, avoiding cropping at all costs"): `menuMaximumWidth` of 460 caps the content beside the existing aspect cap, so the menus centre on the background rather than spreading across it, and the playfield still solves for height and spends the remainder on borders. Height is never capped, which is the mistake round 180 made and round 181 undid. **(c) A button that has been a square on every device below iOS 26** since round 52, found while looking at something else. **(d) Fifteen events wired for sounds that do not exist yet** ("are you able to put in some placeholder sounds. Are you able to also produce a list of new items that need sounds"): the call sites are in and named, and §8.5 carries the list James records against. `SKAction.playSoundFileNamed` is built eagerly and traps on a missing file, so every one checks the bundle first and a recording can be dropped in without a code change. **(e) The documentation reviewed** ("please review the documentation and make sure everything is up to date"), by running the §8.6 sweep over both specs, every backticked identifier against the whole Swift source. `endlessIIApplyRowLegend` does not exist and never did: I wrote it into the Millrace row the day before, in the round whose whole subject was notes that do not match the code, while describing the builder that made the note stale. It is `applyEndlessIIDesignedSpecs`. And SPECIFICATION.md said `UIRequiresFullScreen` "is still true, so the app does not participate in iPadOS multitasking": the key is not in Info.plist and not in the build settings, absent rather than true, and iPadOS 26 ignores it regardless, which is how James came to be playing in a window he could drag into any shape. Two rounds of layout work had followed from that fact while the document went on denying it. **(f) The relaunch count is a typical value, not a rule**: two full runs each relaunched twice where CLAUDE.md said to expect one, each preceded by the CoreAudio burst round 118 diagnosed, every named class passing alone. Recorded because a count stated as a rule sends somebody looking for a second explanation the moment it reads two. **(g) The twist badges redrawn**, all twenty-two at three scales, every one different from what the catalogue held. Checked before importing, which is the whole of the value: `DroughtTwistIcon` arrived with square corners and a pale border where every other badge fades to nothing, its worst corner 177 of 255 opaque against a set that runs 0 to 2. Twenty-one imported, Drought held back, and the check is a test now rather than an eyeballing, asking the four corner pixels of every badge at 3x against a bar of 40 that sits an order of magnitude clear of both the anti-aliasing below it and Drought's 177 above. It rejected the second delivery too, and passed the third. |
| **Round 313s-u: three paddles that did not agree, a power-up reported twice, and one slip made three times** | From one screenshot with four power-ups in it. **(a) The safety paddle's sticky band hung under it.** `paddleSticky` is anchored (0.5, 0) in the scene file, so the paddle's own eleven points grow *upward* from the line they are placed on; the bar's strip is built as a plain `SKSpriteNode`, anchored in its middle, and placed on the same line - five and a half points of it below a ten-point bar, measured. **(b) The "blue portal texture between the sections of the paddle" was not a texture.** A split paddle stops drawing itself and keeps its full span, because the bounce measures where the ball landed across the whole of it; `dressEndlessIIPaddle` then painted that span portal blue, and a textureless sprite with a colour draws a solid rectangle. The halves covered the ends and the gap did not - so the gap was the paddle itself, showing through the hole it is supposed to have. The pieces wear the tint now. **(c) The safety paddle splits with the paddle**, from the paddle's own layout rather than a second copy of the arithmetic: one body of several rectangles, each piece wearing its own face. That closes the last two cells of round 203's parity list - the bar has followed the paddle's width, scale, picture and shape since then. **(d) Ball Spin, reported a third time.** See the lesson above: round 305's still-paddle curve came from the collision offset and delivers nothing where a still paddle actually meets the ball. It is the ball's own horizontal speed now - 15.3 degrees over a flight at a 45-degree arrival dead centre against 0 before - and it *replaces* the collision term rather than joining it, because the two disagreed about which way friction acts and nearly cancelled half way out. **This supersedes round 305's (e)**, which is left in place as written: it was the right report and the wrong quantity. **(e) And the render written to check (a) to (c) showed the same anchor slip a third time**, one function along. `refreshEndlessIISplitOverlay` builds a fresh sprite per piece of a split paddle and copies the source's texture, size, zPosition and cap rect - not its anchor - so every split sticky and laser strip stood half a strip below the strip it replaced. Three instances in one round, all the same cause: **a node built in code standing in for a node built in the scene file, without asking what the scene file made it.** The tests assert a bottom edge rather than an anchor value, because the edge is the thing that has to be true. Worth recording about the render itself: it could not show this at first, because a `GameScene` built in a test rather than loaded from the .sks starts with a bare `SKSpriteNode` for each of these, so the property under test was already the wrong one and copying it changed nothing. The render sets the scene file's anchors explicitly now, and says why. **This is the only one of the five nothing reported** - it was found by looking at a picture, which is what CLAUDE.md asks for and the reason it asks. |
| **Round 313r: what killing `coreaudiod` did to the relaunch trap** | James ran `sudo killall coreaudiod`, which CLAUDE.md names as one of the two fixes for round 118's audio-server abort. The reported total went from around 870 to **1,059**, so far more of the suite now survives into the final launch - and the run still ended with exactly one relaunch and one named test that passed when run alone. **Worth doing, and not the whole answer**: the milder round-302 form of the trap is still there at roughly one per full run, which is what the CLAUDE.md note already says to expect. Recorded so the next round does not re-derive it from a suspicious green run. |
| **Round 313q: the Game Center door joins the button row, and the twists find their middle** | Two from James. **(a) "The Game Center button on the game over / complete screen is in the top right. Move it to the bottom right to line up with the other buttons, using the small right-side button position and style."** It was a loose disc pinned to `homeButton.centerYAnchor` - and `homeButton` is *hidden* on that screen, left wherever the storyboard puts it, which is the top corner. So it was mirrored across the top of the screen rather than lining up with the row it belonged to: a constraint to a hidden view is a constraint to a position nobody maintains. The row's right-hand slot is the run's detail, and already the leaderboard for a daily; a classic or endless ending had nothing to put there, which is what left this door homeless. It goes there wearing `ButtonLeaderboard`, the same picture the mode menus use for the same door. The rules it carried are one named property now: not while paused, where the slot is Settings and there is a run to go back to; not for a daily, whose own board takes the slot; and not for a player who is not signed in, because a button that opens an authentication sheet at the end of a run is a door nobody asked to be shown. **(b) "Centre the twists in the gap between the game mode heading and the bottom of the container."** A classic day carries a pack-and-level line under its mode name and the twists sit a fixed distance below *that*. An endless day has none - "the mode name and the twists are the day" (round 306) - and the label was left holding an empty string: **not hidden, so zero points tall and still taking the stack's spacing on both sides.** Thirty points of air above the twists and eighteen below, measured. Hidden now, with the gap after the mode name set to the card's own bottom inset, so it is one number rather than two. Put back to the stack's ordinary spacing on a classic day, because the pager reuses one card for every day - and there is a test for exactly that sequence. Checked by reverting: three of the four fail, reporting 30 and 18. |
| **Round 313p: a multiplier change carried into the next power-up** | The better half of round 313o's Multi-Ball finding, and found only because that one was being fixed. `applyPowerUp` resets `powerUpScore` at the top and has never reset `powerUpMultiplierScore`; after the switch the scene runs `multiplier = Scoring.adjusted(multiplier, by: powerUpMultiplierScore)`. **An arm that sets nothing therefore applies the previous collection's change a second time.** Sixty-four of the sixty-five set it, which is why this never showed - the one that does not is Multi-Ball, so catching an extra ball after a bad power-up docked the multiplier again for it: 2.0 down to 1.9 for collecting something *good*, measured. Both are reset now, so saying nothing means no change rather than "whatever the last one did". **What was nearly done instead is worth keeping:** the first fix gave Multi-Ball a `powerUpMultiplierScore` of 0.1 to match its neighbours, which would have hidden the missing reset behind a plausible number and quietly answered a **balance** question - whether an extra ball should move the multiplier at all - that is James's, not a bug's. It was retracted. And two drafts of the test measured nothing at all: `ballLostBool` starts **true**, a scene beginning with no ball in play, and `applyPowerUp` correctly refuses everything until there is one - so the collection under test never happened. It asserts the tally moved before asking what the collection did. |
| **Round 313o: the CRAP metric's top function paid out** | `applyPowerUp` led that list from the first pass - complexity 168, coverage 1%, thirteen hundred lines, and the one funnel every collection goes through. The reason it had no tests was mechanical: the list it works from is built halfway through `didMove`, behind a dozen force-cast `childNode(withName:)` lookups only a presented .sks scene can answer. Moving that order to a property both `didMove` and a test can read put the whole switch under test in ten lines of setup. **Two findings. (a) Extra Ball trapped** on a scene whose life icons do not exist yet: `rollInGainedLife` ran `shown..<lifeIcons.count`, and an inverted range traps - round 312's `Int.random(in: 0...(-1))` in a new place. The other two uses of `lifeIcons` in that function were already defended against a short list, so the third was an oversight; one clamp answers all three. **(b) Multi-Ball has never been counted.** Sixty-four of the sixty-five arms bump their own slot in `powerupsCollected` and index 28 bumped none - nothing else in the app writes it. The cost is bigger than the stats row it obviously breaks: `powerupsGenerated` *is* counted centrally, and achievements 30 and 31 are `generated - collected`, the power-ups a player left behind. **So every Multi-Ball anyone ever caught has counted as one they missed**, and those two achievements have been running fast since Multi-Ball shipped. The counting is right from here; stored history is left alone, because rewriting a player's totals is worse than an old number being low. This is CLAUDE.md's parallel-arrays trap in its quietest form - the power-up itself works perfectly - and it is precisely what the metric was pointing at. **A third thing worth keeping:** the first version of the sweep passed while doing nothing, because `powerUpTextureArray` is empty on a scene that has not been through `didMove`, so the loop ran zero times. It only surfaced because a later assertion indexed that empty array and crashed. Both sweeps now refuse to run against a list of fewer than sixty. |
| **Round 313k-l: the pause swipe had been dead since it was tuned** | **(a) "Swipe up to pause is no longer working at all."** Round 312 added the distance check James asked for and measured it from `UISwipeGestureRecognizer.location(in:)`. **A discrete recogniser reports where the gesture began, not where it got to** - so on a 450-point swipe, measured on the device with an instrumented build, `here` and `start` came back equal to six decimal places, the travel was always exactly zero, and the guard could never pass. The line meant to tune the feature switched it off, and nothing failed, because there was nothing a test could hold. **The second half is why the obvious repair does not work either**: UIKit recognises the flick about *forty points* in and then cancels the touch, so the scene's own tracking stopped at forty too - at the moment the recogniser fires, neither party knows how far the finger is going. The recogniser keeps its touches now (`cancelsTouchesInView = false`) and does the one thing it is good at, saying "that was a flick, upwards"; the scene measures the distance; the pause happens the moment both are true. `PauseSwipe` is that rule where a test can reach it, and the paddle is no longer yanked out from under a flick that turns out to be too short. Verified on the device both ways. **(b) "A square brick was over the low brick line by one brick height."** `brickHasReachedTheBottomZone` asked `sprite.frame`, and a styled brick's sprite is not its cell - `makeFace` and `makeRounded` shrink it and tuck it behind the picture they draw, which is round 270's trap met a function along. A Square brick is two rows tall, so the shrink is worth most of a row: measured with the old code, a rounded one rested **20 points past the line against a brick height of 20**. `endlessIIFieldRect` answers from the two things that still know the cell after a style has gone on, and had its own half of the same error - it centred the cell on the *node*, right for an ordinary brick and half a row out for a Square one. For a brick nothing has shrunk it reduces to `brick.frame`, so play-test round 10's Big brick is untouched. **(c) The level intro's wordmark stopped joining in.** The mode icon travelling with the name is deliberate and the code says so; round 312 added the wordmark to the same view and inherited that for free, without anyone deciding it should. It is hosted one level out now, where the intro's transforms cannot reach it, with its fade driven by hand. |
| ~~**Open: the resuming card is still airy on a 13-inch iPad, and that is a taste call**~~ | **Closed, round 314, by the only person who could close it.** James, asked directly: "iPad 13 inch resume screen looks good." It was a taste call about a screen he sees every launch, so his eye on the device is the answer and there is nothing further to build. *The note as it stood:* Round 313f-h capped two things about it, both measured rather than chosen: `resumeCardMaximumDrop` of 300 points, because a 393x852 phone lays the card about 250 under the wordmark and the tallest phone the app supports is not far past that, and `resumeCardMaximumWidth` of 420, for the same reason `menuMaximumAspectRatio` caps a shape rather than a size. Both bind only on an iPad, so no phone moved by a point. **It is better and it is not settled.** On a 13-inch iPad the card now sits in a large field of empty screen rather than being stretched across it, which is the right kind of wrong but still reads as sparse. Anything further - a second column, the wordmark closer, a card that grows its type rather than its box - is a design decision about a screen James sees every launch, not a bug, so it wants his eye on the device rather than another number from me. |
| ~~**Open: the phantom-brick watch still reports one row in the original Endless mode**~~ | **Closed in round 313z6: it was the staged build-in row, and the fix for it already shipped.** Chased by running the mode rather than waiting for another log. **(a) It does not reproduce.** A clean debug build installed on the iPhone 16 Pro, an original Endless run watched from the level intro through the whole build-in and twenty-five seconds beyond it: the watch audits once a second and said nothing at all. **(b) The number identifies it.** `GameSceneLayout.brickColumns` is `brickRows/2` - **eleven** - so "11 solid but unseeable, all at one y" is one *full row* of the endless field at a single height with nothing drawn. That is a staged build-in row, and original Endless stages its rows exactly as Mayhem does, through `prepareEndlessIIBuildIn`, which parks them at `topRowY + brickHeight` with alpha zero. **(c) The exclusion for that postdates the report.** The `row -1` rule came out of a *Mayhem* log (`16x alpha 0.00 plain row -1`) and excuses any brick whose whole cell is above the top line; the Endless report is in the older unenriched format, so it was written before that rule existed. Its test now takes its width from `GameSceneLayout.brickColumns` rather than a typed eleven, which is what lets it say which report it is covering. **If it recurs the enriched line names the reason, the role and the row in one go**, so there is nothing further to build here on a guess. | **A second false alarm found and closed in round 313z5, which is not this one.** Round 312 closed the watch against "a brick that is meant to be unseeable" and its note said Hide Bricks and Mayhem's Invisible brick were both covered. They were not: the guard asks about the *texture*, and Hide Bricks is a power-up that writes `isHidden` and leaves the texture alone, so every brick it hid walked through it. The two were written up as one thing because from a log they look like one - a row of solid, unseeable bricks - and only one of them was fixed. The watch now asks `endlessHiddenBricksIsRunning`, off the tray bar the power-up already uses to say so, and excludes `isHidden` alone so a brick faded or shrunk to nothing during those ten seconds is still caught. **That is not the open report**, which was eleven bricks two seconds into a run - before anybody could have caught a power-up - so this stays open. Round 313d stopped it crying at the *Classic* build-in, where every brick still waiting its turn is alpha zero behind a `wait` - so `hasActions()` covers it. A live Endless run still logged `PHANTOM BRICKS: 11 solid but unseeable`, eleven being one row, all at one y, two seconds into the run. `EndlessRowCreation` runs its two build-in groups as separate unkeyed actions rather than as a sequence, so the reason `hasActions()` misses this one is not yet established - it should be true for the 0.05 seconds they take. Not chased further because it is my own instrument rather than anything James can see, and a guessed fix to a tripwire is worse than a noisy one. The next Endless log with a timestamp near a row arriving should settle it. |
| **Round 313f-h: the iPad's windowed mode, with James's screenshots** | He sent ten, taken while "pulling the window into all sorts of shapes" in iPadOS 26's windowed mode, and they turned out to be four separate faults with one thing in common: **nothing in this app had ever been asked to survive a window changing shape under it**. A phone's window never does. **(a) The game view was being cropped.** "The top and bottom were cut off when the app was more square and the sides were cut off when the app was more tall and thin" - which is what `aspectFill` *is*: it scales the scene until it covers the view and discards the overhang, and the scene's size is taken once, at presentation, from the window the app opened in. `aspectFit` letterboxes instead, and that is the right answer rather than merely the safe one: the play zone holds a fixed 1.8236 ratio by design, so no shape of window has more of the game to show. The new arithmetic puts a number on the old behaviour - on his square window, **less than half the scene was on screen**. **(b) Eighteen screens never followed the window.** Round 188 found that `child.view.frame = self.view.frame` carries two bugs - a frame measured in the *grandparent's* coordinates, because every screen here is a subview of another, and a frame assigned once that nothing revisits - and wrote `fillSelf(with:)` to replace it. It converted one caller. Eighteen were still doing it by hand five rounds later, including the splash over the main menu. `ScreenPresentationTests` reads the sources and fails on the line coming back, because that is what it is: a line, writable again at any time, invisible on a phone. **(c) The daily's pager held an offset in points, not in days.** A page is the whole viewport; change the width underneath it and the offset stops naming the day it named, so the card sits part way between two pages - the right edge of one and the left of the next, which is exactly the screenshot with "CLASSIC" and "Food Pack" running off the edge. **(d) The main menu's mode table was 450 points tall whatever the window was**, from the storyboard, centred in a container that is 495 points on a phone and 248 in his window - so it hung 100 points out of each end, over the wordmark above and the icon row below. Both of those screenshots come from that one number. The row height could not catch it because it was circular - `min(150, tableHeight/4)` asked of a table four rows tall, so every height is its own fixed point - and now comes from the container. **A visible change on iPhone**: rows go from 112.5 to 123.75 points, the card inside staying 75, so what grows is the gap. **The lesson worth keeping** is the test kind, not any of the four fixes: `ResizedWhileOpenTests` lays a screen out at one size and *then* gives it another. Every window test before it laid a screen out *at* a size, which is a different question, and all of them passed throughout. |
| **Round 313c: a save is mostly in cells, and the parts that are not broke the iPad's resume** | James: an iPad run "started by resuming an existing game, but the game had misplaced bricks below the low level line and no ball in sight". **Almost none of a save is in points** - a brick's cell, a falling power-up's cell, every counter - which is why this survived four years on phones, where the layout is the same size at every launch. Three things are: the ball, the paddle, and **Mayhem's rich brick record**, which keeps an exact position and size because those bricks drift, shrink and sit between rows (round 150). An iPad opens at whatever size the window has, and since round 312a it can open the other way up - so those points meant a different place, which is a field below the low line and a ball outside the play area, exactly as reported. A save now records the play width and the top of the field it was written in, and `ResumeGeometry` reads its points into the layout running now: x scales about the centre, a length scales, and **y is a distance below the top of the field measured in bricks** rather than a scale about the origin, because the top bar's height does not scale with the play area. Both fields are optional, so every existing save decodes and reads as the identity - which is also what a phone gets, to the last bit. **Also audited on an iPad Pro 13-inch simulator** (iOS 18.5, the runtime that builds here): the main menu's cap works, its buttons and the pause alert are centred within it, and the game view's top row - pause button, height, power-up HUD - is already pinned to the play area rather than the screen. A sweep of every window size from 200x300 to 1400x1400 confirms the bar plus the play area never exceeds the window. **Still open:** the menu distortion James reported was on *resized* windows (split view, slide over), which simctl cannot easily reproduce, and three screens never call `limitMenuContentSize` at all - `DailyChallengeViewController`, `RunStatsViewController` and `InbetweenViewController`. That needs his screenshots again, or a look on the device. |
| **Round 313: three faults in my own log, and a random kick that was only half removed** | **(a) The log lied three ways about a run James had just played.** `RUN Classic Mode, rack 0` for an Endless Mayhem game - the line asked the scene's `endlessMode`, which the *pause menu* sets, so it is still false at pre-game; `rack 0` in the same line was the tell, since the rack branch two lines up had already read `startLevelNumber == 0` and taken the endless path. The choice lives on `GameMode.forRun` now, where a test can reach it. `RUN ENDED 0 pts in 0s` printed when the pause menu went up: the summary is built by `showPauseMenu` because every one of its numbers is a question about *that moment*, and pausing is one of the three things that opens it - only "Game Over" and "Complete" print. And `0 paddle hits` for a Classic level played with the paddle: the summary read `paddleHitsPerLevel`, which the level-completion achievement block zeroes a few lines earlier. That counter has to keep resetting, because two achievements ask "in one level", so the run counts its own in `InGameRecents`. **A log that is wrong is worse than no log**, and all three were only findable by reading one James sent. **(b) "Randomised bounce doesn't seem to be working."** It works - a test now drives a real bounce through `ballHorizontalControl` with the clock running and watches the angle scatter, which is round 88's lesson - but the game was already randomising bounces without being asked. Rounds 84, 88 and 98 reported the ball "changing direction slightly" with nothing to blame; round 98 found one correction in ten taking up to five degrees of random deflection and removed it **from `ballHorizontalControl` only**. The identical block was still standing in `ballVerticalControl`, and a brick strike runs both, so it had been firing on one brick contact in ten ever since, in every mode, for as long as this document has said the kick was gone. With that as the weather, a power-up whose whole job is to throw bounces off has nothing to stand out against. Nothing replaces it: the loop-breaker and the shallow-angle escape jitter do the anti-loop job on the same contact. **(c) Endless Mayhem's scores now sync.** James: "on my iPad, there were no scores on the Endless Mayhem mode as I'd only played it on my phone". Of the fifty-four fields in the stats file, fifty-three reached the key-value store; the one that did not was `endlessIIModeHeight`, which is why nothing looked broken from the inside. The heights' *dates* now travel with them too - they were written only by the reset path, so a device that pulled twenty heights kept its own three dates. A test reads the two files and fails on any stats field the sync never mentions, because a field is missing here by being absent. **(d) The halo fades below the paddle line** (James: "it stops very harshly at the paddle level"), as a faded skirt of the disc's own pixels hung under the diameter - a child of the halo sprite, so the three places that position, resize and remove it need no second node. **(e) The sync got its first test seam**: nine identical `let iCloudStore = NSUbiquitousKeyValueStore.default` locals became one property, and an in-memory subclass now stands in for the store. |
| **The CRAP pass, round 313: what it is worth, what it moved, and what it says now** | Ran twice on fresh coverage bundles, before and after the round's work. **The metric earned its keep on the first pass**: `ballVerticalControl` came eighth - a hundred lines of branching at **zero** coverage - and that is the function that was still carrying a random kick this document had recorded as removed five years' worth of rounds ago. The metric does not find bugs, but it is right about where to look. **What moved:** `updateToiCloud` went from 10,920 to **1,522** and `updateFromiCloud` from 8,930 to **1,369**, because the sync got a test seam and its first five tests - the two largest single drops the app has had. Both angle correctors came off the list entirely. **What the list says now**, holding out implicit closures and the level tables: `applyPowerUp` (cc 168, 1%), **`saveCurrentGame` (164, 3%)** and **`resumeGame` (50, 2%)** - which surfaced only once the sync dropped, and are the two functions the iPad's resume bug lived in, so the metric named them a second time - `buildNewEndlessRow` (151, 0%), `achievementsCheck` (128, 0%), `didBegin` (70, 0%), `loadDataReset` (42, 0%). 145 functions sit over the usual threshold of 30. **Two things learned about the tool itself.** A closure's declaration line sits inside a statement, so a brace walk measures the statement around it - three `implicit closure` entries were scoring 2,862 apiece off one executable line, and they are held out now. And a report is only true of the build its coverage came from: one of this round's reports was read against sources edited since, and quietly said `ballVerticalControl` had a complexity of 3 rather than 34. The script compares file dates against the bundle's and says so now. **Open:** `applyPowerUp` is the app's single riskiest function and is hard to reach - it wants a scene that has been through `didMove(to:)`, which wants a view controller - so the win there is a harness rather than a test. |
| ~~**Open: the two endless modes' run lists still replace rather than merge**~~ | **Built, round 314c, on James's word** ("yes, merge the lists"). A run is a height, a date and a duration, and `CloudKitHandler.mergedRuns` unions the two devices' lists rather than comparing totals and letting the larger win, so a device that has played less no longer loses everything it held. Both directions go through the same merge and both write the whole result back, so the store converges whichever device syncs last. **The durations came out of their own rule**: they used to travel as whole arrays under "biggest total wins" to match the heights, with a comment saying the two *had* to move under one rule or a run would be paired with a stranger's clock - merged as a triple that pairing is structural and the rule is not needed. **Two faults found by the existing tests, both worth keeping.** Keying identity on the date alone collapsed distinct runs, because two `Date()` values taken in quick succession can be equal - in real play a run takes seconds and it could never have happened, which is exactly why it would have gone unseen; the identity is the height *and* the date now. And the sort had no stability guarantee, so a sync that changed nothing quietly rearranged a device's own run order. **And an undateable list is not merged at all.** A device carrying history from before the dates existed has heights that cannot be identified, and the first version silently dropped them - losing a player's run history to a sync being the worst thing that file could do. `runs(heights:dates:durations:)` returns nil for such a list and the caller keeps longer-list-wins for it. | A sync compares the two lists by total metres and the larger one wins outright, so a device that has played less loses its runs. That is the rule the original Endless has used for years and Endless Mayhem now matches it deliberately - two modes answering the same question two different ways would be worse than either answer - but neither is what a fresh design would pick. A true merge would union the runs by date, which is why the dates now travel with the heights: the material for it is in the store. It is a change to a mode with years of scores on it, so it wants James's word before it ships. |
| ~~**Open: a shaped paddle has an angle profile but no height profile**~~ | **Built, round 313.** *The note as it stood, kept because its estimate is the finding:* “Round 312 fixed most of James's "the ball floats slightly above the paddle... sometimes it goes below the paddle too" - and the cause was not that the shaped answer was missing but that it kept being **overwritten**. `refreshEndlessIIPaddleShape` has written the shaped resting height into `ballStartingPositionY` since round 232, and `setBallStartingPositionY` wrote the plain paddle's numbers back over it from any of its nine callers: a resize, a theme change, a laser collected. Right or stale depending on which ran last, and above or below depending on the shape - a dome stands taller than the plain paddle and a wedge tapers past it, which is why he saw both. Both now compute it from one place. **What is left is the curvature.** A Convex face is a dome and a Concave one is a dish, and the resting height is still one number for the whole width - so a ball held at the very edge of a dome sits slightly proud of the slope. `PaddleBounce.Surface` maps a landing offset to an *angle* multiplier and nothing else; there is no height profile to ask. Two ways to get one: add a `height(at:)` beside `shaped(_:by:)` for the six faces, which is six curves to draw and keeps everything pure and testable; or sample the traced body's silhouette, which is exact and costs a rasterisation. The first is the one this project's other geometry already looks like. Not worth doing until somebody sees it: the remaining error is a fraction of a ball at the extreme ends of two of the six shapes.” **That last sentence was never measured.** Measured (`testHowFarAHeldBallFloatsAboveAShapedPaddle`), against a twelve-point ball: **a dome's ends are 8.96 points low, three quarters of a ball**; a wave's are 6.38; and **both wedges' low ends are 11.07, ninety-two per cent of a ball** hanging in the air beside the slope. And the dish breaks the estimate's *shape* as well as its size, being worst **half way out** at 6.08 against 1.82 at its ends, because a dish turns up into its corners. **Five shapes, not two, and most of a ball, not a fraction of one.** So the queue entry that said this was not worth doing was itself the reason it went undone - which is the third time this round a number nobody had measured turned out to be the whole question. **Neither of the two routes the row proposed was needed.** It offered six hand-drawn `height(at:)` curves, or a rasterisation - and `PaddleOutline` has been rasterising the exact silhouette since round 277 to cut the physics body from it. `profile(for:size:)` keeps that same run beside the body in the same cache, and `top(for:size:atOffsetFromCentre:)` reads it, interpolating between boundaries rather than snapping to one, because twenty-one boundaries across seventy-five points would step a sliding ball by a third of its own width - the staircase `PaddleOutline` exists to remove, put back one layer up. **The ball now rests on the surface it will bounce off**, which is the part worth having: one measurement answers both, so they cannot disagree. `restingBallY` gains an offset and the three places that hold a ball at a known x use it - the waiting ball, the sticky queue's spread, and both Aimed Sticky catches. **Nothing moves where there is no shape**: a paddle wearing none answers with half its height, the same expression as before, so Classic and the original Endless do not shift by a point, and there is a test saying so at every offset. **And the tests found the one shape nobody has drawn.** Jagged has no picture - `endlessIIPaddleShapeSuffix` returns nil - so it wears the plain paddle and only the angle half of its shape is in play; the first run of the silhouette tests failed on it, asking the *plain* paddle's rounded ends about a ball resting at the box top. That is now its own test, so a jagged paddle that one day gets artwork cannot keep the flat answer silently. Rendered as well as measured: five markers across four shapes at both heights, and the old row hangs in a straight line above the curve while the new one sits on it. **And the first version of it was wrong in a way round 232 had already written the test for** (313z4). "The ball is never inside the paddle" failed on four of the five shapes - correctly, because its yardstick was the *bounding box*, which had been the same number as the resting height and so made the assertion true by construction. It was still pointing at something real: a twelve-point ball reaches six points either side of its centre and every one of these faces climbs within that reach, so resting on the height directly beneath put **3.09 points of dome, 3.07 of wave, 2.90 of wedge and 2.71 of dish** inside the ball - a quarter of it buried, on all five, not just the concave one. The height is the **highest the face reaches under the ball's own width** now, which is what a circle on a curve does and is bounded by two answers already known safe. The test asks the silhouette across the whole face and the whole ball, which is what "inside its own silhouette" always meant. **And the first footprint tests asserted nothing**: `PaddleSurfaceTests` sets `ballSize`, the scene's setting, and never `ball.size`, the node - so every test in the class ran with a ball zero points across, invisible while the height was one number. Dividing by that radius crashed the batch, which is the only reason it was found rather than passing green and empty: the same shape as round 313o's sweep that looped zero times. |
| **Round 312: James's play-test list, worked end to end** | Twenty-six items over four commits. The ones worth remembering: **(a) The crash was an empty range** - No Power-Ups zeroes every weight and the next line is `Int.random(in: 0...powerUpProbSum-1)`, which on nought is `0...(-1)` and traps. **(b) The iPad answered itself in a log** - `restrictions now (0.0, 0.0)` and, one line later, iPadOS's own `Support for all orientations will soon be required`: `sizeRestrictions` is ignored for a single-orientation app, so the floor was never mine to set. All four orientations on iPad now, and the window audit's short-and-wide rows - carried since round 301 "if the orientation decision is ever revisited" - are live and passing. **(c) Two fixes that had never once run.** The mirrored paddle's power-up collection (round 209) set the contact masks on both sides and could not work, because both bodies are static and SpriteKit reports no contact between two static bodies; the main paddle is dynamic, which is the only reason the identical branch works there. And Auto-Aim after a sticky catch, whose guard waited on a flag set by a paddle *contact* - and a sticky catch happens in `update`, before the physics step, precisely so the ball never bounces. **(d) Three bugs that were the same bug**: a contact reports the velocity *after* the engine's bounce (§8.6's first trap), which is why the safety paddle's haptic was intermittent; the engine shoves a resting ball out of the paddle's traced body, which is why it slid along the paddle at a wall and drifted on a shaped one; and a cross-fade of two half-opaque layers is three-quarters opaque, which is why a spinning brick went see-through. **(e) A layout bug of mine, from the same log** - the resume card's labels moved into a stack but every storyboard constraint naming *two* of them survived, so two sets of spacing fought and UIKit broke the stack's on the device and the storyboard's in the simulator. Which is why every render test agreed with itself and the phone still looked wrong. |
| **Round 312: the crash, and the iPad answered from a log** | **(a) The No Power-Ups crash.** James, twice on a 30 August daily and again on a Classic day with the same twist. `powerUpProbSum = powerUpProbArray.reduce(0, +)` and the next line is `Int.random(in: 0...powerUpProbSum-1)` - which on a sum of nought is `0...(-1)`, and a range whose lower bound is above its upper **traps**. The twist zeroes every weight, which is exactly what it says it does, so the first brick that tried to drop anything killed the app. It needed the twist to be found and was never about the twist: any state that empties the weights reaches the same line. An empty pool now means this brick has no power-up to give. **(b) The iPad, answered by his log rather than by guessing.** `window floor asked for (320.0, 568.0), restrictions now (0.0, 0.0)` - the assignment does not take at all - and immediately below it iPadOS prints `Update the Info.plist: Support for all orientations will soon be required`. So `sizeRestrictions.minimumSize` is ignored for an app that declares one orientation, the floor is entirely the system's, and the only lever is landscape support on iPad. That is a product decision and Apple is going to force it. **(c) A layout bug of mine, also from the log.** `V:[UILabel]-(10)-[UILabel]` against `'UISV-spacing' V:[UILabel]-(14)-[UILabel]`: putting the resume card's labels into a stack reparents them, and a constraint naming a view that has left its superview dies with it - but every one of these names *two* of the card's labels, and all four moved together into a stack that is a child of the same container. Nothing was orphaned, so nothing was retired, and the scene shipped with two sets of spacing fighting. UIKit broke the stack's on the device and the storyboard's in the simulator, which is why every render test agreed with itself and the phone still looked wrong. The storyboard's constraints on those labels are retired by identity now, with a structural test rather than a measured one - which of two conflicting constraints UIKit breaks is not a thing to assert on. **(d) A sticky catch spends every paddle turn**, which is James answering round 311's open question: "with portal paddle and sticky paddle active together, sticky paddle counts turns down, but portal paddle doesn't so you get extra turns." **(e) The stats page's two power-up counts now come off one list.** Seen was `sightings.count` and collected was `powerUpsCollectedPerLevel`, a scene counter `applyPowerUp` increments on every path that grants an effect whether or not anything was seen - so the page could show 51 collected against 19 seen, at 268%. Collected can never exceed seen when a collected sighting *is* a sighting. Plus the wording: NEW HI-SCORE, the Statistics button and the page heading in `menuTitleFont`, and the power-up highlights sub-heading gone. |
| **From the CRAP metric: the iCloud sync is the riskiest untested code in the app** | Round 311 ran a coverage pass and scored every function by `cc^2 * (1 - cov)^3 + cc`. Held out the level-layout tables - a `loadLevel` scores 218 and is a level *drawn* as range checks, where a mistake is visible the first time anyone opens it - and the top of what is left is `applyPowerUp` (cc 166, 0%), `buildNewEndlessRow` (151, 0%), `achievementsCheck` (128, 0%), `saveCurrentGame` (128, 2.5%) and then **`updateToiCloud` (104, 0%) and `updateFromiCloud` (94, 0%)**. Those last two are the ones worth acting on, because they are the code that once crashed the app on launch for a player with years of synced data - the reason `padded(_:toMatch:)` exists at all - and nothing tests them. **The metric found a gap the project had already written down**, which is a fair result for a first run: `CloudKitHandlerTests`'s own header says "there is no seam to inject a store... round-tripping the real save/load paths needs the store behind a protocol first; that is worth doing as part of the save-format work, not before it." So this is confirmation and prioritisation rather than discovery - but it does put a number on how much is riding on that seam not existing. The work, when it is taken: `NSUbiquitousKeyValueStore.default` is taken *locally inside each method* rather than held as a property, so the seam is one injected store plus the extraction of the two merge rules the 499-line function repeats by hand for every array - OR for the flags, take-the-higher for the counts. Both are pure and would test exhaustively. Not attempted in round 311: a 500-line edit to the sync on a release branch is a round of its own, not a footnote to one. |
| **Answered in round 312: a sticky catch spends every paddle power-up's turn** | James: "with portal paddle and sticky paddle active together, sticky paddle counts turns down, but portal paddle doesn't so you get extra turns. Both power-ups should count down turns together. This should apply to all the turn based power ups when active together." So `catchStickyBallBeforeStep` calls `endlessIISpendPaddleTurns()` rather than setting the one Auto-Aim flag round 311 gave it. The reasoning round 311 wrote down still holds and is now acted on: a sticky catch **is** the ball landing on the paddle, eleven Mayhem paddle clocks count paddle landings, and a player running Sticky Paddle was getting free turns on every one of them. Aimed Sticky is not double-spent - its own catch happens on contact and the sticky path returns before this point while its clock runs. |
| **Round 311: the sticky band, found in the frame order rather than the maths** | **(a) Four reports, and the mover nobody had counted.** James, again: the sticky band and the laser turrets sit a point or two off the paddle while it is held against a wall - and this time "at normal width, on the classic theme", which retired every theory rounds 300, 305, 308 and 310 had. Positions match. Centres match. **Edges match**, which was the untested half and was measured this round. The bug is that every *writer* of `paddle.position.x` is innocent: `paddle.physicsBody!.isDynamic = true` and its `collisionBitMask` includes `boarderCategory`, so a paddle driven into a wall is a body in contact with another body and **the solver pushes it back out after `touchesMoved` has placed the overlays and after `didEvaluateActions` has run the clamp**. Nothing put them right again, so the strips kept the pre-physics x for the whole of the frame that was drawn. `didFinishUpdate` re-places them - the last callback before rendering, after constraints as well as after physics, so nothing can move the paddle behind their back - guarded by one comparison so a still paddle costs nothing. **A round-310 theory, retracted:** the retro paddle's scale factors (1.42, 1.82, 2.24 against 1.5, 2.0, 2.5) look eyeballed and are not - each is `(plainScale + 0.22)/1.22`, the rule "the artwork is the paddle's width plus a fixed margin". They are out by up to 1.28pt on a hundred-point paddle from being rounded to two decimals rather than computed, which is real and is *not* what James was seeing. The rule is written down in a test so the next reader does not spend the hour twice. **(b) Aimed Sticky × Auto-Aim never aimed.** `endlessIIAimTheStickyLaunch` guarded on `endlessIIAutoAimOwedTurn`, a flag `endlessIISpendPaddleTurns` sets on a paddle *contact* - and a sticky catch is not a contact: `catchStickyBallBeforeStep` takes the ball in `update`, before the physics step, precisely so it never bounces. So the flag was whatever the last real contact left behind, which is nearly always false, and the guard refused the case it was written for every time. It asks `endlessIIApplyAutoAim` directly now, whose own question - the clock is running, or this ball's contact owed a turn - is the honest one. The last-turn release loop had no aim call at all. **(c) The breathers keep breathing during an aim hold**, on a clock of their own. The hold freezes the world deliberately (round 210) and this is the one exception, on the brick's own terms: "the interest is in the gap it opens and closes... a brick you time rather than one you aim at", which freezing removes entirely. **(d) The iPad floor came down to 320x568**, the smallest phone the app supports, derived rather than guessed - and immediately failed the window audit: the daily's pager arrows were pinned at exactly ±150 from centre and sat 5pt off each edge. They are a preference with edge inequalities now. **The floor is not what James is hitting, though.** The app has been asking for a phone-sized window since round 310 and his iPad gives it near-full-height in both orientations, so something above it is refusing; the iPadOS 26 simulators on this Mac will not boot, so `SceneDelegate` logs what it asked for and what it got back, to be read on the device. The likeliest answer is that the app declares **portrait only** on iPad and iPadOS 26 keeps a single-orientation app's window at the screen's height - which would make it a product decision about landscape rather than a number in that file. **(e) The resuming screen, refined**: the score in Fugaz One at 30 with its label centred on the digits rather than sat on their baseline (measured off the two faces' cap heights), the level's own name under the pack line, the daily's date on a line of its own, the rack back under the score, four groups of air at one shared gap, the heading's halo widened to the wordmark's radius because it sits alone on an empty field, and the button unrimmed - the app's plain close button at the large size, not the lime one that means *confirm*. |
| **Round 310: James's play-test list, the resuming screen, and the workbook's last twenty-one achievements** | **(a) The resuming screen, rebuilt to James's spec.** It is only reachable by force-quitting a game and relaunching, and nine shapes of save reach it, so the words moved into `ResumeCard.lines` where the suite can read them and the arrangement into `layOutResumeCard` where it can be measured. The heading wears `menuTitleFont` and the green halo every menu title wears; under it the mode, then its detail, then the score and its label on **one line**; under all of it the app's large round button with an `xmark` on it, in place of a table view whose single row said Cancel. "Still your scoring attempt" is gone, replaced by **Competition run** in the detail line where James asked for it. The old Cancel table is *removed* rather than hidden, because a hidden view keeps its frame and seventy points of it sat in the middle of the new card - the same trap round 308 hit from the other side. **And a stack view skips the custom spacing after a hidden arranged subview**, which is how hiding the detail line in the endless modes silently closed the gap above the height; nothing failed, and rendering the three cards and looking at them is what found it. **(b) Extra Balls was reading its own answer.** `dailyStartingLives` returned `numberOfLives + 2` and `PreGame` assigns `numberOfLives = dailyStartingLives ?? 3` - the property was on the right-hand side of its own assignment and answered with whatever the last run left behind, which is nought on a fresh launch. Two more than nothing is two, which is exactly what James counted. It asks `classicStartingRack` now. **(c) The last twenty-one achievements from the workbook**, indices 77 to 97, across the eight parallel arrays. Three needed no new storage where a note said they would: `powerupsCollected` already counts every power-up separately, so Power-Up Completionist is a question about an array that exists; the daily's records already carry the day and whether it posted, so the four counts and three streaks come off them, with `DailyStreak.longest` doing the arithmetic it already owned; and a day's twists are pure in the key, so Twist Completionist is derived from days played rather than recorded. Only the two placings are time-critical, and they are read the moment Game Center answers, because the daily board is *recurring* and a rank not taken today cannot be asked for again. **The daily's blanket exemption is over.** `achievementsCheck` returned early for a daily so that a lent level could not unlock what earning it would have; that is right for everything about levels and packs and was only a blanket because the daily had nothing of its own. `AchievementCatalogue.earnableInDaily` is now the workbook's Daily Challenge column, `award(_:)` enforces it in one place rather than at every call site, and the Daily tab on the achievements page has rows in it. **Two things James needs to settle**: the twenty-one Game Center identifiers have to be created in App Store Connect before any of them can report (the list is in the round's report), and **the workbook's "Paddle Master" is already taken** - index 42 has worn it since 2020 for the ten-paddle-hit level clear, so the new one ships as "Backwards Compatible" until he says otherwise. `testAchievementNamesAreUnique` is what caught it. |
| **Round 300b: Blackout, which was already half-built and calling itself something else** | James kept Blackout in 1.3 and has no old iPhone to settle round 284's performance gate on. Both halves of that turned out to be the wrong shape. **The twist already existed.** `monochromatic` has shipped since round 229 with the briefing "All the colour is gone. Classic, and only Classic" and an implementation that forced the Classic *theme* - which is not colourless. The second sentence was built, the first was not, and a player on a Monochromatic day has been reading a promise the run does not keep. That is a live bug rather than a missing feature, and fixing it *is* §4's Blackout: a separate twist would now be the same day under two names. **And the filter needs no scene surgery.** `SKScene` is an `SKEffectNode` subclass, so `filter` and `shouldEnableEffects` go straight on the scene. The alternative - wrapping the world in an effect node so the HUD can stay outside - reparents every brick into a new coordinate space, and a brick's `position.y` is its row (§8.6). Two lines against the one refactor this project protects hardest, so the HUD is greyscale too; James's "for the HUD, leave it" was about cost, and there is no cost here to avoid. **The performance gate closed by being the wrong question.** The palette-swap fallback reads as the cheap alternative and is not: this game makes two thousand texture assignments, so a grey copy of each is a second art pipeline. What actually settles it is that 1.69ms against 3.08ms are both small, and a device that misses frames with the filter is close to missing them without it - which makes it a question about the minimum supported device, not the twist. The deployment target is iOS 15 and reaches A9 hardware; raising it is the one-line change that retires the question, and it is James's to make because it drops players. |
| **Round 308: a play-test list where two of the five were mine** | **(a) The stats icon was enormous, and round 306 made it so.** It replaced an SF Symbol carrying `pointSize: 12` with `UIImage(named:)`, which carries no size at all - it arrives at whatever the asset was drawn for, which is a 44pt information-screen row. **A symbol's size travels with it; a PNG's does not.** Drawn to 15pt now, by a renderer rather than a constraint, because a button's configuration sizes itself around its image and a constraint fights that rather than settling it. **(b) The posted-score line sat too low, and that was round 306 as well.** It started hiding the lives line on endless dailies, and **a hidden view keeps its frame** - so everything hanging below it sat past a blank line plus its margin. Collapsed to zero height while hidden. **(c) The twist detail sat too low for the same shape of reason but not by my hand**: an endless day writes the mode into `levelNumberLabel` and leaves `levelNameLabel` empty, and the rules were pinned under the empty one. They follow whichever of the two actually has text now. **(d) The trajectory line, narrowed.** James: "too wide over its length - make the widening less extreme - it also looks weird when it overlaps itself." Those are one complaint: a wide translucent line crossing its own path composites twice, so the fatter the far end the more the crossing shows. The swell was 1.5 to 5.0 and is now 1.5 to 3.1, blur eased to match, and **narrowing reduces the overlap rather than curing it** - the cure is drawing the whole polyline into one node so a crossing composites once, which is queued rather than smuggled in. It also **stopped short at the paddle**: the post-bounce stub was a flat five ball-widths however much reach the power-up had, so a ball falling straight at the paddle drew a long line to it and a stub off it, which reads as the prediction giving up exactly where it is about to be needed. The line is `reach` long wherever it goes now. **(e) The card shows twists by icon and name only**, with the block answering a tap - the same pop-up the pause menu shows, built by `DailyTwist.explainer` for both. This overturns play-test round 16, whose reasoning was right about the *pause* screen and is being overruled about this one. **And extracting that caught real drift.** The test that draws the trajectory to be looked at claimed to use "the same three numbers" the game does. It did when written; round 299 halved both alphas in the game and not in the test, so **the picture being judged had quietly stopped being the line the game draws**, which is the one job a render test has. Nothing failed - that is the point. Both now call `FadingLine.certainty/coreThickness/blurWidth/coreAlpha/glowAlpha`. |
| **Round 307: three play-test items measured before they were touched** | **(a) The near-vertical drift is the loop-breaker.** James: "the ball chasing direction when travelling near vertically near the low brick line... maybe 5-10 degrees." A bounce signature is quantised to half-brick cells and five-degree headings, so a near-vertical ball striking the *same brick in the same column* repeats it precisely, and three repeats in nine bounces prove a "loop" - which this is not; it is a ball hammering a multi-hit brick, which is the game being played well. Then the escalation supplied the number: the nudge doubles each proving, 1, 2, 4, 8, and it reset **only on a paddle hit**, which in Mayhem can be a long rally away. `fieldChanged()` clears it when a brick dies, for the same reason `playerIntervened` clears it when the paddle is struck - the old signatures describe a field that no longer exists, and a ball that just broke a brick is not stuck. **(b) Progressive disclosure was right at the start and wrong at depth.** Measured: gaps open at 18-26m, which is exactly the band James asked for, then quicken *past* it - under 10m by about 183m of height and settling at **6.3 to 9.1m**, twice as often as he asked, which is what "too many new things at once" was describing. The floor share goes 0.35 to 0.6 and the range narrows to 16...22, so a run now settles at 9.6 to 13.2m. The quickening he asked for is untouched; it just stops rising past the point he named. **The measurement also found the wrong dial first**: `styleSpacingRange` and `powerUpSpacingRange` read like live pacing and are the *fallback* for saves predating the unified queue - a run started today puts every element into one `releaseOrder` at `elementSpacing`. A note now says so beside them. **(c) The stutter is the HUD ring, not the drop.** James: "the ball still seems to be stuttering sometimes when a power up graphic appears after hitting a brick. Generally the first time it happens in a game." The falling graphic is innocent - a drop costs **0.29ms** the first time and nothing after - and the texture preload already exists. It is the first *ring*: `PowerUpIcon.ringTexture` builds its picture on first ask at **10.6ms median, 11.7 worst**, against 8.3ms for a 120fps frame. One dropped frame, once per power-up, and "generally the first time" is a cold cache. Round 291 measured this same machinery at 30.8ms *per frame* and cached it, which fixed the repeat cost; what nobody measured then was the cache's first answer. `warmEndlessIIRingTextures` pays it during the build-in, behind the splash, and a warmed ring measures **0.005ms**. It has a test of its own because the warm-up works by discarding three return values, which is the shape of code a later tidy-up deletes as useless. **(d) Random Bounce: measured, and recommended unchanged.** It already throws a 45-degree bounce by 24 degrees and a 75-degree one by 45, because the swing is proportional to how far the bounce is from horizontal. Round 283 widened it once on this same feedback and widening again would make steep bounces effectively random while leaving shallow ones untouched. If it still does not read, the problem is legibility rather than size - the player cannot separate the twist's swing from their own aim - and that is a design call for James rather than a constant to raise a third time. **And two corrections of my own**: `isTimed` in `PowerUpCatalogue` means "has a running clock", not "measured in seconds" - Landing Marker counts paddle hits and is `isTimed: true` - so round 305's Safety Paddle change had it wrong for a round, and `testNoUntimedPowerUpClaimsToExtendADuration` is what said so. And the suite's own failures were being read through a "Failing tests:" summary block that missed two whole classes; counting `error:` lines in test files is the reliable signal. |
| **Round 306: the daily's menu, and the hour the suite was spending on Game Center** | **The suite's own logs had been saying it for weeks.** `reportAuthenticationFailedForPlayer: Authentication failed for player in 207.38 seconds` - and 81, and 76, and 65. A simulator cannot sign in to Game Center and GameKit takes minutes to admit it; every test batch launches the app, every launch called `authGCPlayer`, and every one of those stalls was charged to the batch. That is why a suite of fast tests took the best part of an hour, why batches appeared to **hang after their last test had already passed**, and why it read as intermittent rather than systematic. `GameCenterHandler.isRunningTests` guards it now, and a three-class batch that used to take minutes runs in **45 seconds including its build**. Nothing under test loses anything - authentication fails in the simulator either way, so this skips the *waiting* for a failure rather than any behaviour - and it is a runtime check rather than `#if DEBUG`, because the question is "am I being tested", not "is this a debug build". **This corrects a note written in round 302**, which filed the same symptom under CLAUDE.md's relaunch trap and said an app-side guard was not the answer. That note was about a different signature and stands; this one was measured. **The daily's menu, from James's list.** The rank is `1/100` everywhere, which is one change because `LeaderboardStanding.text` is the one property the card, the pause result and the endless boards all read - the ordinal and the thousands grouping both went, since "1st / 1,200" and "1/1200" say the same thing and only one of them reads at a glance beside a score. The result line reorders to "Posted Score: 115m, Rank: 1/100"; the placing led from round 126, and reading it before the number it describes is what made it hard to take in. **An unposted score is now no score** on that card: it is the leaderboard's own row, so a score that never reached a board has nothing to say on it. The countdown changes *wording* rather than arithmetic once the attempt is spent - "Next challenge in 5h 12m" against "Closes in" - because the window's end is both when this one closes and when the next opens, and a player with nothing left to post is being told when to come back rather than hurried. Past days read "Free play only". Both free-play pop-ups take James's words, and the closed one names the day it closed on. The objective line under the mode name is gone from both branches - it said "high score on a single level" where the mode name already did, and on the two endless modes it said the same sentence twice on days that play very differently - and the pack now wears its own badge beside its name, as an attachment rather than a second label so the line wraps as one sentence. An endless daily stops announcing its single life unless a twist granted extras. "Free play" loses its trailing clause, "More Stats…" becomes "Stats…" and wears `iconStats` rather than a filled star, and a one-level run stops reporting levels cleared - a Classic daily either restates its own shape or reports a nought against a target nobody was given. **Asked and answered: a past day's rank cannot be shown.** The daily board is a *recurring* leaderboard, so it resets at each deadline and the placing no longer exists to fetch. |
| **Round 305: a play-test list, and three reports that were already fixed** | James's list, worked through. **(a) The height lines go with the ball**, cleared in `ballLost` and guarded on the mode rather than on a lives count, since markers are Mayhem's and Mayhem is a single life - named so that the guard is the thing revisited if that ever changes. **(b) Fixed bricks are capped at three on screen.** Each one anchors where it stands and destroys what descends onto it, so every Fixed brick is a hole punched in the descent: three is a hazard, a dozen is a wall the field cannot get past. Counted from the field rather than tracked in a counter, because a brick can leave in more ways than it can arrive - destroyed, exploded by a neighbour, Culled, Wiped, cleared with its row - and a counter that missed one of those would drift until it forbade the fourth for the rest of the run. **(c) Deep Green gains a purple top**, and "similar to how it is on the deep blue background" was answered by *reading* Deep Blue, which is a picture: sampled down its centre column it starts at rgb(24,0,26), within two points of `purple`, and has become its own blue by about a quarter of the way down. Deep Green now uses the same purple the side borders use, so the join with the tray above is a continuation rather than a meeting. **(d) The Safety Paddle counts five bounces**, not ten seconds: a timer spends itself whether or not the bar is doing anything, so one collected while the ball was up in the field could expire having caught nothing - the player paid for a surface and got a countdown. That exposed a contract `EndlessIIClock` states about itself and never enforced: "a turns clock does not decay with time at all" was true only because the one turns clock lived outside the seconds tick, and the Safety Paddle needs that tick for its goodbye. `run(down:)` honours it now. **(e) Ball Spin curves off a still paddle.** James: "it should cause the ball to curve even if it's not moving, it should just cause more of a curve the faster it's moving." Where that curve comes from is the part worth choosing rather than inventing, because "as if there were friction" gives no direction when nothing is moving: a ball meeting a grippy surface *off-centre* is already sliding across it, and that is friction either way. So the baseline is the collision offset - dead centre gives nothing, the edge gives a third of a flick's turn - and the paddle's speed adds on top. **And the bounce itself is steeper while it runs**, at one and a half times the paddle's angular influence, which is the other half of what grip means. Inert still wins outright and Flipped's over-correction stays its own statement rather than something to multiply. **(f) A split paddle is separate paddles that move together.** James: "the paddle sections should be treated like separate individual paddles that move together, rather than a single paddle with a hole in the middle." The bounce angle is taken from where the ball lands across the paddle, and measuring that across the *whole span* is exactly what made the split read as one object: the inner ends of the pieces sit near the span's middle, so a ball striking the very edge of a piece came back almost straight up - the flattest bounce there is - and half of every segment could not steer at all. `endlessIISplitCollision` measures per segment; a ball over a gap answers nil and keeps the span's number, because it has struck nothing. **(g) And the halves were wearing the wrong picture** ("paddle graphics aren't applying to the split paddle correctly"), because they were rebuilt only when the paddle's *width* changed. A dress that changed while already split - Lasers collected, a theme swapped, the sticky band arriving - never reached them. Compared by identity, since `SKTexture` has no useful `==` and identity is the question asked. **Three reports needed no code.** The sticky graphic's drift at the wall was round 300's fix already: every strip hangs off the same two functions, and only the laser was being asserted - the test covers all four now, which is how a report about the sticky could arrive after the laser was fixed. The mirror has collected falling power-ups since round 209. And a test written for the split paddle assumed two pieces and found three: the *turrets* are always two (round 286), the pieces grow with the span, and the tests are layout-agnostic now. |
| **Round 304: four unlock arrays that would have hidden 1.4's new content** | Still reading rather than building. `makeStoredArraysConsistent` exists because of round 118's crash - a player with years of synced progress opened the build that added the twenty-ninth power-up and the app read one past the end of their stored array - and it pads the achievement and power-up arrays against a fresh `TotalStats`. It did **not** pad `themeUnlockedArray`, `appIconUnlockedArray`, `levelPackUnlockedArray` or `levelUnlockedArray`, each of which is indexed by a catalogue that grows between releases. **This is not the crash, and that is why it survived.** The screens take their row count from the *stored* array rather than from the catalogue, so twelve stored entries draw twelve rows and index nought to eleven, which is self-consistent and safe. It is the quieter failure: a thirteenth theme is simply **not there** for anyone with an older file, and from the outside that is indistinguishable from a theme nobody ever added - §8.6's own lesson about pools, arriving somewhere new. The cloud copy has padded these all along (`CloudKitHandler.padded`), so a player with iCloud would have seen the new item eventually and a player without it never would; padding the file makes the two agree on first launch, for everybody. Checked before shipping: the tail of all four fresh arrays is `false`, so padding can only ever append a *locked* entry and cannot hand anybody something unearned. It would have landed the moment a pack or theme was added in 1.4, and it would have looked like missing content rather than a data bug. |
| **Round 303: the day-pin extended to where the layout twists actually live** | Reading the generator to check something else. §2.1's absolute requirement is that two devices asking about the same date get the same answer, and `testTheDaysAlreadyPlayedStillReadExactlyTheSame` is what enforces it - thirty real days pinned with their modes, levels and twists. **Every one of them was August**, and the `layout` category does not activate until 2026-09-01, so no pinned day could draw Mirrored or Upside Down and `DailyTwist.levelsUnchangedBy` - the table round 297 rewrote with an 80% bar - was **completely unguarded**. Editing a level into or out of symmetry, or moving that bar, changes the pool those days roll against, and because the draw takes an index *against the pool's length* the whole day comes out different. Thirty September days are pinned now; 09-02 and 09-24 are the two that draw a layout twist and are the reason the rows exist. **Proved before believed**: excluding level 11 from Upside Down makes 09-02 draw `brickSwap` instead, and the test names the date. **And one comment was quietly undermining all of this.** The level draw said the fixed draw order means "adding a pool later cannot shift what an old date drew". That is true of a new *twist*, kept out of an old day's pool by its `activationKey`, and **untrue of a new level**: `classicLevelCount` is derived live from the pack tables, so a pack added in 1.4 changes this roll's modulus and every past Classic day draws something else. The fixed order protects the *shape* of the stream, not the outcome of a roll whose range moved. Nothing needs building for it - the pin catches it - but a comment that says "you need not worry" in front of a thing worth worrying about is worse than no comment, so it now says which half it guarantees and points at the test that catches the other. |
| **Round 302: the clamp checked against the old modes, and what that check found** | CLAUDE.md's rule is that a fix touching shared mechanics applies to all three modes and that Classic and Endless deserve real scrutiny, because theirs are the leaderboards with years of scores. Round 300 rewrote the wall clamp and moved it to a different point in the frame, and every test of it had been written against Mayhem - the one mode whose boards are new. **(a) `PaddleWallClampTests` asks all three.** The claim worth testing is not that the new code works but that **Classic and Endless cannot tell it changed**: the clamp is deliberately mode-blind, `endlessIIPaddleHalfWidth` is `paddle.size.width/2` everywhere, and `endlessIIWrapPaddleX` is a plain clamp unless Wrap-Around is running, which needs `gameMode == .endlessII`. All four claims hold, including that a collected wrap cannot open Classic's walls - that would be a *scoring* change, not a visual one. And the clamp is idempotent over six hundred frames, because one that moved the paddle a little each time it was asked would make it creep, which is the exact class of bug rounds 293 and 300 were both chasing. **(b) The check found a real bug, in all three modes.** The clamp moves the paddle on frames where *no touch is happening* - that is its whole purpose - and the ball waiting to be served is re-placed only inside `touchesMoved`. So an Expand at the wall nudged the paddle inward and **left the ball behind**: 50pt of nudge put the ball 50pt further out along the paddle, and enough of it would put the ball past the end and send the serve somewhere nobody aimed. Measured at 70pt from centre where it should have been 20. The clamp now carries the waiting ball, and the aim point with it for round 275's reason - the angle is measured from the ball to the point the finger last pointed at, so a ball walking out from under a stationary point swings the arrow, and it is the same walk whether a finger or a resize caused it. Held balls needed nothing: round 293 made them a *share* of the half-width, so they already survive a paddle moving for any reason. **(c) A third copy of the clamp, folded in.** `EndlessIIWrapAround` wrote the arithmetic out again for the one-shot cleanup when the wrap ends and the walls come back. It was correct and it was a third copy, so it goes through `endlessIIWrapPaddleX` too - which by that point in that branch answers as a plain clamp anyway. Round 293 showed what the *second* copy cost. |
| **Round 302a: an iCloud key that named the wrong array** | Found while reading the achievement machinery to draft a proposal, which is the only reason it was found at all. `saveToiCloud` read the achievement **percentages** out of the key holding the **unlocked flags** and cast the result to `[String]`. That key holds `[Bool]`, so the cast could never succeed: the merge branch **had never once run**, and every sync fell through to the `else` that pushes this device's percentages and dates over the cloud's wholesale. Two devices, and the second to sync could overwrite years of achievement dates with its own empties - and the merge written to prevent exactly that was unreachable. Nothing crashed, nothing warned, no test failed, because the code *reads* correctly: the variable is named after the array it wants and only the string literal disagrees. **Fixing the key made a dead line reachable**, which is the part worth remembering: `achievementDatesCloud![i]` was force-unwrapped, safe only because nothing ever got there, and it would have crashed for any player whose cloud holds percentages but a shorter dates array - which is every player who last synced before an achievement was added. Padded and guarded now. `testEveryICloudArrayIsReadFromTheKeyNamedAfterIt` is the check, and it is mechanical rather than clever: every array read out of the key-value store must use the key named after the variable, which is the convention the file already follows everywhere else. It was proved against the real bug before being believed, and it covers the **load** path as well as the save path - the load path was clean and unguarded, which is precisely where the save path had been. The write side was audited by hand: 113 writes, three apparent mismatches, all legitimate. |
| **Round 301: the minimum goes to iOS 17, the iPad stays portrait, and the audit becomes a test** | Two decisions from James and the work that follows them. **(a) iOS 17.** "Go with iOS 17." It was 15, which reaches A9 hardware - the device behind Blackout's performance gate and behind every future question of the form "will this hold 60fps on the oldest thing we support". Raising it retires the *class* of question rather than one instance, and it drops players, which is why it was his call rather than a recommendation acted on. Six build configurations, and two availability guards that could no longer be reached went with it. One of them mattered: the review prompt's `#available(iOS 16)` fallback called `SKStoreReviewController`, so a **deprecated StoreKit API was sitting unreachable in a binary that goes to App Review**. The haptics guard was `#available(iOS 13)` and had been dead for years. **(b) No landscape.** Slide Over and Split View on current iPadOS need only that the app resize, which it does since round 300; the four-orientation requirement belongs to older iPadOS, and meeting it means designing landscape *menus* - the play zone would simply letterbox. Portrait and resizable, written down in FUTURE-RELEASES.md as a decision rather than left as the default nobody revisited. **(c) `WindowSizeTests`, and why it is a test and not a screenshot.** The shapes that matter cannot be got at without driving Split View by hand, and "does that text fit" is the question an eye answers badly and a frame comparison answers exactly. It walks the view tree at eight shapes - the 420x640 floor, three splits, a Slide Over column, two phones and a full 13-inch iPad - and flags any **label or button** outside the window; containers and shadow-casting wrappers overhang by design and flagging them would bury the one finding that matters. **It passed the moment it was written, which has two explanations and only one is good news**, so the detector was proved before it was believed: at a deliberately impossible 120x140 it fails and names the day pager's two arrows with their frames. Nothing at the real sizes because there is nothing to find. That run also showed the *card* test is the weaker of the two - a card's labels stay inside the card while the container clips them - and both tests now say which case each actually covers. |
