| **Round 300: a clamp that overruled a power-up, a frame it was reading one frame late, and a card that showed the wrong level** | Three play-test reports, and two of them come back to the same eleven lines. **(a) Wrap-Around stopped wrapping.** James: "the wrap around power up isn't letting the paddle wrap around any more." Round 293 added `endlessIIKeepThePaddleInsideTheWalls` and ran it every frame, and it carried its *own copy* of the wall clamp - so from that round on a paddle driven off the edge was hauled back before it could reach the other one, and the power-up existed and did nothing. The rule was never wrong; a second writer was overruling it. It now goes through `endlessIIWrapPaddleX`, which already holds both halves - clamp ordinarily, carry a crossed centre round while the wrap runs. **One writer, one rule**, which is the general form of this mistake and the reason it is worth a line here: the bug was not in the wrap and not in the clamp, it was in there being two of them. **(b) The turrets were still drifting, because the fix was in the wrong hook.** James, twice now: "laser turrets are still moving away from the paddle a pixel or two when the paddle is dragged against the edge." Round 293 read the cause right - Expand animates `xScale` over 0.2s and a paddle at the wall grows out past it - and put the correction in `update`, which is *before* SpriteKit evaluates actions. So the clamp measured the paddle as it was one frame ago and the paddle grew out from under the answer, every frame, for the whole fifth of a second. One frame of that growth is a pixel or two at the end of the bar, which is exactly what was reported. Moved to `didEvaluateActions`, the first hook after the growth and before physics. **The lesson is the frame order, not the arithmetic**: a correction has to run after the thing it corrects, and an `SKAction` runs after `update` returns. **(c) The daily's card shows the day it will actually play.** James: "for daily challenges where the level is mirrored or upside down, the image of the level on the daily challenge menu view should reflect how the level will be presented." `DailyTwist.presented(_:under:)` turns the picture by `.upMirrored` or `.downMirrored` - an orientation, so the same bitmap is reused rather than redrawn on every card the pager passes - and it reads `layoutFlip(in:)`, the same question `applyDailyLayoutFlip` switches on, so a third layout twist arrives here already answered. Brick Swap is in that category, moves no brick, and falls through untouched. **(d) A test written into the wrong class, again.** Both quit tests were appended to `DailyLayoutFlipTests` because it happened to be last in the file, and the class they were meant for reported the same 50 tests it always had. Round 292's trap exactly. They live in `DailyQuitTests` now, and the class was run by name before it was believed. |
| **Round 300a: the daily's quit question, and the iPad's contradiction** | **(a) Quitting a daily asks what to do with the score.** James: "quitting a daily should post the partial score - ask the user with a pop up, otherwise assume not." Neither answer the open question offered, and better than both: the player decides once, on the way out. `GigaBallConfirm.postDailyScore` follows the main-menu confirm whenever `leavingWouldAbandonAScoringAttempt` - a daily running, the scoring attempt rather than free play, **and the day still open**, because offering a post the boards will refuse is worse than never asking. Both buttons leave, since leaving was agreed to on the pop-up before it, and only the green one posts: "Don't Post", not "Cancel". The yes runs `recordDailyResult()`, the same recorder a natural end runs, so a partial obeys every rule a finished run obeys rather than growing a second set of its own. **(b) The iPad settings contradicted each other and had been shipping that way.** `UIRequiresFullScreen` said the app does not multitask; `SceneDelegate`'s 420x640 minimum window size only means anything if it does. James: "I want genuine multitasking on the iPad, but the app's ratio must remain the same / near the same." The flag is gone. The ratio was never at risk - `GameSceneLayout` solves for 1.8236 at whatever size it is given and clamps to the available width - but the *shape* of what it returns changes: a device screen is always height-limited, so bar plus field fills it exactly, while a Slide Over column is width-limited and letterboxes the slack instead. Both are correct, and the tests now say so in two lists rather than one, because the existing "fills the screen" test is true of devices and false of windows. |
| **Round 299: a marker that outlives its ball, an outline instead of a ring, and a rectangle that is not worth a moved brick** | **(a) A power-up's graphic goes when the ball does.** James: "auto aim graphic needs to disappear immediately if the ball is lost whilst its visible. This is the same for any other new power-up graphic." `ballLost()` now clears the cluster balls, removes the auto-aim marker and hides the direction marker, so nothing drawn for a ball outlives it. **(b) The marker is the brick's own outline.** James: "can we make it a glow outline around the brick, matching the shape of the brick it's aiming at." `endlessIIAutoAimOutline(of:)` reads the face's path where there is one and the brick's rect where there is not, so a Diamond gets a diamond and a Big brick gets its own size - the marker cannot disagree with the brick because it is drawn from it. **(c) An invisible brick is a target.** James: "auto aim should be able to aim at invisible bricks even if they are not visible." `isHidden` means exactly one thing on a brick here - an Invisible brick nobody has struck yet - and it is solid, it scores, and it is the brick a free shot is *most* use against, because it is the one the player cannot aim at themselves. A Portal is still never a target: it is struck rather than destroyed, so aiming at one spends the shot on nothing. `testAutoAimIgnoresHiddenBricksAndPortals` asserted the opposite and is **rewritten rather than deleted** - as `testAutoAimIgnoresPortalsAndTakesInvisibleBricks`, the name added here in round 313 because the old one is not findable and a reader chasing it finds nothing - the Portal half was and is right, and the hidden half was a decision reversed, not a rule broken. **(d) The halo at 0.5**, and the trajectory line at half its previous opacity, both as asked. **(e) Gradient is Deep Purple** everywhere the player reads it. **(f) The flashing Diamond's rectangle: diagnosed, and the fix backed out.** James: "flashing diamond bricks have a small rectangle appear underneath them when they go transparent." The cause is child-alpha compositing - Flashing fades the brick *node*, a node's alpha is inherited by its children, so the silhouette and the picture hiding behind it fade together and their overlap composites denser than either; on a Diamond that overlap is the rectangle. Shrinking the sprite to nothing does stop it drawing and **breaks resume**: `ResumeBrickCreation` recovers a shaped brick's offset from `(0.5 - anchorPoint) * size`, which is zero for a zero-sized sprite, so a saved Diamond came back on its row centre instead of a cell below it. `testASquareDiamondSurvivesBeingSavedAndRebuilt` is what said so, and it is the reason the change is not in: a cosmetic rectangle during a flash is not worth a brick that moves when a game is resumed. **Still open**, and the fix is structural - the texture wants to live on a *child* of the brick so it can be hidden independently of the node whose alpha is animated - which is a round of its own. |
| **Round 297: an alikeness bar, a page with less on it, and three backgrounds** | **(a) The 80% bar.** James: "for anything >=80% similar, exclude from the mirrored and upside down twists." That turns "significantly different" from a judgement that has to be re-made by hand into one made once with a number under it - the table is now his review *plus* every level where at least four bricks in five land on another of the same kind when flipped, and `DailyLayoutFlipTests` fails if anything over the bar is missing. It adds seven levels to Mirrored (City 4, Computer 2, Body 6, 8 and 10, Emoji 5, Numbers 9) and none to Upside Down, where the three at or above the bar were already on his list and the next one sits at 75%. The most alike level still offered is 79%. **(b) A brick's page is a picture, a name and a description.** James: "the information in the table view for the bricks isn't needed." The rows were derived facts - which behaviours a style suits, which sizes it comes in, what it stacks with - read off `EndlessIIStyle` so the page could not disagree with the game. They were correct and they were more than the screen is for. **The derivations went with them**, because a fact nothing displays is dead code, and the rules they were reading are checked directly by `EndlessIIStyleTests` - which asks `suits` rather than asking how it reads, and is the better test of the two. Four tests that asserted the *sentences* went as well; nothing that tests a rule was lost. **(c) Prism**, James's diamond artwork, added as a picture background beside Deep Blue. **(d) Deep Green**, drawn rather than an asset, like the gradient it is a sibling of. The difficulty is in the brief: "dark so it doesn't clash with the game's colours". `glowGreen` is 210,255,0 - it is the ball, the grip, the halo and half the power-up badges - so the hue is kept and almost all of the light taken out, at about a twelfth of its brightness, which is already darker than the purple gradient's top. Same three stops, middle pinned to the paddle, so the two read as one background in two colours. **(e) The Glow moves.** "Make the giga-ball yellow/green fuzzy hue dynamic, so it moves and evolves slowly behind the game, almost like a lava lamp." Each pool is its own node now and drifts on **two periods that do not divide into each other** - 37 seconds across and 53 down for one, 43 and 29 for the other - which draws a Lissajous figure whose path only closes after half an hour. Because the two pools drift at different rates the *shape* of the light between them changes as well as its position, which is the difference between a picture sliding about and something evolving. A third period swells each one. **(f) The Clouds evolve too**, and only vertically, which is not a stylistic choice: the horizontal wrap works because each strip is moved by exactly the field's width, so anything touching a layer's width breaks the seam it is hiding. A `yScale` grows the strip downward from its top anchor, where there is nothing to expose. |
| **Round 296: the flip list becomes James's, and a measurement learns its place** | James reviewed the levels by hand and found **fifty-two** worth keeping out of Mirrored and **twenty** out of Upside Down, where round 294's measurement had found three and none. The measurement was not wrong; it was answering a different question. It could only test *identical* - build the level, flip it, compare every brick - and his note says what the rule actually is: "the levels available for these twists should be **significantly different** when mirrored or upside down to make it interesting." Significantly different is a judgement. **All three measured levels are in his list**, which is a measurement agreeing with a judgement rather than contradicting it: exact symmetry is the strictest case of what he is describing. So the table is his, and the test changed job - it now holds a **floor** underneath the list rather than reproducing it, asserting that any level which is exactly unchanged by a flip must be excluded, because that one is not a matter of taste and a level edited into it later would slip through unnoticed. **And it prints the near misses**, which is the part worth having next: the share of each level's bricks that land on another of the same kind when flipped. The highest still offered Mirrored are levels **99 at 90%**, 68 at 88%, 85 at 85%, 52 and 70 at 82%, 34 at 81%; Upside Down, level 12 at 75%, 3 at 74%, 99 at 72%. That is the list James's "lots of other levels ... quite similar" points at, with numbers on it, so the next pass is reading data rather than opening a hundred and ten levels by hand. **One thing to confirm: his note names Space Pack twice** and never names Challenge. The list otherwise runs in exact pack order and the duplicate sits where Challenge belongs, so it is read as Challenge - which excludes nine of that pack's ten levels from Mirrored, a lot to exclude on an inference. If it was meant for Space, then Space's levels are wrongly in the pool and Challenge's wrongly out. **Also fixed: the test was building a `GameScene` per level.** A scene holds something like two hundred `SKTexture` properties, so a hundred and ten of them - two hundred and twenty once the report was added - exhausted the asset catalogue and failed with `CUINamedRenditionInfo bufferId: unrecognized selector`, which reads like a bug in the levels and is nothing of the kind. One scene, reused, with its bricks cleared between levels: the crash goes and the run drops from 56 seconds to 13. |
| **Round 295: a queue that meant two things, and Aimed Sticky reaches the bar** | Found while reading the aimed catch for a different reason, which is the only reason it was found. **Round 293 changed what `endlessIIHeldOffsets` means** - from a distance in points to a fraction of the paddle's half-width, so a held ball rides a resize - and converted the two catches in `EndlessIIStickyPaddle`. **It missed two more, in two other files.** The one in `EndlessIIAimedSticky` was a live bug of the worst kind: the tick multiplies the stored number by the half-width, so an extra caught forty points from the centre was placed **twenty-four hundred points out** - off screen, on the very next frame, every time an Aimed Sticky caught a second ball. The one in `EndlessIISafetyPaddle` was harmless only by luck, since nothing reads a bar-held ball's entry. **Nothing failed**: every test of the aimed catch stopped at the catch, and every test of the tick used a ball caught the ordinary way, so the two halves were each covered and the join was not. `EveryCatchLeavesTheBallOnThePaddleTests` asks the question of every catch there is - whichever door a ball comes in by, one frame later it is still on the paddle - and asserts that every entry in the queue is a share rather than a distance, which is a check on the *unit* rather than on any one caller. This is §8.6's parallel-arrays trap wearing a new coat: it is not only adding an entry that breaks these, it is **changing what the entries mean**. **And Aimed Sticky reaches the safety bar**, which closes the last cell of James's round-200 parity list. The bar caught for a plain Sticky from round 285 and refused for an aimed one, and that was never a decision: `endlessIICollectAimedSticky` sets `stickyPaddleCatches` to zero - the two are one power-up and only one may own a launch - so a guard asking about catches said no, where the paddle's own aimed catch asks the clock. Three lines of the paddle's own sequence and the existing aim machinery does the rest, because the aim's target is the head of the held queue and this ball has just joined it. **Also: the Laser Beam priced** (0.21ms for one ball, 5.96ms for four - a third of a frame at worst, so §12.0's deliberate decision to leave it alone holds), and **the Monolith half of "two phases that need a decision" struck**, built in round 254. **Downpour is the one still owed**, and it needs James rather than code: "the descent runs faster for the phase", and there is no cadence to speed up because the field steps when the bottom row empties rather than on a timer. |
| **Round 294: the last two twist badges, and a flip that did nothing** | **(a) Full Deck and Level Pegging have their icons**, the pair round 290 reported missing because they have no row in the twist matrix, along with updated ClassicMayhem and NoPause. Every live twist wears James's artwork now; the only two placeholders left are Loaded and Sudden Death, which are retired and can never be drawn. `ArtStillToDrawTests` prints "twists: none". **(b) A symmetrical level is never given a flip that would leave it alone.** James: "for the daily challenge twists mirrored and upside down, don't have these set for levels that are symmetrical and won't look different when flipped" - a twist that changes nothing is a wasted day, since the briefing promises a rule change and the level arrives exactly as it always is. **Whether a level is symmetric is not written down anywhere and cannot be**: the levels are a hundred and ten Swift functions placing bricks with `if` statements, so there is nothing to read it off. It is *measured* - build every level, apply the game's own transform, compare what is on screen. **The answer is small: 62, 92 and 104 are unchanged by Mirrored, and not one of the hundred and ten is unchanged by Upside Down.** **Colour is part of the comparison and earns its place**: level 94 is mirror-symmetric in shape and not in colour, so mirroring it does change what a player sees and it stays in the pool - it is the only level the colour decides. The generator cannot build a level (a day is computed from its date on any device, with no scene), so the answer is a table in `DailyTwist.levelsUnchangedBy` and `DailyLayoutFlipTests` is what keeps it honest: it re-measures and fails the moment a level is edited into or out of the list. A third test walks a year of real days and asserts no day ever pairs a flip with a level it does nothing to. **(c) The level dispatcher moved onto `GameScene`.** The hundred-and-ten-case switch lived in `Playing`, which is a game state rather than a catalogue, so nothing but the running game could ask for a level - and this round needed exactly that. Moved rather than copied, because a hundred and ten cases written twice would disagree the first time a level was added. |
| **Play-test round 293: a resize the ball did not follow, and a clamp that was right all along** | **(a) A held ball keeps its share of the paddle.** The queue kept each caught ball's offset in *points*, so a paddle that expanded underneath it left the ball where it was in the field - a ball caught at the tip of a paddle that then grows ends up in its middle, and one on a paddle that shrinks hangs in the air off the end. It is a fraction of the half-width now, which is the same number the launch angle has always been measured in, so a ball carried by a resize also leaves at the angle where it *is*. **(b) An aim no longer holds the field.** Round 215 took the freeze out of Aimed Sticky - "the game doesn't pause" - and left one line standing in a different file: `endlessIIFieldIsHeld` read the aim flag, so the descent stopped for as long as a ball sat on the paddle. The owed *turn* still holds it, because that is a moment rather than a state. **(c) Ball Control is slacker again**, 8 rad/s at 0.45 of critical where round 291 set 11 at 0.5: about 1% of the gap in the first frame against the old exponential pull's 16%, and a fifth of the way past the paddle before it comes back. "More like a piece of string." **(d) The laser turrets at the wall, and the round's own mistake.** The report was that the turrets creep a couple of pixels after the paddle stops. The first diagnosis was that the wall clamp reads `paddle.size.width` while Expand animates `xScale`, so the clamp was multiplied by the scale a second time - and **five tests failed in the same direction**, which sent it to a measurement: `SKSpriteNode.size` *carries* the node's scale, a sprite built 100 wide at 1.5 reports 150. The clamp had always been right; the change had broken it. **The same measurement had been made in round 284** for the Aura and written down there, and two comments in the paddle file contradicted each other about it - the answer was taken from the comments rather than from a sprite. Both are corrected. **The real cause is *when* the clamp runs**: Expand nudges the paddle inside the walls the instant it is collected and *then* animates the scale over a fifth of a second, so a paddle at the wall is judged to fit at its old width and grows past the edge - centre still, ends walking outward, and the end is where the turret is drawn and the only part of a bar whose movement the eye can see. It is re-asked every frame now, behind its own early return. **(e) Square says "Each side is the same"** and Rounded is **Round** - display names only, since the enum case and every asset name are save keys and files. **The brick page's other details were checked and nothing had changed**: round 291 replaced description strings and nothing else, and the fact rows predate the workbook import entirely. |
| **Round 292: the queue audited, because five of its entries were lying** | Chasing "still owed: the Wipe's half of it" found it built - `wipeClassicTimers` ends all eleven of the original twenty-eight's timed power-ups through the ending registry the same round put in. That is the **third** stale entry found in six rounds, after the shaped-paddle play-test weight (287) and the Big-brick formation convention (286), so the rest were checked rather than trusted. **Four more were stale.** The twin's portal was built in round 285 and the row still asked for it. Three of the four things "still to come from the workbook" were built in round 218, the round immediately after the note - the endless Quicksand and the halo travelling with the field have their own row two above it, and the laser overlay has scaled with Expand for as long as Expand has existed; only the 447-cell interaction matrix is genuinely owed. The Endless 2.0 leaderboards went live on 21 August 2026 and the blocked-on-something-outside note still listed them. The grippy paddle texture and its placeholder icon were delivered in rounds 261 and 262 - seventy-two imagesets and a drawn badge. **And one of the five was mine, written seven rounds ago in this same table**: round 285 recorded "still open: Aimed Sticky and the shaped faces on the safety bar and the mirror, and the split's own sticky", and two thirds of that was already false when it was written - both surfaces have traced their shape since rounds 216 and 224, and a split paddle's sticky is the paddle's, because a split is one node with a compound body. It was written from the row above it rather than from the code, which is the exact fault being audited, committed by the round doing the audit. **What is actually left is smaller than the document claimed**, and that is the point of the audit rather than a tidy-up: §12.0 is the queue James works from, and a queue with dead entries in it is one that has to be re-checked by hand every time it is read. **The lesson is a general one and it is now in §8.6**: a note that says what is *not* done is a claim about the code like any other, and unlike the code nothing fails when it goes out of date. It has to be struck by the round that closes it, and where that is missed the only remedy is to re-ask the code. **This round then made the fault twice more while fixing it**, which is worth writing down rather than tidying away. It wrote a fresh `AWipeEndsTheClassicTimersTests` to "make the closure durable" without looking for existing coverage - `WipeEndsTheOldTimersTests` was forty lines further down the same file and covered every one of its assertions and two more, so the new class was deleted. And it reported that class as passing on the strength of a green run that had never executed it: twenty-six tests passed, and twenty-six is what the neighbouring class runs on its own. That is CLAUDE.md's first trap - `xcodebuild test` does not always relink - and the check it prescribes is to look for the symbol in the product rather than to trust the count. **A green suite is not evidence that the thing you just wrote ran.** |
| **Play-test round 291: the stutter was the HUD, and it was never the shaped paddle** | James's overnight list, and the third item on it is the one that matters. **(a) The ball stuttering with a shaped paddle, reported for the third round running** - and this time with the clue that settled it: "it's almost as if the ball is constantly speeding up and slowing down rather than a frame rate issue". That description *is* the diagnosis. A uniformly low frame rate looks slow; **uneven** frame times look like acceleration and braking, because `holdBallSpeeds` keeps the ball's speed exactly constant and so a long frame is a long jump. So the question stopped being "what is slow" and became "what is lumpy", and the answer was measured rather than guessed: **the power-up ring HUD is rebuilt from scratch every frame and cost 30.8ms - 185% of a 60fps frame budget.** Two lines did almost all of it. `SKTexture(image:)` is **9.8ms** - it is a GPU upload, not a wrapper - and it was called once per running entry per frame by all three ring builders; and the paddle-shape entry called `paddleSurface(_:)`, which *renders* a badge through `UIGraphicsImageRenderer`, the only one of the twenty whose icon was a function call rather than a cached `static let`. `PowerUpIcon.ringTexture` caches by key and takes its image as an **autoclosure**, which is the point rather than a nicety: after the first frame neither the render nor the `UIImage(named:)` behind it happens at all. **30.810ms to 0.008ms**, and a tripwire on it. Two rounds of seam-merging (284) and body-tracing were spent on a paddle that was never the cause; what made the shaped paddle look guilty is that it is a *paddle* power-up, so having one running is what put entries in the ring. **(b) Aimed Sticky, three faults in one gesture.** "It jumps down to a low angle when dragging the paddle" - `.keepAiming` is returned for three different gestures and the release path applied the lift position to the aim for all of them, so carrying the paddle and letting go pointed the shot at wherever the thumb was, which is *below* the ball, floored at the minimum launch angle. Round 232 asked for "a tap **above** the paddle moves the arrow"; above was the half that was missing. "It's inconsistent when dragging if it moves the paddle or the arrow" - the intent was recomputed from the finger's current height on every move event, so a drag crossing the paddle's top edge changed job halfway through; it is decided once, where the finger lands, and a catch that happens under a finger already carrying the paddle leaves it carrying the paddle. And the jitter had a third cause, which is (c). **(c) A held ball stops colliding with the paddle.** "With a sticky shaped paddle, the ball moves on the paddle after it has landed. Like it slides down the paddle's shape." A held ball is *placed* every frame at `ballStartingPositionY`, which is one height for a surface that is a dome or a dish - so anywhere but the middle it is placed slightly inside the traced body, and the engine's job is to push overlapping bodies apart, which on a slope means sideways. Written, pushed, written back, every frame. Clearing the paddle's bit on *the ball's* body is the safety bar's own move for a climbing ball, and it takes the arrow's jitter with it, because the aim's angle is measured from the ball. **(d) The last catch empties the paddle** ("launch both balls at the same time") - the release loop stopped at the primary ball, so the last turn launched one and left the other stuck to a paddle that was no longer sticky. **The first attempt at this fix was wrong and its test hid it**: the guard asked `ballIsOnPaddle` to tell a caught first ball from a waiting serve, and the classic sticky catch sets that flag *and then* puts the ball in the queue - so it skipped exactly the case it was written for, and passed because the test had set the flag to a value the game never produces. Being in the queue is the whole test, because only a catch puts the first ball there. **(e) Cluster balls go with the ball that fired them.** **(f) The beam fades over a second** before the burn starts, where round 288 cut it dead. **(g) Endless Mayhem's own logo on the run-history screen** - both endless modes share level 0, and level 0's picture is the infinity mark, so Mayhem wore Endless's logo there and its own everywhere else; that screen asks `GameMode.menuIcon` now. **(h) The twist badges are larger**, 1.9 capitals rather than 1.5, one number in the one builder every screen goes through. |
| **The brick page speaks the workbook's words** | James, round 291: "for the brick and power up info / details, use the information that's written in the document I shared previously. The descriptions should match the ones I wrote." **Twenty-three brick descriptions replaced** with the lines from `Giga-Ball 2026 - Brick Details.xlsx` - the same call round 229 made for the sixty-six power-ups, in his own words then: "shorter is better". What went was paragraphs written here over a dozen rounds, which read as documentation on a screen a player opens mid-run to find out what a brick does. **The power-up side needed nothing**: all sixty-six names and descriptions are byte-identical to what round 229 imported, nothing has drifted and nothing has been added since, which was checked against that round's own commit rather than assumed. **What is missing from the sheet: the Square size.** It arrived in round 247, after the workbook was drawn, and its Sizes section still lists only Tiny, Normal and Big - so Square is the one entry on the page whose description is not James's, written in the register of the three beside it and flagged. **Two slips keep the game's grammar**, exactly as round 229's three did: the Fixed row reads "until its destroyed" and "any other brick the hits it". The brick *states* the sheet lists - Multi-hit's four levels, Indestructible's two - are on the page as facts rather than descriptions, which is where they were already. |
| **Round 290: the daily's twist badges** | James delivered twenty, one per row of his twist matrix, and asked "are there any I am missing from the set?" **Two, and they are the ones the matrix does not have rows for:** Full Deck and Level Pegging, the `disclosure` pair added in round 258 - Mayhem-only, dated 2026-12-01, and the last two live twists still wearing a drawn placeholder. Loaded and Sudden Death are retired and need nothing: they are furniture in the save so that a case name which is a key in `retirementKey` never changes meaning. **Two of the twenty were mapped by looking rather than by reading**: `GoodNewsTwistIcon` and `NoBadNewsTwistIcon` are the two news twists, and which is which is in neither filename - the pictures settle it, a down arrow for No Good News ("what is coming down is not good", which is what the placeholder drew) and an up arrow for No Bad News. `ClassicMayhemTwistIcon` came with the set and is imported against §12's Mayhem Rules, which is not a `DailyTwist` case yet and so is wired to nothing. **Vanilla changes a decision rather than filling a gap.** Round 11 said "for vanilla icon, just use app icon", which was the right answer when there was no drawn Vanilla badge; there is one now, in the set's own violet, and a day with no twists is a *kind* of day rather than an absence of one. The app icon stays as the fallback below it. Each badge asks `artwork` for its file and falls back to its drawing, so the two still owed need no code when they arrive - and `ArtStillToDrawTests` asks `DailyTwist.allCases` which are missing rather than keeping a list, so a twist added tomorrow with no badge fails on the day it is added rather than on the day somebody spots a placeholder on the briefing screen. |
| **Round 289: the halo is drawn, and the bricks page stops showing a block** | **(a) The Paddle Halo wears James's `Halo` artwork.** It was a filled `SKShapeNode` semicircle with a stroke round it, which stated the reach exactly and looked like a geometry diagram rather than a glow. The picture is a soft disc, so the two numbers it turns on were measured off the file the way the Aura's were: alpha is flat at 148 across the middle and falls away at the rim, with the half-of-peak contour at 0.612 of the canvas, the quarter at 0.674, the tenth at 0.738 and the last trace at 0.817. **The quarter is taken as the edge**, which is the contour round 284 settled on for the Aura after the tenth read too small in play - two glows in the same field measured by the same rule is worth more than either being individually perfect. **Only the top half of the picture is used.** `haloTouches` refuses any brick whose top is below the centre, so the halo is the upper semicircle and always has been; a `SKTexture(rect:in:)` sub-texture is the half of it that exists, and anchoring the sprite on its flat edge means the centre this already computes needs no adjusting. **The drawn semicircle stays as the fallback** when the file is missing, which is against the habit of this file - every other effect answers a missing picture by drawing nothing, and that is right for a decoration and wrong here. Round 102's complaint about the Laser Beam was "doesn't appear to do anything", and a halo with no glow is a power-up eating bricks in silence in a part of the field the player is not watching. **(b) The bricks page shows a power-up as a power-up.** James: "rather than showing the power-up brick as a yellow block, use the PowerUpClearAndRetreat graphic as a generic power-up graphic." Round 284 had read "a generic power up graphic" as the brick's own badge - `PowerUpBrick`, the block an icon is cut into - which was the more literal answer and the worse one: a yellow rectangle beside eight other brick pictures reads as another kind of brick, and it is the one entry on the page whose picture a player never meets, because in the field the block always has an icon on it. The field is untouched and still draws the block; a page picture and a field picture are different questions. Named as `BrickTypeIcons.genericPowerUpArtName` so that the day a purpose-drawn generic badge exists (§8.5) it is one line. **One thing to watch in play:** the glow is drawn at the artwork's own opacity, which is flat at 148 of 255 across its middle where the shape it replaced filled at 0.14 and stroked at 0.45. That is the faithful reading of a delivered picture and it is a good deal heavier than what was there - the render puts it over a field so the wash can be judged, and if it reads as obscuring the bricks it covers, the sprite's own alpha is the one number to turn. |
| **Round 288: the black borders gone, and the beam leaves a burn** | **(a) Sixty-one corrected brick pictures imported.** James: "I've fixed all the bricks with the incorrect black border" - the fault §8.5 had recorded against the Diamond art and which turned out to reach Convex, Concave, Wedge, Diamond and DiamondSquare across the Indestructibles and the whole Retro set. **Fourteen of them arrived under a name the catalogue does not use**, and the two kinds of mismatch wanted different answers. Ten were case alone - `RetroBrickInvisibleConcave0` for the catalogue's `retroBrickInvisibleConcave0` - and went into the existing imagesets under the existing names, because `SKTexture(imageNamed:)` is case-sensitive and a second imageset differing only in case is a duplicate asset waiting to be picked at random. The other four were `retroBrickWedge0`/`90`/`180`/`270`, with the behaviour missing from the name entirely and no such asset in the game. **Which behaviour they belonged to was measured rather than guessed**: each was compared against all six retro behaviours' wedge of the same angle, ignoring the border band, and every one came back within 1.9 of Normal against 24 to 45 for everything else. They are `retroBrickNormalWedge*`. **(b) The Laser Beam leaves a burn.** James: "the LaserBeam graphic flashes for a fraction of a second, then it's replaced by LaserBeamAfterGlow which then slowly fades out with its opacity dropping to 0 over the next 1-2s. This will give the impression of a burn in effect after the powerful laser beam." The beam used to fade out over 1.2 seconds *bloom and all*, which is a beam still firing faintly for over a second; a burn is the opposite shape, everything at once and then something left behind that is no longer the shot. 0.12s of flash and 1.5s of burn. **The two pictures need no lining up at all**, which is the part worth knowing: measured off the files, `LaserBeamLength` and `LaserBeamAfterGlow` are both 372 across at 3x with their opaque core running pixels 153 to 218 - the afterglow *is* the beam with the glow either side taken away - so one sizing function serves both and the burn lands exactly where the beam was rather than approximately. **The afterglow is added first and outlives the beam** rather than being swapped in: "replaced by" read literally is a remove and an add on one frame, which is a frame with an empty column if either action slips, and the shared core means it is invisible underneath until the beam goes. |
| **Round 287: every strip the paddle wears, and two formations that were owed** | **(a) The split cuts up all four of the paddle's overlays.** Round 286 fixed the laser dress and the spec had been carrying a note since round 233 that the rest would do the same thing: the paddle wears four strips - the laser dress, the sticky band, and the Retro theme's own two - each sized to the whole paddle and drawn over it, and a split paddle keeps its node's full width, so every one of them goes on being painted across the gaps. A sticky-looking paddle with holes in it, which is the exact fault round 233 fixed for the Retro paddle art and called out as reading like a bug rather than like the power-up. One function does all four now. **The two answers differ on purpose**: the lasers dress the two outermost pieces, because a laser comes from two turrets, and the sticky band dresses every piece, because a sticky paddle catches anywhere the paddle is. The source strip is hidden by *alpha* rather than by `isHidden`, since `isHidden` on these is how the power-ups themselves say whether they are running, and a second opinion in the same property would fight them. **(b) Colonnade and Rampart**, the two drafted sequences round 284 declined to write - see that row. **(c) `TwoRowShapesInFormationsTests`**, which is the check that made writing them safe: it walks every set row and every cluster in both catalogues and asserts that the cells a Big or Square brick hangs into are written empty, deriving the covered cells from `BrickSize`'s own two axes rather than restating "two across and two down". Every existing formation already obeyed it - the rule was real and simply had nothing enforcing it, which is a good part of why it was easy to believe there was none. **(d) A stale release blocker removed:** §12.0 still said the shaped-paddle play-test weight of 30 had to go back to 3 before release. Round 282 did it, and the note outlived the fix by four rounds - which is the same fault the note was written to prevent, one round up. |
| **Round 286: two turrets, not five, and the twist matrix enforced** | James on round 285's delivery. **(a) "Only the outermost section of paddle should have a laser turret on its outer edge. There should only ever be 2 turrets with a split paddle."** So round 285 had gone one step too far: it gave every piece a turret, which is a stronger power-up than the one that was asked for. Taken back out, and the interesting part is what was left - **the generator needed no change at all, and never had.** It alternates between `paddle.position.x` plus and minus half the span, the paddle node keeps its full width when it splits, and so the span's two ends *are* the outermost pieces' outer edges. The whole of what was ever wrong was the dress: one strip drawn across the entire span, saying "armed" about the gaps and about the middle pieces. Two pieces wear it now, which is the same count as the turrets and the same count as the shots, and `laserGenerator` is byte-identical to what it was before round 285 touched it. **(b) The twist matrix is enforced.** Round 285 recorded four cross-category pairs the daily could draw against James's matrix and left them alone, because refusing a pair changes what past days drew. James: "don't worry about the past daily challenges so far. We only need to worry about them once the app is released." So the refusal is a plain pool filter with no activation date on it. `DailyTwist.refusedPairs` is **every "No" cell in the sheet**, not only the four that were live - seven of the eleven were already impossible because a day draws at most one twist per category, and keeping them makes the table a copy of the matrix rather than a copy of the leftovers. `testTheCategoryRuleAlreadyCoversWhatItCovers` proves that overlap rather than assuming it, so the day a category is re-cut, the test says which refusals stopped being free. A year of generated days is walked to check the filter is actually applied, and the same test asserts the year still has plenty of two-twist days in it - a filter that quietly emptied every pool would have passed the first assertion on its own. **(c) The square ball was the render harness**, not a theme: it took its texture from another bare `GameScene`, which has none, because the ball sprite is loaded from `GameScene.sks` at run time and a scene built with `GameScene()` never loads it. SpriteKit drew the plain colour rectangle. The harness loads `ballNormal` now. |
| **The paddle family's three open cells, answered and built** | The matrix has been open since round 200 - "the safety paddle, mirrored paddle, split paddle power ups should match power ups of the main paddle" - and it was never a coding question. Three surfaces by eight effects, and the three cells nobody could answer from the code were put to James in round 284. **(1) A sticky safety paddle "goes up, like it would from the paddle".** The bar catches a ball landing on its top face and holds it there, and the launch is `endlessIILaunchAngle` - the same arithmetic the paddle's own held balls use, so a ball caught near an end leaves steeply and one caught in the middle leaves near enough straight up. All that changes is whose width the landing spot is a fraction of. **It joins the one queue**, because there is one order of launches in this mode and a second list would be a second order. **What it does not do is ride anything**: a held ball follows the paddle because the paddle is under the player's finger, and the bar stands in the middle of the field and never moves sideways - which is also why the *primary* ball can be caught here when the queue otherwise leaves it to `releaseBall`. The six-year-old code that moves the first ball with the paddle only runs while it is on the paddle, and a ball resting on the bar is not. **(2) "Each split has one laser turret on its far end"** - and, on the round after, "there should only ever be 2 turrets with a split paddle", which is the reading that stands (round 286's row above). The first attempt armed every piece; the answer was that the *generator* was already right and only the dress was wrong. **(3) "Yes - a mirrored paddle gets its own portal, and both send the ball to the top".** Three lines: the mirror asks the portal before it writes a bounce, exactly as the paddle does, and `applyEndlessIIPaddlePortals` neither knows nor cares which surface swallowed the ball. **The twin's portal spends the turn**, which is the one thing James did not say and the one worth a play-test: the mirror deliberately spends none of the paddle's turns for an ordinary bounce, but a portal is the whole of what a Portal Paddle turn buys, and a free one would make the mirror the way to farm the power-up rather than a second place to use it. It leaves `endlessIIPortalPaddleOwedTurn` alone - that flag is the paddle's promise to itself that an effect already paid for still lands, and a mirror contact in the same step consuming it would take the paddle's own portal away from it. ~~**Still open in the matrix:** Aimed Sticky and the shaped faces on the safety bar and the mirror, and the split's own sticky.~~ **Two thirds of that sentence was already wrong when it was written** (corrected round 292, and it is the audit's own lesson landing on the round that wrote the lesson). **The shaped faces are on both surfaces and have been since rounds 216 and 224**: `endlessIISafetyPaddleBody` and `endlessIIMirrorPaddleBody` each trace the shape's silhouette while one is running, and both hit paths call `endlessIIApplyShapedBounce` before their own arithmetic. **The split's sticky needs nothing**, because a split paddle is the same node with a compound body - a ball can only meet a segment, and a segment contact is a paddle contact, so `paddleHit` catches it exactly as it always has; round 287 gave it its own dress. **What is genuinely left is Aimed Sticky on the twin and the bar**, and the two are not the same size of job: the bar can hold a ball as of round 285, so it wants the aim pointed at a target held somewhere other than the paddle, where the mirror has no held-ball machinery at all. |
| **Play-test round 284: two power-ups that were the wrong shape, and a brick that would not say what it was** | James's five, and three of them are the same kind of finding as round 283's: correctly wired, wrong *shape*. **(a) Ball Control has inertia now.** "Ball control is too controlling over the ball... its momentum should take it slightly beyond the paddle and then swing back." What was there closed a fixed share of the gap every frame, and the defining property of an exponential approach is that it **cannot overshoot** - it comes in from one side and slows for ever. So "slightly beyond the paddle and then swing back" was not reachable by tuning the number, at any value, and the fix had to be a different kind of arithmetic: the ball is on a damped spring, with a sideways velocity of its own that the paddle's column pulls on. Two readable numbers describe the whole of it - 11 rad/s and a damping ratio of a half, which overshoots by a sixth of the distance and settles on the swing back. **The first value tried was 14 and the test argued it down**: at 14 the ball crossed the paddle's column at the tenth-of-a-second mark, which is no more lag than there had ever been, so the overshoot was there and the inertia was not. And the comparison the tests make is with *the old pull itself* rather than with a frame count, because "it arrives later" turned out to be the wrong claim - an exponential never arrives, so a spring that overshoots reaches the paddle sooner however slowly it sets off. What the player feels is the opening tenth of a second: 2% of the gap in the first frame where it used to be 16%. Round 209's frame-rate lesson is kept and made stronger - a spring integrated once per frame does not merely feel different at 120fps, it can gain energy and throw the ball out of the field, so the frame is consumed in fixed slices of a 240th. **(b) The Aura rides on the ball.** Four complaints - too small, drawn on top, lagging too far behind, and not growing with the ball - and three of them had one cause: the glow was a *sibling* on the scene, told the ball's position once a frame from `update`, which runs before the physics step. It was always drawn where the ball had been one frame ago, which at playing speed is most of its own width. It is the ball's child now, so it has no position of its own to be stale, a `zPosition` below zero puts it behind the ball's picture instead of over it, and it inherits the ball's scale - which is Increase and Decrease Ball Size answered for free, since those animate the scale. The reach is read off the ball's own width rather than off `ballSize`, so what the aura *eats* grows with it too and the picture does not lie. Ghosting stopped needing a line at all: round 182's alpha copy existed only because the glow was not a child, and SpriteKit multiplies a child's alpha by its parent's. **The size was the one real judgement.** `BallAura` fades to nothing at its own edge, so "how big is it" has no single answer; the drawn share had been set to the tenth-of-peak contour, and a tenth of a faint alpha is not something anybody sees. Moving it to the quarter contour draws the ring 23% wider and changes nothing the aura does. **(c) A directional brick keeps its own face.** "The open side should show the brick underneath. Right now, that side looks grey. The brick underneath can be any brick type." `tint` writes `colorBlendFactor = 1`, which does not shade a texture, it *replaces* it - so a directional Multi-hit and a directional Indestructible were the same grey oblong. The tint is conditional on there being no panel art now: where a panel is drawn it says which side is open and painting the brick as well only hides what it is; where one is not, the grey is the only mark there is. **(d) Aimed Sticky could not aim**, and this was a regression rather than a design fault. `touchesBegan` set `endlessIIAimTouchPredatesHold = endlessIIAimHold`, which is the inverse of what the name says: once an aim was running, every later touch was marked as *predating* it, so the release always read as "keep aiming" and the ball could never launch - and because the field's hold reads the same flag, the field stopped descending too. One word. **(e) The reference page's power-up brick and directional brick** are drawn from the real artwork. |
| **The Endless Mayhem brick sequences, drafted (nine in round 284, eleven from 286)** | James, round 284: "please start to draft sequences and I can play test them" - the heading §12.0 had held open since round 190. **Nine, and the rule behind the choice of them is one sentence: each is made of something the catalogue had never been made of.** The twenty-two designed rows drew on four brick kinds, four shapes, one size and three actions, while the field around them has five behaviours, four shapes, four sizes and fifteen styles - so there was no designed row anywhere that was Multi-hit, that breathed, that turned, that exploded, that could not be seen, that refilled itself or that went anywhere. A landmark is worth having precisely because it is the one row in a run that is unmistakably *about* something, and a landmark made of the same four things as the field is not one. In order of depth: **Seam** (60m, three blocks of Multi-hit - the shallowest, because it asks for nothing but persistence, and the one that shows twenty-two rows had never used the brick that takes four hits); **Bellows** (120m, a solid row of breathing bricks with no gap in it at all, passable because each shrinks to half a cell and swells back out of step with its neighbours - the shot is *timed* rather than aimed, which nothing else in the pool asks for); **Chevron** (140m, the Palisade's two halves facing each other so both sides feed the one open column - the first shape in the pool that helps); **Lattice** (140m, rhombi alternating with ordinary bricks, so a ball that gets in is passed along the row rather than sent back out); **Orrery** (160m, three spinners under a row left deliberately empty - the empty row is not decoration, a spinner sweeps wider than its cell and a formation cannot ask the row above it for anything); **Magazine** (170m, two charges buried in whatever the field was making anyway - the only shape here worth more than the bricks in it); **Mirage** (190m, six invisible bricks with three ordinary ones standing among them, which is the mazes' dotting rule applied to a row that cannot be *seen* rather than one that cannot be broken); **Wellspring** (210m, one spawner alone in an empty row - the emptiest formation in the catalogue and the only one that fills itself in); and **Conduit** (240m, a portal at each end of the field, which turns the two walls into each other). **Round 286 added the two sizes that had been left out:** **Colonnade** (130m, six Square bricks with a full-height corridor between each - the Comb turned ninety degrees, since a Square brick blocks a column where every other brick blocks a row) and **Rampart** (200m, three Big blocks with two clear cells between them, the heaviest thing in the pool by area and still ordinary bricks, because a wall of Big Indestructibles would be the maze problem again). They were held back for a round on the belief that no convention existed for the cells a two-row brick hangs into; four clusters had been obeying one all along. |
| **Shaped bricks get their lighting back, and the grip moves to Ball Spin** | James's round-262 delivery: updated Rounded for both Indestructibles and every retro type, **orientation-specific Wedges** for the Indestructibles and all of retro, the classic theme's **Convex, Concave and Diamond** for all six brick types, and the wedge and convex/concave sets carrying `0`, `90`, `180`, `270` suffixes - "the lighting on these bricks wouldn't look right in the rotated / mirrored versions". **He is right, and it is the one thing a reflected node cannot fix**: the face's outline is turned by scaling the shape node, and a picture inside it turns too, so a wedge lit from above is lit from below the moment it is flipped. Where the oriented art exists the sprite now cancels its parent's scale back out - the outline is turned, the lighting is not. **The number names the transform, not a rotation**, and how many there are depends on the shape: only the Wedge has a handedness worth varying, so it is drawn four ways (`180*mirrored + 90*flipped`) and Convex and Concave two (`0` and `180`, "right-side-up or upside-down"). **Pinned to the pictures rather than to a reading of them**: the mapping was worked out by looking at four thumbnails, which is the sort of evidence that is right most of the time, so a test compares the **alpha silhouettes** - the colours cannot be compared, since redrawing the lighting is the whole point, but the shape must survive the transform. **Two naming slips, both checked before being renamed**: `retroBrickWedge<deg>` drops the `Normal` every other retro name carries, and `BrickIndestructible1Concave90` is the *flipped* concave, which its own pair and every other two-way shape in the delivery calls `180`. **And the grip moved**: James, correcting round 261, "grip is for the ball spin, not ball steering" - which is what §5.4 and §8.5 had said three times, and is his own phrase from round 200. One line. |
| **The paddle art is complete, and the grip is a new power-up face** | James's round-261 delivery: the last three power-up badges (Portal, Wrap-Around, Ball Spin), round HUD icons for all three plus an updated Aimed Sticky and Sticky Paddle, the corrected `ballWreckingGigaRainbow`, **retro's fifteen shaped pictures** - paddle, lasers and sticky across five shapes - and a **grip** set: twelve themes by six, complete on arrival. One hundred and one new imagesets and four replaced. With retro answering for itself, every theme now has every shaped picture and the fallback has nothing left to catch: the test that *found* whichever picture was missing (round 258, after the one that *named* one went stale) has become a test of the fallback itself, on a name that will never exist. **Retro needed code as well as art**, because it is the one theme drawn as a decorated layer over the ordinary paddle sprite, with its own laser and sticky nodes taking their pictures from the scene file - there was nothing to swap them for until now, and there is now. **The grip is not a fourth overlay but a fourth reason for the one the paddle already has.** Sticky, Aimed Sticky and the grip all dress `paddleSticky`; the grip art is drawn to sit exactly where the sticky art sits, so every path that could already place a sticky face needed nothing said to it. Collecting it **ends** Sticky and Aimed Sticky outright - "it replaces the sticky and aimed sticky power-up and textures when caught", the power-ups and not only the pictures - and releases anything the face was holding. **Retro is the exception it always is**: its plain sticky picture is its own style and its grip is in everybody else's, so the grip goes on the shared overlay even there and retro's own layer stands down. **One thing to confirm, and it is worth confirming**: James's note says the grip is worn while **Ball Steering** (Ball Control) runs, and it is built that way. §5.4 and §8.5 have said three times that the grippy texture belongs to **Ball Spin** - it is his own phrase from round 200, "ball spin grippy texture" - and a paddle that grips the ball and spins it is the reading the picture suggests. Built as asked rather than as guessed; `endlessIIWearsGrip` is the one line that changes if the earlier note was the right one. |
| **The trajectory line, drawn rather than redrawn** | James, round 260, choosing against new artwork: "rather than me making a new graphic perhaps we can just improve what already exists... make the line more blurry and wider the further it gets from the ball. The line itself should be a blurry white with a giga-ball yellow/green glow... The line currently isn't consistent, it's a bit dotty. I'd prefer it to be more consistent / get more faded the further it is from the ball." **The dottiness was geometry, not art.** A segment is a rotated rectangle and consecutive segments are different widths - that is what the widening *is* - so butting them together shows a step at every join and overlapping them, on an additive blend, shows a band. Round 258 drew both and looked at both. The answer is neither: the picture now ramps to nothing across its **ends** as well as its edges, `centerRect` holds the ramp at a fixed size while the middle stretches, and each segment reaches one ramp past its own tail - so its fade-out lies exactly over the next one's fade-in and two linear ramps sum to one. **Past the tail only.** Reaching past *both* ends makes the overlap two ramps long where a linear crossfade only sums to one across a single ramp, and the first attempt at this came out striped, twice as bright at every joint - drawn, looked at, and fixed. **White core, green glow**: each segment is drawn twice, a wide faint one in the mode's own yellow-green under a narrow white one, same texture and same blend so it is two batches rather than two draws a segment. The glow reaches past the core by a share of the core's own width, so it opens out as the line widens instead of staying a fixed halo around a growing stroke. And the widening and blurring are both turned up, because they were tuned against an `SKShapeNode`'s glow, which spread further for the same figure than a stretched picture does. |
| **Aimed Sticky becomes a variant of Sticky** | James, round 260: "it should be another variant of the sticky power-up, so it should apply the sticky paddle texture. If the sticky power-up is caught during aimed sticky, the aimed sticky power-up should be maintained with the number of paddle hits reset. If aimed sticky is caught during sticky, it should become aimed sticky." So the two never run side by side any more. Collecting the aimed one **ends** any plain Sticky outright rather than leaving both clocks going and letting the launchControl group decide which answers a catch; collecting a plain one over an aim is a **refill** of the aim rather than a downgrade, and still counts and scores as the Sticky it was. One paddle, one face, one rule about where the ball goes. **The face is the tell**, and it needed a place to be taken off: the aim's clock *lingers* after its last turn so the ring can be seen ending, so there is no single moment to hang it on, and `refreshEndlessIIStickyFace` asks every frame whether anything still wants it - a plain Sticky underneath, a ball still held, a hold still owed. It only ever takes it off; putting it on is a collection, and a collection is a moment. **The aim line is the trajectory line now**, with no arrowhead - the two aids were always meant to speak one visual language (round 39) and were saying it twice, the second copy in the glowing shape nodes round 258 took out of the first for costing forty-six offscreen passes a frame. The aim line got the white core and the green glow for nothing, and keeps the one thing that was always different: an alpha floor, because this is the control the player is actively steering and a vanished tip is an aiming aid that has stopped aiming. **The rest of the request was already built** and is recorded here so it is not asked for a third time: the world stopped pausing in round 215, drag-above-aims / drag-on-or-below-carries has been the rule since the same round, a tap releases, and `endlessIIAimTarget` reads the head of the sticky queue, so Multi-Ball already shows one aim line on the next ball out. |
| **What is still to draw, asked rather than remembered - and what Mayhem Rules would really cost** | **The art lists are derived now.** Three separate tests went stale in three rounds by *naming* a gap James then filled - "Convex and Concave, both themes" became "retro's Convex", became "retro's Concave", became nothing at all - and each time the test was asserting that a picture which now exists did not. The mistake they share is that the gap was written down. `ArtStillToDrawTests` asks the catalogue instead: every brick type in every theme against every shape, and every paddle theme against every shape, in all four kinds. **Both lists come back empty.** The only two names it finds are the two that borrow on purpose - ice's plain sticky wears glass's and candy's plain lasers are `stripyLasers` - and those are named in the test so they cannot look like work outstanding. So §8.5's shaped-face and shaped-paddle lists are **closed**, and the test is now the forward-looking half: a new brick type, a sixth shape or a thirteenth paddle theme added without art fails there rather than quietly wearing a stretched rectangle until somebody sees it in play. **And Mayhem Rules is not the data change it reads as.** §4 has it as "Mayhem's power-ups drop in a Classic level... a curated list excluding anything that needs the descending field", which sounds like weights. It is not: **forty-three `gameMode == .endlessII` guards** sit across the Mayhem power-up files, and seven of the collect functions refuse outright outside the mode. Dropping them in Classic without going through those would hand the player power-ups that do nothing, which is exactly the lie round 211 refused. The twist is a round of its own - a review of every guard, one power-up at a time - rather than a curated list, and it is worth knowing that before it is scheduled. |
| **A spinning brick's light stays put while the brick goes round** | James, round 266: "when a brick with multiple variants is spinning, is it possible to fade in and out the corresponding variants so it looks like the light on the brick is changing as it spins? Each brick will have a maximum of 2 variants it can fade between as the 90 and 180 are mirrors of 0 and 270, so not the same shape." **His reasoning is the design.** The four pictures are a base and three *reflections*, and a spin is a **rotation** - so a turning brick only ever passes through the two a rotation can reach. Half a turn inverts both flags, so the pairs are {0, 270} and {180, 90}, and a spinning brick never looks like its own mirror. For the two-way shapes the pair is simply the only other picture. The blend is a cosine of the brick's own `zRotation` - nothing at rest, everything at half a turn, equal at the quarters where the brick is on its side and neither lighting is the right one - and it is driven from the frame like everything else that moves on a brick (§8.6). **The piece the tests could not have found**: the partner is the picture of a brick *already* standing at half a turn, so its silhouette is the base rotated 180 degrees, and laid in unturned it drew a wedge pointing the other way to the one the brick actually was - two triangles crossing rather than one changing colour. Turned back by 180 inside the brick, its outline lands on the brick's own at every angle and what shows through is the lighting. Nothing about the alphas or the texture names says any of that; the first version passed its tests and was wrong on screen, and the render is what said so. **And the render nearly missed it too** - it copied the sprites' texture, size, alpha and scale but not their `zRotation`, which is precisely the transform the fix uses, so the first re-render came back identical. A harness that copies some of what it is checking is a harness that can only find some of what is wrong. **And retro's shaped set finished while this was being built** - Convex and Concave in a 0 and a 180, and Diamond, for all six types, so **both themes now have every shape drawn** and §8.5's shaped-face list is closed. Three names needed placing rather than copying: `RetroBrickNormal…` and `RetroBrickInvisible…` are spelled with a capital R where the game spells those two with a small one, and asset lookups are case-sensitive, so as delivered they were pictures the game would never have found; and **`RetroBrickMultiHit3Diamond Copy` is MultiHit4's diamond** - it is the salmon pink of `RetroBrickMultiHit4` where the file it claims to copy is MultiHit3's red, and MultiHit4's diamond was otherwise missing. The same slip as `ballWreckingGigaCandy Copy` in round 183, and placed the same way: on what the picture actually is. **Three tests had to stop naming a gap.** "The shape with no art yet" was Convex and Concave in both themes, then retro's Convex, then retro's Concave, and it went stale three times in three rounds because James kept drawing them. What each of them was really holding - that a picture which does not exist answers nil rather than the blank `SKTexture(imageNamed:)` hands back - is now asked of a power-up brick and a name that will never exist, neither of which can be drawn away. |
| **The ball is held at the run's speed, every frame** | James, on the audit: "let's update the physics. To me this classes as a bug fix / improvement, so it's ok to change those older game modes." So `holdBallSpeeds` runs in `didSimulatePhysics` - §8.6's one safe place - after every other writer and just before the tripwire, so the tripwire judges the ball as it will actually travel. **The tooth is one frame long now**: a sixtieth of a per cent, where it was 2% over a two-second flight and 7.7% over an eight-second one. **Asked every frame rather than the damping simply set to zero**, because that catches drift from *any* source - the engine's own solver, a future writer - rather than from the one source measured, and because it leaves the damping doing its job where something wants it. It writes nothing on the frames where nothing has drifted, which is most of them. **Four things are left alone, and each is something deliberately changing the speed**: gravity, a ball resting on the paddle or held for an aim, a ball that is not moving, and a ball whose damping something has raised on purpose - which is how the Lose A Life animation brings the ball to a stop, and which a hold that fought it would have kept travelling for ever. **It reaches Classic and the original Endless**, which is the half James signed off, and there is a test on each saying so. Those two want a few levels played before this ships, because they are the boards with years of scores on them. |
| **The ball-jitter audit, and what it found** | The open half of round 200's item - "the continuous effects each write velocity per frame... and any of them interacting with a contact's own correction in the same frame is a speed wobble" - done by arithmetic rather than by waiting for a sighting. **Three of the four suspects are cleared.** Magnetism, Ball Control and Ball Spin are pure functions, so they can simply be asked: swept over headings all the way round at three speeds, every one of them changes the heading and leaves the magnitude alone to within a hundredth of a point. Random Bounce is swept with them, from the other side of round 258's fix. Gravity is the fourth and is *meant* to change the speed, which is why it cannot be cleared this way and is said so out loud rather than left as an absence. **And the drift is somewhere else entirely.** The main ball carries `linearDamping = 0.01` and is renormalised to `ballSpeedLimit` by `ballSpeedControl` at the end of every corrected bounce. Between two corrections it slows; at the correction it is put back in one frame. That is a sawtooth, and its size is the length of the flight: **0.5% over half a second, 2.0% over two seconds - exactly `CrookedBallTripwire`'s threshold - 3.9% over four and 7.7% over eight.** Which explains the shape of the report. *The power-ups do not cause it*; they lengthen the interval between snap-backs. A Ghost Ball passes through bricks without a corrected bounce, a Halo clears the field ahead of the ball, a Portal transit is long, and a Safety Paddle returns a ball that would otherwise have been lost - so "it seems to happen when certain power ups are enabled" is the sawtooth getting longer teeth. The extras are consistent and untouched: `EndlessIIMultiBall` gives them `linearDamping = 0` and `ballSpeedControl` only ever renormalises `ball`, so the ball that is snapped back is the one that slows. **Not fixed here, deliberately.** Both fixes - damping to zero, or renormalising every frame instead of at corrections - end with the ball travelling at exactly the run's speed always, and both are a change to physics shared with Classic and the original Endless, which carry years of leaderboard scores. That is James's call and wants a few levels of each played after it. |
| **Play-test round 259, built** | James's four, and three of them turned out to be one sentence each and one of them a hunt. **(a) The landing marker is bigger** - half again, and applied to the *triangle* rather than to the picture, because the picture is mostly glow and scaling it would have grown the halo faster than the mark inside it. The centre calculation reads the size, so the mark still lands exactly where the ball will. **(b) The overlays now sit on the paddle's own underside.** They were placed at `paddle.position.y - paddleHeight/2` - the *plain* paddle's underside - and a shaped paddle is taller and its node rises by half the growth to keep its own underside on the same line (round 213). So the laser and sticky were the height of that lift too high, by a different amount for every shape: a quarter of the paddle's height under a dome or a wedge, a tenth under a wave, and nothing at all on the plain paddle. Which is exactly "some of the graphics". `paddle.size.height` is the shape's height when one is on and the plain height when it is not, so the fix is one expression rather than a branch - and it is called from the shape refresh as well as the paddle move, or a shape collected while standing still would sit wrong until the player moved. **The first attempt at looking at this missed it**: a render that drew the three pictures against a shared bottom line by hand showed them all lining up, which proved only that the *art* shares a bottom line. Drawing them through the scene's own placement is what showed the wedges hanging in the air. **(c) One landing spends one turn.** `paddleHit` has one call site and spends one turn, so two turns is two calls - and `didBegin` is reported per contacting *fixture* pair rather than per node. A dish or a wave traced from its picture is several convex pieces, so a ball landing where two of them meet begins contact with both, and the plain paddle, being one rounded rectangle, never did. Guarded per ball per frame, because two balls genuinely can land in one frame and the same ball cannot land twice - and guarded around the whole of `paddleHit`, since the sound, the haptic and the bounce were equally being done twice. **(d) The goodbye second stops re-filling the ring.** It was built by overwriting `total`, `remaining` and `countsTurns` with a one-second timer, which had two consequences nobody asked for: the ring refilled and swept round a second time, and the segment marks came off because the clock had stopped counting turns. The goodbye has its own field now, so everything the ring reads about turns survives it and simply reads as spent - and a hit landing during it takes nothing off it, where it used to cost a whole second of the farewell. |
| **Random Bounce was broken in the one direction nothing had ever tested** | James, round 258: "Random bounce power up is still broken." It was, and this is the third round on it: round 180 narrowed the spread from 35 degrees to 25, round 185 changed the unit from degrees to a share of the angle, and the report did not move. **The bug was the sign.** `atan2` reports -180...180 and the arithmetic was written for 0...180, so clamping a ball *descending* at -45 degrees to "at least the minimum" returned **+10** - a fair descent slammed into a shallow climb, at every brick contact, for the whole ten seconds. And +10 is `minAngleDeg` exactly, which is the escape hatch's own trigger, so the shallow climb was then re-aimed with a random jitter frame after frame. That is the vibrating and glitching, and it survived two narrowings because **every test written for it passed an upward angle**. The sign is the ball's up or down now and never a magnitude. **The unit changed with it**: the nudge is a tenth of the *room* the bounce has above the minimum, measured from the nearer horizontal, rather than a tenth of a heading measured from the right-hand wall - which is what made 170 degrees, a ball skimming leftward, get seventeen times the nudge of the 10-degree bounce that is the same ball going the other way. A bounce with no room is handed back untouched, because the escape hatch owns those and two things rescuing one bounce was the other half of the vibrating. |
| **The stutter, measured rather than guessed at** | James, round 258, named four: Drift, shaped paddles, Ball Trajectory, Quicksand. Reading all four and picking the one that looks expensive is how the last two Random Bounce rounds went wrong, so `EndlessIIFrameCostTests` builds a 121-brick field and times each against a sixtieth of a second. **Every tick is cheap** - Drift 1.5% of a frame, the brick faces 1.5%, Quicksand 0.8%, the trajectory's arithmetic 2.6% - which is the finding that mattered, because it says the cost is not in the code that was being read. Two things it *did* find. **A shaped paddle traces its body in 8.8 milliseconds**, over half a frame, and the plain paddle is 7.5 - the cost is the picture, not the shape - so every shape collected, every Split Paddle, every safety bar and every mirror dropped a frame. `TracedBodyCache` traces each picture-and-size once and hands out `copy()`s after: **8.8ms becomes 0.002ms**, and the set of pictures is fixed at build time so it settles at a few dozen entries. **The Ball Trajectory lays forty-six glowing `SKShapeNode`s a frame, per ball**, each re-tessellated when it is handed a path and each rendered through an offscreen pass of its own - and Multi Ball puts four balls on the field. None of that appears in a timer around the tick, which is why it needed counting. They are sprites wearing one soft picture now (`FadingLine`), so there is no path to tessellate, no offscreen pass, and one batch instead of forty-six draws. **The blur comes from the stretch**: the picture is a solid core with a falloff either side, so a thin segment reads as a crisp line and a thick one as a blur, which is exactly what `glowWidth` was being asked for. The three numbers going in are the ones the shape node had. **Looked at, not assumed** - the line is rendered to a file and the first attempt at hiding the seams by overlapping the segments was drawn, looked at and thrown away: two soft bars laid over each other add up, so every join became a bright band and the line read as a ladder. Ends that meet exactly came out smooth. **What is left unmeasured**: Drift and Quicksand both move every brick's position every frame, which is a hundred-odd static physics bodies re-proxied on the physics thread, and no test on this side can see that. If either still stutters after this, that is where to look, and the fix is architectural - the field would have to move as one node, which §8.6 forbids while a brick's `position.y` is its row. |
| **One queue for everything new, and the two twists that empty it** | James, round 258: "Don't introduce new elements too quickly. Perhaps a new element every game view of bricks, so 22m. So that may be a new brick type, a new power-up, and new brick shape... The order in which elements are introduced should be randomised." **Five schedules were the problem, and each was sensible for itself**: a style every 35m, a power-up every 14m, a set row every 30m, a phase every 30m. Nobody had added them up - a player meets all of them at once, so the mode was introducing something new **about every seven metres**, a third of a screen. `EndlessIIElement` is the one queue: a style, a power-up, a multi-hit tier, a set row or a phase, shuffled **together** so the queue is not five blocks in a row, one arriving every 22m give or take this run's own spacing, with the quickening still on top so a deep run meets things faster - "start gentle but quickly ramp up". **Nothing that was already free stays queued**: the classic power-ups are never in it at all, Standard is what a field is made of before it is made of anything else, and a two-hit multi-hit brick is what Mayhem's multi-hit brick has always been. **Three and four hits are elements**, which is the first time either has existed in this mode - the textures count *up* to destruction, so a brick taking n hits starts at `MultiHit(5 - n)` and Mayhem's `MultiHit3` has always meant two. **A run saved before this keeps its own schedule**, because a schedule is a promise made to a run in progress. **The density steps follow the queue now**, which is both a fix and the truer reading: they used to walk the style order and assume it was in height order, which the shuffle broke - the gap went negative, clamped to a metre, and landed the step on the very metre the style arrived, which is the one thing that function exists to prevent. **What the number produces**, worked out rather than felt: a run opens with its opening set already in play - two to five styles, four to ten power-ups, two to five set rows, three to six phases, so eleven to twenty-six things - and then meets about **five new ones by 100m, thirteen by 200m, twenty-six by 300m and fifty by 500m**, with the whole hundred-odd out around 900m. That is a genuine slowing: the old five clocks had handed over about fourteen by 100m. If it is too gentle in play, `elementIntroductionSpacing` is the one number to move. |
| **The daily's Mayhem is the same day for everybody** | James, round 258: "Is there a way to make sure the endless mayhem in the daily challenge is still random for everyone, but the element introduction for that game's day is similar, so the challenge for everyone is similar so the leaderboard is fair?" Yes, and the answer is three lines, because the two halves were already separate things. What a run *knows* is the schedule - which elements it has met, in what order, at what pace - and what a run *is* is the field, rolled brick by brick as it descends.
