Seeding the schedule from the date makes everybody's day the same shape without touching a single roll the field makes: two players on the same day meet the same things at the same depths, and neither one's field is the other's. `EndlessIIProgression.make` takes a generator now, which is `SystemRandomNumberGenerator` for an ordinary run and a `DailySeededGenerator` for a daily. **Its own offset in the seed space** (`0xE2E2`), like every other stream the day draws, so adding a twist later cannot silently redraw everybody's elements. And it is the *day's* key, not the attempt's, so a practice run is practice of the day you will play. **Two twists come with it**, both Mayhem's alone because what they turn off is Mayhem's queue: **Full Deck** opens the whole thing at the first metre and leaves rarity alone, and **Level Pegging** does that and levels the odds as well - every behaviour, every style, every phase and every power-up as likely as any other, which is a genuinely strange field and is the twist doing what it says. They share a new `disclosure` category, so a day never draws both, and both are dated 2026-12-01 - the category too, which is the trap §4.2 documents: a new *category* rewrites what every past day rolled unless it is dated. |
| **The outline theme's sticky pad was the wrong way up** | James, round 257: "it turns out I had given 2 outlineStickyConvex files, one of which was actually outlineStickyConcave". Confirmed before replacing anything - the installed `outlineStickyConvex` art was **byte-identical** to the newly delivered `outlineStickyConcave`, so the outline theme has been putting a dished pad on a domed paddle since round 248. Both are right now, and `outlineStickyConcave` exists at last, which was the one missing picture in the shaped set. **Nothing automated would have caught this**: both files were present, both loaded, and the fallback never fired - the art was simply the other shape. It needed looking at, which is what §13's rule about visual work is for. The audit that found it also lists what is still absent: retro's fifteen shaped pictures, which James has said are still to come, and the plain `iceSticky`, which is not a gap - ice borrows `glassSticky` deliberately, and candy's plain lasers are `stripyLasers` for the same historical reason. |
| **The wall, measured rather than counted** | The bookings can be right and the bricks still wrong: a Big brick hangs off its top-left cell and is drawn about an anchor, which is exactly the arithmetic that comes out half a cell off and looks deliberate. So a test builds a Monolith row's bricks and reads their **frames** - none may overlap, and the channel has to be at least a brick wide rather than a seam between two of them. And the whole thing was put on the simulator: a clean build, the symbol checked in the dylib rather than trusted (`nm` for `endlessIIMonolithWall`), installed, launched, and a run started. The opening field builds and plays as it did. |
| **Monolith, and the row that can hold more than one shape** | §6.2's eighteenth phase, and the last one that could be built: "one enormous Big brick formation with a narrow route". It was recorded as blocked eight rounds ago and the block was real - a row held **one** shape, because anything taller than a row is booked by the row below it and `endlessIIPendingBuild` was a single slot. Four Big bricks abreast is four reservations from one row, so the slot is a list now. **The rule underneath did not change**: a row either reserves or builds, never both, so there is still only ever one generation of bookings in flight and it is still the row below that leaves the cells empty. **And every other phase still books one shape to a row** - more than one roll can come up and only the first is ever taken, which is the density the mode was play-tested at; booking several is a thing a *phase* asks for, and this is the phase that asks. A test walks the other seventeen and says so. **The phase builds itself**: a Big brick is two rows tall, so it alternates a row of holes with a row of wall without being told to, and what comes out is solid except for a channel one or two columns wide. The channel is **rolled once and held for the phase**, because a route that moved from row to row is a wall with gaps rather than a way through - which is the whole difference between this and a dense stretch of Giants - and it is forgotten when the phase ends so the next monolith's channel is somewhere else. Density 0.5, because the wall is booked rather than drawn from the density and all that governs is the bricks in the channel. **Counted as a uniform phase**, which it is more completely than the other four: they settle a choice once and this one has no choice left to settle. That is what keeps "no uniform phase is commoner than a varied one" true at weight 3, the rarest in the mode. **Palisade Post and Hourglass come back as they were first drawn** - three posts abreast, and two courses of them round a pinch - because the thing that condemned them a round ago was the one-shape rule, and Plumb Line stays beside them. **That leaves Downpour**, and Downpour is not a code problem: the field steps down when the bottom row empties rather than on a timer, so "the descent runs faster" has no lever to pull and would have to mean something new. It is the one phase in §6.2 still waiting on James. |
| **The Square a formation could ask for and never got** | Round 247 added the Square size, round 250 taught a formation to book a Big brick, and round 251 wrote two formations out of squares. Nothing joined them up: the booking asked whether a cell's size was **Big** rather than whether it was **taller than a row**, so a `.square` cell validated, built, and came out as an ordinary oblong. Palisade Post and Hourglass were *about* their squares and both were quietly being drawn as plain rows of bricks - a shape that validates, builds, and is not the shape, which is the exact failure the format's tests exist to catch. `endlessIIBookFormationShape()` books either size now. **And the brick a formation booked wears what the formation asked for.** A Big or Square brick is built by its own function from the field's own mix, which is right for a rolled one and wrong for a drawn one: a formation asking for a multi-hit Big brick was getting a standard one, with nothing anywhere to say so. `endlessIIPendingSpec` rides beside the pending slot and is set, cleared and reset in the same places, because the two are one booking; a rolled shape clears it rather than trusting it to have been consumed. **Three rules a formation has to obey, now checked**, all three from the same fact - a brick taller than a row is booked by the row below it: it needs a row below it, the cells it grows into have to be drawn empty, and there can be one per row because a row has one pending slot. The check condemned both of the shapes written the hour before it. **Palisade Post is staggered now**, a post per row with the row under each drawn empty, and **Hourglass is gone**: two courses of three squares is six of them and a row can build one. In its place, **Plumb Line** - a multi-hit course with a single weight hanging off the middle of it, which is the easy shot under four hits a brick, so the shape gets played from the bottom up whether or not that was the plan. |
| **The emission order becomes a function, so a test can drive it** | Round 249 found every designed formation being drawn upside down and fixed it with two `.reversed()` calls at two assignment sites. The test written with it seeds the queue *by hand* and then asserts what comes out of it - which passes whether or not either `.reversed()` is still in the code, so the thing that had just been wrong was the one thing not covered. `endlessIIQueueFormation(rows:legend:)` is the reversal said once, and the tests call it: one drains a three-row shape and reads it back up the field, and one watches the legend go out with the last row and not before, because `A` means something different in every formation and a key that outlived its shape would dress the next one's bricks. **The drain takes exactly as many rows as it queued**, not "until it returns nil" - an empty queue rolls for a new formation, so draining past the end would pick one up about one run in six and this would be a test that failed for no reason. **And the last of round 213's open clauses is struck**: Wave rode the `.wavy` case because the power-up was called Wave Paddle, and round 229's workbook renamed it back to Wavy Paddle, so the case says what the power-up is. The artwork keeps James's `Wave` prefix, which is a filename and not a name a player reads. That leaves the play-test drop weight of 30 as the one thing in this table still owed before release. |
| **Four shapes made of a size, and two phases that need a decision** | Tiny, Square and Big all became things a formation could name inside three rounds, so these are the shapes that could not have been drawn before: **Palisade Post** is three square bricks with gaps between them, and every other brick in the field is wider than it is tall - these block a *column*, so a shot that would have cleared an ordinary brick meets this one. **Gravel** is a ring of quarter-cell bricks around a hole: sixty-odd bricks, none worth aiming at, and a target in the middle that is easy to reach and worth nothing. **Hourglass** puts two courses of squares round a pinch. **Millstone** is a Big brick with a column of fallers beside it and a floor of them underneath - clear the floor and the column comes down, clear the column and the Big brick is left standing on nothing, because a Big brick is not a faller and does not follow. ~~**And two of §6.2's phases turn out to need James rather than code.** *Monolith* is "one enormous Big brick formation", and a formation can place one Big brick per row because a row has one pending slot - an enormous one needs several per row, which means more than one reservation outstanding at a time and is a real change to the rule that a row either reserves or builds.~~ **Monolith was built in round 254**, which made the row hold more than one shape - `endlessIIMonolithRoute` is the route it keeps open - and this note was never struck (found in round 295). **One** of §6.2's phases needs James rather than code, not two. *Downpour* is "the descent runs faster for the phase", and there is no cadence to speed up: the field steps down when the bottom row empties, not on a timer, so Downpour would have to mean something new. Both are left alone rather than guessed at. |
| **A formation can draw a Big brick** | The last of the three things round 246 unlocked, and round 249 is what made it easy. A Big brick spans two rows and cannot be built when its turn comes, so one row leaves the cells empty and the next builds down into them - and rows are emitted bottom-first, so **the row that reserves comes out before the row the brick is drawn on**. That is only answerable by looking one row *ahead*, which a formation can do and a dice roll cannot: the queue holds the whole shape, so a drawn Big brick is already written down where a rolled one has to guess a row in advance. **Only the top-left cell carries the spec**, and the other three have to be drawn empty - a rule with a test over the whole catalogue, because a formation asking for two bricks in one place would build both and come out wrong in a way that reads as a generator bug rather than as a grid with a typo in it. **The formation gives way to a reservation the generator has already made.** A row either reserves or builds, and the roll for this row has happened by the time a formation row is asked for; one brick missing from a shape beats two shapes arranging the same cells and neither surviving. **Keepsake** is the first shape to use it: a Big Indestructible block on a multi-hit floor, where what is worth having is the room beside it and the block is in the way of the shot that gets there. |
| **Every designed formation was being drawn upside down** | Found while working out which row of a formation a Big brick would have to be reserved by, and it is older than the formations themselves. **The field descends**: `moveEndlessModeRowDown` takes everything down a row and `buildNewEndlessRow` puts the new one at a fixed y at the top. So the row emitted *first* ends up *lowest* - and the queue was drained from the front, which laid every shape out bottom-first. **Both catalogues have always documented "top row first"**, so the shapes were authored correctly and the queue was reading them backwards. It survived thirty rounds because most of the catalogue is symmetrical - a ring, a cross, a diamond and a chequer look the same either way up - and because a shape that is upside down still looks deliberate. **What gave it away is the six that say which way up they go**, and all six were saying the opposite of what the field drew: the Anvil's "indestructible lid with the field's own bricks underneath it" had its lid at the bottom, the Vault's lid sat under the thing it was lidding, Pillars had bricks hanging off the *tops* of its posts, and Warren and Bastion both had their "mostly closed lid" as the floor. Reversing the queue as it is filled fixes all of them at once, which is the right shape of fix: thirty shapes were correct and one line was not. **The Funnel is the one whose comment now needs changing rather than its grid** - it said "opens downward", which was a true description of what the field was drawing, and it is the shape that made the bug hard to see, because a funnel the wrong way up is still a funnel. |
| **The shaped paddles wear the player's theme** | James delivered 164 pictures: five shapes for eleven of the twelve themes, each with its laser and its sticky overlay, with retro still to draw. 149 are new and 15 replace the regular theme's. **The installing was the small half.** Every shaped paddle wore `regularPaddleConvex` and its two overlays whatever the player had chosen - the theme prefix was written into the lookup - so a candy player catching a Convex Paddle watched their paddle turn white for five turns and change back. `paddleThemePrefixes` mirrors the order `paddleSetting` indexes `paddleTextureArray` by, and `endlessIIThemedShapeArt` builds the name from it. **The fallback is per picture, not per theme**, which matters twice over: retro has no shaped art at all and falls back on all fifteen, while outline is missing exactly one sticky overlay and keeps its own fourteen. A paddle with no texture is an invisible paddle, so borrowing the plain theme's picture is the honest answer to a file that is not drawn yet. **The body follows for free** - the paddle's physics body is traced from whatever texture it wears (round 213), so each theme's shape bounces off its own silhouette with nothing else being told. Seven tests, and the one that earns its keep walks all 180 names and asserts each resolves to artwork the catalogue holds; a second asserts the themes are genuinely *using* their own, since a fallback that fired everywhere would look right and be the whole feature failing silently. **Two notes for James**: outline's Concave sticky overlay is the one picture missing, and the ice theme has no *plain* sticky paddle at all - it borrows glass's, which was invisible while ice had no shaped art either and will now read as a mismatch. |
| **The Square size, and a brick that is not square in cells** | The first of the three things round 246 unlocked, and it was the one §12.0 had been describing for a while: "the power-up brick already builds one, so the machinery exists; it wants the reserve-and-build sequence generalised out of `EndlessIIPowerUpBricks` and offered as a size". `EndlessIITallBrick` is that plan with the power-up taken out of it, and a Square brick is an ordinary brick built to it - drawn from the field's own mix rather than always Standard, because it is a *size* and not a kind. **One cell across and two down, which is square on screen**, since a cell is twice as wide as it is tall. It is the only brick taller than it is wide, so it blocks a column where every other brick blocks a row. **`BrickSize` had to grow a second axis.** Its whole model was "extent in half-cells, on both axes" - a perfectly good description of three sizes that scale uniformly and no description at all of a fourth that does not, which would have drawn a Square brick as a Big one in the grid, in the reference page's picture and in its own size. `halfCellsWide`/`halfCellsTall` and `scaleWide`/`scaleTall` replace the single pair, and `BrickGrid` - dormant, used only by its own tests - was corrected with them. **And `endlessIISizeOf` had been asking about width alone**, which cannot tell a Square brick from a Normal one: they are the same width. It asks width first and then height, in that order, because a Big brick is two cells on both axes and the other order would call *it* Square. The style rules needed no change at all: everything that demanded `size == .normal` already refuses it for the right reason, since its sprite hangs off its node exactly as a Big one's does. |
| **The two-row sequence becomes a value** | Groundwork, and the one piece three queued items all wait on. Anything taller than a row cannot be built when its turn comes - rows arrive one at a time from the top with nothing above them - so the field builds those over two rows, one leaving the cells empty and the next building down into the gap. That sequence lived as **four optional columns on the scene**, one per shape, each cleared at the top of the planning function and checked in a fixed order: four ways of saying one thing, four properties to clear on a reset, and four places to remember when a fifth shape arrives. It is `EndlessIITwoRowBuild` and one pending slot now, because a row either reserves or builds and so there is only ever one outstanding. **The spinner stops being a special case.** It is not tall - it is one cell that sweeps a circle wider than its own - so it runs the same sequence over three rows: reserve below, place, book the clearance above. Written as two shapes rather than as an exception, because "what does this row leave empty" and "what does this row build" are the same two questions for it as for everything else, and they are the only two questions the type answers. **No behaviour changed**, which is the whole of what the suite had to say - and nine new tests say what the old chain of early returns never could: that a shape clears exactly what it reserved, that a spinner is the one that does not, that nothing reaches outside the field, and that a row which owes a shape books nothing new. **What it unlocks**: the 2x1 square size (§12.0), a formation asking for a Big brick rather than rolling for one, and §6.2's Monolith, which wants both. |
| **Windfall and Static, two phases the spec had and the game did not** | §6.2 lists eighteen phases and fourteen were built. These are the two of the remaining four that need nothing else first - Monolith wants Big bricks inside a formation, and Downpour wants a hold of the descent cadence, so both wait. **Windfall is a gap rather than a table**: it halves `powerUpProbFactor`, which is the one-in-n roll a destroyed brick makes, so whatever the level allocated gets rainier instead of being overruled - a pack with its own odd weighting stays that pack, only wetter. It is applied *before* the daily twists, deliberately: a Power Shower or a Drought is the day's headline and a phase is a stretch of field, so the day keeps the last word. Its density is Standard's, because "normal density, noticeably more power-ups" is the whole of it. **Static turns the flashers' stagger round.** Everywhere else they are staggered on purpose - "or a whole row would breathe in unison" - and that sentence is exactly what this phase wants. It takes all three numbers, not just the start: a field that begins in step and holds each brick solid for a different length drifts apart within a few blinks, which is a phase that looks right for two seconds. Density is a little under Standard's, because a field that goes passable all at once is generous enough without being crowded too. Gated at 250m against §6.2's High, with Windfall at 60. **Six tests, and two of them are about the phases that are *not* these two** - that only a Windfall moves the drop rate and only a Static flashes in step, since a lever added to an enum is a lever every case now has an answer for. |
| **The fall measures frames, and Tiny bricks can fall** | The last piece of "any size can take any motion", and it needed the fall rewritten rather than relaxed. It walked the occupancy map a whole row at a time, and that map can say how full a cell is but never *where* in it the space is - so a quarter-cell brick dropped a row went straight through the three siblings it shares a cell with. **`endlessIIWanderLimits` had the same problem going sideways** and solved it in round 175 by measuring against the frames of the bricks beside it; this is that answer turned ninety degrees, and it happens to serve every size at once - the Big case that round 237 built by hand falls out of it for free. **Everything but a Tiny brick still comes to rest on a row centre**, because a brick's `position.y` *is* its row (§8.6) and one resting half a row off is cleared at the wrong moment or blocks generation for ever. The snap rounds **up**: a brick that has come to rest on a Tiny one is half a cell off the grid, and rounding down would push it into the thing it just landed on, so it sits a little high instead. A Tiny brick is not snapped at all - it lives off the row centres by design and lands on the quarter-cell grid on its own, because whatever it came to rest on is itself on the grid. **The stack still resolves in order**: a table of resting rectangles is carried through the pass and each landing written back before the next brick is asked, which is what the mutable fill map was doing before. A brick about to be destroyed by an anchor claims nothing, so the next faller down the column may have the space. |
| **Twenty-one new formations, Tiny inside them, and the size rule said once** | Fifteen clusters and six set rows, all using the legend, so what a cell *is* can be part of the drawing: the Palisade is a row of slopes all facing one way, which is a redirection rather than an obstacle; the Weir is domes hanging point-down; the Portcullis only takes damage from below and has the reason to be there sitting underneath it; the Hatchery and the Powderkeg are the same cross of four bricks and opposite problems. **Tiny works inside a formation**, and needed almost nothing: a quarter-cell set is one brick split where it already stands, which is a thing that can be done after the row is laid down. Nothing else on a Tiny spec is applied - every style a quarter-cell brick cannot carry is refused when the legend is authored, and the ones it can would have to go on all four quarters or none, which the format has not been asked to decide. **Big is still refused**: it spans two rows, so it has to be reserved by one and built by the next, and that machinery owns the row it runs on. **The size rule now lives in `EndlessIIStyle.suits(_:BrickSize)`** and this is the round that found out why it had to: it was written twice, in `endlessIICanTake` and again as hand-typed sentences on the reference page, and round 237 changed the rules and moved only one copy. The page has been telling players that Fixed is Normal-only and Moving is Tiny-and-Normal ever since, when both take any size - inside a reference page whose own header says its facts are derived so it cannot disagree with the game. A test pinned the wrong answers too. **And a phase of nothing but Big or nothing but Tiny already exists**: Giants (90% Big, gated 200m) and Miniatures (100% Tiny, gated 150m), both at the rarest weight, which is why they are seldom seen rather than why they are missing. |
| **The builder reads the legend** | The other half of round 238, and it turned out to be a small change in the right place. A designed cell becomes an `EndlessIIBrickSpec` instead of a texture, the spec's behaviour picks the texture, and anything else it asks for is put on by `applyEndlessIIDesignedSpecs` after the generator's own styling passes have run. **`endlessIIStaysPlain` stays on**, which is the point rather than an obstacle: the generator's passes skip a designed brick entirely, so a shape somebody arranged is never handed a random spinner, and the styles it wears are the ones written beside it in the catalogue. **The old six characters go through the same path** - they are `EndlessIIBrickSpec.classic`, a legend everybody shares - so there is no legacy branch to drift, and a test pins that the thirty-one existing shapes build exactly what they always built. **The legend travels beside the queue rather than inside it.** Only one formation is ever in flight, since the queue is drained before another can start, so one legend is always the right one; it is cleared as the last row goes out, because `A` means something different in every shape. **Orientation and open side are the first things a legend buys.** `makeFace` keeps an orientation the brick already carries rather than rolling one, and `makeDirectional` keeps a side - both bargains made years ago for the resume path, and both exactly what a formation needs to say "these all face the same way". Millrace is that: two rows of wedges a half turn apart, so their slopes run parallel and the shape does what a funnel does without being drawn as one. It is in the generator's pool now. **Size is refused rather than ignored** (`sizeNotYetBuildable`). The builder places one brick per cell; a Big one needs the two-row reserve and a Tiny one is four quarters, and neither can be asked for while a row either reserves or runs a pattern. Dropping the size silently would have put an ordinary brick where somebody drew a large one, which is a shape coming out wrong with nothing to say why - the fault is meant to be deleted by the round that builds it. **One old test had to be taught the new rule**: it asserted the alphabet was exactly six characters, and now asks whether anything defines the character rather than whether it is one of the six. |
| **A formation format that can reach the whole game** | The alphabet a designed shape was written in had six characters - empty, ordinary, multi-hit, two indestructibles, and "whatever the generator would have put there" - and every designed brick carried `endlessIIStaysPlain`, which `endlessIICanTake` refuses outright. So thirty-one formations were built from four kinds of brick while the field around them had four types, five shapes, three sizes and nine actions to draw on. **A legend per formation, not more letters.** One character per cell is what keeps the shape visible in the source, and more letters does not survive the combinations: a brick is a type *and* a shape *and* a size *and* up to two actions, which one character cannot carry however many are left in the alphabet. So `A` means whatever this formation says it means, a formation rarely needs more than three or four distinct kinds, and the old six characters are just a legend everybody shares - `EndlessIIBrickSpec.classic` - so every existing shape keeps working with no edit at all. **The spec asks and never rules.** Whether a brick can exist is `EndlessIIStyle`'s question and it already has an answer, so the validator asks `stacksWith` and `suits` rather than carrying a second copy of the compatibility grid - the day the matrix changes, the check changes with the game rather than against it. `EndlessIIFormationCatalogue` is one derived list over both tiers, so a shape that exists is a shape that is checked. **It caught one on its first run**: Studs was six Indestructibles and nothing else, which is exactly the wall that flies past with nothing to earn that round 190's dotting rule condemns, and the same fault the Comb had. It has two ordinary bricks between the posts now. **Millrace is the first shape written with a legend** - two rows of wedges whose orientations are *named* rather than rolled, so both slopes agree and the shape does what a funnel does without being drawn as one. ~~It is validated and deliberately **not** in the generator's pool: the builder still reads six characters, so a legend shape would build as holes where its own letters are, which is worse than not existing. `pendingLegendSupport` is where it waits.~~ **Millrace has been in the pool for a long time** (struck in round 313, found by a sweep of every symbol this table names against the code). The "next, in order" below was done: `endlessIIQueueFormation(rows:legend:)` carries the legend, `endlessIISetRowLegend` holds it for the row, and `applyEndlessIIDesignedSpecs` gives every designed brick exactly what its legend asked for. So Millrace is picked by weight like anything else, its wedges face the way it names, and `pendingLegendSupport` is a symbol that no longer exists because the thing it gated no longer needs a gate. **This is the worst kind of stale note of the four found this round**: the others described work as unfinished, and this one described a shape as *held back from players* when it has been shipping. **Next, in order**: teach the builder to carry a spec past the styling passes (`endlessIIStaysPlain` becomes "plain unless the legend says otherwise"), then size inside a formation, which collides with the reserve-and-build rule that a row either reserves or runs a pattern. |
| **The bricks page takes the workbook's headings, the twists take its names, and Big learns to move** | Three from James, and two corrections of my own. **The bricks page is five sections** - Brick Types, Shapes, Sizes, Actions, New Brick Types - which are the workbook's own, in its own order. "Styles" had been fourteen entries under one heading covering three unrelated ideas: what a brick is shaped like, what it does, and two bricks that are neither. The States table is said on the entry it belongs to rather than as a section, because the entry already draws every state in a row above the words, and the count is read off `BrickTypeIcons.states` so a brick that gains one cannot be described here as having the old number. **Three twists were called the wrong thing**: Spare Balls, Mayhem Bricks and No Pausing were built before the twist workbook existed and it calls them Extra Balls, Extra Mayhem and No Breaks. The enum cases keep their old spellings - a case name is a key in the save and in `retirementKey`, and renaming one would change which day is which for every day already played. A test now holds the workbook's twenty names, because the document is not something the build can read. **Time Trial was taking lives** and the workbook's Details column says "unlimited lives" outright: ninety seconds *and* three balls is two limits where the design asks for one, and a bad start ended the attempt with a minute still on the board. **Big bricks can now fall, wander and anchor.** The fall asks about every cell of the footprint and the row under its *lowest*, not the cell below the node - which for a Big brick is part of the brick, never free, so it would never have fallen at all; asking one column would have been worse, settling it with its other half inside a neighbour. Moving was freed by round 175 without anybody noticing: the limits stopped working in cells and started measuring frames, and a frame is a frame at any size. An anchored Big brick holds all four of its cells. **Tiny is the one thing left out**, and only for Gravity: four of them share a cell, so "is the space below free" is a question the occupancy map cannot answer for one, and a whole-row drop would put it through its own siblings. The fix is to measure frames the way the wander limits do. **Two corrections**: the matrix says Spinning and Breathing go together and I had them refusing each other, and the workbook says a Portal "can take on a different shape, size, action" while round 235 refused it a shape. Both now follow the document. **And a bug the shapes have carried since they landed**: `endlessIIFill` measured a brick's share of its cell off the *sprite*, which for a shaped brick is a sixth of a cell by area - so every dome and wedge read as under the blocking threshold and simply was not there to a faller or a wanderer. Nothing asked until round 235, because until then a shape refused every style that asks. |
| **Portal states what it takes, Fixed grows teeth, Spawner rolls its own shape** | The rest of the brick workbook. **Portal's compatibility is one rule instead of seven refusals** (James: "figure out what actions portal is compatible with and allow those. Disallow the others"). `takenByAPortal` names the three - Rounded, Spinning, Gravity - because it is the shorter list and the honest one: seven scattered pairs read as seven separate arguments when a Portal only has two. **It is struck, never damaged**, so Directional, Fixed, Exploding, Spawner and Flashing all have nothing to attach to; Exploding and Spawner would fire on every *hit* on a brick that is never destroyed, which is a doorway that clears or refills its own neighbourhood each time you use it. **Its mouth is a fixed target or it cannot be aimed at**, which rules out Moving and Breathing and does not rule out Gravity (a faller comes to rest) or Spinning (it never leaves its cell, and nothing reads the angle of a brick that absorbs the ball). **A Fixed brick now destroys what runs into it**, not only what descends onto it. Two ways in, one answer: a Gravity brick lands on the anchor and is destroyed on arrival rather than in mid-air - it has to be *seen* to run into it - and a Moving brick stops treating an anchor as a wall to turn round at, walks in, and is destroyed by the overlap sweep on the frame it arrives. That sweep was restricted to Big bricks, on the reasoning that nothing else could ever share a cell; a wanderer can now, so the restriction went. **The inset was always what kept a near miss safe** and it still is: two bricks in touching cells, each inset by a quarter of a cell, leave half a cell of daylight. A brick already resting on an anchor from a build before this is destroyed where it stands, which the "did it actually move?" guard would otherwise have let sit there for ever. **A Spawner draws between one and eight** ("this number and the position of the new bricks around the spawner should be randomised"). It filled every cell it could reach, which made it the most predictable brick in the mode: one in the open always produced the same ring and one against a wall always the same half of one. Shuffled *before* it is trimmed, so which cells get bricks is drawn as well as how many - trimming the neighbour list in its natural order would have given a count that varied and a shape that never did. The floor is one rather than zero: a hit that visibly produces nothing reads as a brick that failed rather than as one that rolled low. |
| **Shape and action become two axes** | The round the rest of the brick workbook was waiting on (James: "this was always the intention, I just didn't have it mapped out until now"). A shaped face refused eight styles; it refuses three, and each of the three is a contradiction rather than a cost - **Rounded** is itself an outline, **Directional** reads a hit against a rectangle's four sides ("directional bricks are always the standard shape"), and a **Portal** is struck rather than bounced off, so the angle a slope gives is an answer to something that never happens. The five that left - Spinning, Gravity, Moving, Fixed, Breathing - turned out to be about *where a brick is* rather than what its outline is. **What they needed was one honest answer to "how much room does this brick take up".** `makeFace` tucks the sprite into a rectangle inside the silhouette, about a third of a cell, so every one of them was asking the sprite and being told a shaped brick was a Tiny one sitting off the grid - the occupancy map, both size predicates, the wander limits and every action's glyph. `endlessIIFieldSize` is that answer, read off the face's own path so it cannot drift from the shape drawn. **A second door on the same trap**: a combination the grid allows and the generator can never produce looks exactly like one that is very rare. The shapes shared the appearance pool with Breathing, and `applyEndlessIIStyles` offers one style per pool - so a brick could have a shape *or* breathe, whatever `stacksWith` said. **The shapes have their own pass now**, first of three, which is what "a separate axis" means in code. It makes shaped bricks more common, and that is the point rather than a side effect: an axis that has to win a raffle against the other axis is not a separate axis. **Breathing rebuilds its silhouette** rather than stretching its sprite - the drawn half every frame, the traced body in steps, the arrangement `advanceBreather` already used. **The matrix's seven new refusals** are in (Spinning with Fixed, Gravity, Exploding, Spawner; Breathing with Gravity and Spawner; Moving with Spawner) and all of them are one objection applied consistently: one style answers in cells, the other has left the cell grid behind. **And its three removals**: Spinning, Moving and Flashing each go with Directional now, on James's "spinning plus directional should be allowed" - a soft side that turns, wanders or blinks is a shot you time, which is what Directional is for. Three tests that pinned the old decisions were rewritten to state the new ones, which is the good kind of failure. **One I decided rather than read**: `[.moving, .portal]` joins the list, because `[.breathing, .portal]` already refuses on "a Portal's mouth is a fixed target or it cannot be aimed at" and a mouth that wanders is not one either. Gravity and Spinning stay allowed with a Portal on the same test - a faller comes to rest, and a spinner never leaves its cell. Worth James's eye. |
| **Diamond, the workbook's one new brick** | The first piece of the brick workbook, and the only thing in it that did not already exist somewhere. A rhombus, corner to corner: the one face in the game with no flat edge anywhere on it, so it is the one brick that never gives a shot back the way it came, whichever side the ball arrives from. It cost almost nothing, which is the point of round 148 having made the shapes a *style* rather than a fourth axis - four corners in `EndlessIIFaceGeometry`, a hiding rectangle, and a case in each of the five switches that name the faces. Everything else came free: the progression's introduction order reads `EndlessIIStyle.allCases`, the compatibility grid is a rule rather than a list, and the two geometry tests iterate `EndlessIIFace.allCases` - so Diamond was proved convex and proved to hide its sprite before a line of test was written for it. What it did need was its own two: **no edge is axis-aligned**, which is the whole of what the shape is and which a tidy-up of the corners could quietly undo, and **it is its own reflection in both axes**, which is what makes `makeFace`'s orientation roll harmless rather than something to exclude it from. It is a single convex body piece, like the dome and the wedge and unlike the notch. **The drawn face was checked by rendering it**, not reasoned about: the four reference icons side by side, dome, notch, wedge, rhombus. **One thing for James: `Diamond` is already the name of a planned cluster** (§4.10's table), an arrangement of many bricks rather than the outline of one. Both would appear on the reference page. The clusters are not built, so renaming either is still cheap, and the note is now in that table. |
| **The play-test list finished: the mirror reflects, the directionals stay hittable, the mode badge grows** | Three notes, and one that turned out to need no work. **The mirror wears the reflection of the paddle's shape** (James, round 233: "if the original paddle is wedge left, the mirror paddle should be wedge right"). It had been copying the paddle's own picture, which for the one pair of faces that is not symmetrical made it a duplicate rather than a mirror - two paddles sloping the same way, on the surface whose whole job is to be the opposite one. `Surface.mirrored` swaps the wedges and returns every other face unchanged, because convex and concave *are* their own reflections and Wave's was never drawn. The twin's body is traced from whatever picture it wears, so naming the reflected artwork is the whole of the change: the bounce mirrors itself. **Directional bricks keep a face the ball can reach.** Two gaps, and the second is the one that was actually showing. A brick whose depth sides were all blocked used to keep that pool anyway, so a brick with an Indestructible above and another below was handed one of those two faces with both sides standing open - it now reaches past the depth ramp for any open side, because an early side-facing brick is rarer than intended and an impossible brick is worse than rare. And a brick walled in on all four is no longer offered the role at all. But the check at birth cannot answer the common case: **the field descends and rows are generated above it**, so a brick is given its top face over an empty cell and an Indestructible is lowered into that cell a moment later. So the rule is enforced again after every new row, where the block happens - a brick that has become impossible is *turned*, keeping its type, colour and place while only the bright bar moves. Re-pointing a brick a player was aiming at is a real cost and it is the smaller one. **The mode badge is 84 on both in-game screens**, up from 56 on the level intro and 42 on the pause screen, and one constant rather than two magic numbers. **And the mirror already collected power-ups** - round 209 built it, contact-only so a drop is taken rather than bounced, and the wiring is still there. Nothing to do but say so. |
| **The aimed sticky, reworked** | Three notes, one power-up. **The arrow points at the finger** ("aimed sticky arrow should move relative to touch and drag gesture", and "a tap above the paddle moves the arrow to the tap position" - the same rule said twice: the shot goes where you are touching, however you got there). That replaces round 10's answer, which mapped the finger's x across the screen onto a fixed arc: absolute, which was the half round 10 had right, but with no relationship to where the ball was, so aiming at a brick meant learning the mapping rather than pointing at the brick. **Never flat and never downward**, held to the same minimum every bounce in the game is held to. **Where the tap was decides what it means**: above the paddle it points, below it fires, split by the same line that already divides aiming from carrying the paddle, so there is one boundary on screen rather than two. A tap used to fire wherever it landed, which made pointing and shooting the same gesture - so every attempt to adjust took the shot. **And the ball stops sliding on a shaped paddle**: the resting height was worked out once at setup from the plain paddle's, and a shaped paddle is taller and sits higher, so the ball was being placed inside the new silhouette. A body the engine finds inside another body is one it shoves out, every frame, in whatever direction the overlap suggests. The sliding was not the hold failing; it was the hold putting the ball somewhere the physics refused to leave it. Read off the live paddle now, so it is right for a shape, a resize or neither. |
| **The brick workbook, reviewed and answered - queued behind the play-test list** | James delivered `Giga-Ball 2026 - Brick Details.xlsx` and answered every question on it. **Only one thing in it is new: Diamond**, a shape whose every face is angled. Four brick types, four multi-hit levels, three sizes, nine actions and the wedge's four orientations are all built - the geometry has taken `mirrored` and `flipped` since it was written. **Shape and action become two axes** ("this was always the intention, I just didn't have it mapped out until now"), where `EndlessIIStyle` holds them in one list and refuses a shaped face to eight of the nine actions. Splitting them is the round everything else here waits on, and the cost is uneven: shape with Flashing, Exploding and Spawner is free today; with Gravity, Moving and Fixed it is cheap, because those decide where a brick sits and never touch its outline; with Spinning it means a traced body that rotates with the drawing, and with Breathing a traced body rebuilt at every size step. **Shape with Directional is disallowed outright** on James's call - "directional bricks are always the standard shape" - which is the one pair that would have needed hit detection against a non-rectangle. **The matrix is taken as written**: seven pairs to start refusing (Spinning with Fixed, Gravity, Exploding and Spawner; Breathing with Gravity and Spawner; Moving with Spawner) and three to stop refusing (Spinning, Moving and Flashing, each with Directional). **Spinning plus Directional is deliberate** - the open face rotates and the hit has to be timed. **Portal keeps a refusal list** rather than taking every action: it cannot be destroyed, so Directional contradicts it outright, and Exploding and Spawner both fire on damage it never takes. **Fixed gains teeth**: it destroys any brick that runs into it, so an anchor becomes a hazard to the field rather than only to the player. **Spawner draws 1 to 8**, both the count and which adjacent cells, per hit. Big and Tiny against the movement actions are built last and tested hardest, because a Big brick keeps its node on a row centre and hangs its body off-centre (§8.6). |
| **The landslide wraps, and three from the play-test list** | **Landslide is a conveyor** (James, round 231: "if a brick makes it to the bottom un-hit, it should wrap around back to the top. This way the level doesn't end once all the bricks vanish off the bottom"). That is better than the stopping rule of round 230 and settles a question Classic has never had to answer: the field cannot empty itself by falling past the paddle, and the only way to clear it is still to hit it. A wrapped brick is written straight to the top row and faded in, not glided - a brick travelling the height of the field would be between rows for most of a second, and a brick's `position.y` is its row (§8.6). **The shaped paddles stop distorting**, and round 214 was wrong about why they did. It forced the whole texture to stretch, reasoning that `paddleCapRect` is in the plain art's coordinates - but every shaped paddle is drawn at the *same width* as the plain one, all 75 across, and the cap rect only slices horizontally. The same fractions protect the same strip on all of them. What was distorting was the rounded ends stretching into ovals, which is exactly what round 201 fixed for the plain paddle the same way. The middle still stretches, which is right for all five: a wider dome is a wider, shallower dome. **A spent turn-based power-up gets a second before it goes** ("they look weird when they immediately change to a normal paddle when the ball bounces"). The last turn swaps the clock's turns for a second of ordinary time, so everything that asks "is it running" goes on saying yes: the shape holds, the ring keeps its icon, the effect keeps working. The ring stops being segmented for that second, because there are no turns left to mark. **Asked for rather than assumed**, and Descent is why - it counts rows, and a Descent running a second past its last row would stop being worth exactly its rows. **And the shaped HUD icons are wired**: the ring had been drawing the profile `PaddleBounce.shaped` traces since round 213, which is a fair picture of the old formula and no picture at all of the artwork the paddle wears. |
| **Always On and Landslide, the last two new twists** | **Always On is three lines rather than forty**, and that is the whole design: every lasting power-up already knows how to start itself, in the one switch that has mapped a power-up to its effect since 2020, so the twist asks "is it still running, and if not, collect it again" rather than teaching forty power-ups a second permanent mode - which would be forty chances to teach one of them wrongly. What is running is `activeRecentPowerUpIndices`, the same question the pause screen asks and answers for both halves of the game. The re-collection goes through `applyPowerUp` with a new `silently` flag: no sound, no haptic, no statistic, no count of power-ups on screen and no badge flying up, because none of those is true of a power-up that has simply not ended. **The pool is read off the duration column** ("timed or paddle hit based"), the daily's own bans apply before the draw so a permanent Lock is never offered, and Mayhem's own power-ups are excluded outside Mayhem. **The disallowed list is the game's own**: neither the standing power-up nor anything `EndlessIIExclusions` says would end it can fall, which is the round-223 table doing a second job. The bridge from that table's names to power-up indices is written out rather than derived from the case names, because the two have drifted once already - Ball Steering became Ball Control while the case stayed `ballControl` - and a mapping that guesses is one that is quietly empty next time. **Landslide steps the Classic field down a row every six seconds and stops** a paddle's gap above the paddle. It stops rather than kills on purpose: Mayhem's descent is pressure, not a death rule, and Classic has no lower limit - a landslide that kept going would push bricks through the paddle, and one that ended the run on arrival would be inventing a way to lose that this mode has never had. **That stopping rule is my call and wants James's play-test**, along with the six seconds. Quicksand does not fall on a Landslide day, per the workbook. Both twists sit in categories of one, because the matrix allows them beside everything except Vanilla. |
| **Monochromatic and Theme, and the middle ground the news twists had lost** | Two of the workbook's four new twists, in a `look` category of their own so they cannot happen together (James, round 229: "ok, they are not possible together then"). They could not join `dress`, because Fog of War lives there and a fogged day wearing a drawn theme is a pairing he does allow. **A theme is three settings** - ball, paddle and brick - so a theme twist owns all three, applied where the three are read rather than at each use: a twist that reached only the paddle would be a half-dressed game rather than a different one. Nothing is written to disk, so the next ordinary game reads the player's own choices. Monochromatic forces Classic; Theme draws from the date on its own seeded stream, the way `DailyBrickSwap` does, and never draws Classic - a twist announcing "one theme, chosen for you" that hands back the one already on screen is a twist that did nothing, and Classic is Monochromatic's whole answer besides. **And the news twists learned the middle** ("wipe can stay for daily challenge modes. It can be considered a neutral power-up as it depends what power-ups are enabled"). No Good News zeroed everything that was not *harmful*, which is not the same sentence as the workbook's: it took the neutral ones down with the good, so a Mystery could not fall on a bad-news day and a Wipe could fall on neither. Each twist bans one side by its multiplier chip now, and a power-up with no chip is banned by neither. Wipe has to be named, because its chip says -0.1 and on a No Good News day it is the kindest thing on the field; Mystery arrives on its own. Lose A Ball's blank chip is not neutrality, it is the game declining to put a number on it, and it stays harmful. **Still to build: Always On and Landslide.** |
| **The twist workbook's first pass, and retiring a twist becomes safe** | James delivered `Giga-Ball 2026 - Twist Details.xlsx` with twenty twists and a matrix of 190 cells. Sixteen of the twenty are built; four are new (Always On, Monochromatic, Theme, Landslide) and Classic Mayhem is parked on his instruction. **Vanilla was already built** and my review was wrong to list it as new: a day with no twists happens thirty per cent of the time and has been named and badged as Vanilla on the card, the pause screen and the between-levels screen since play-test round 3. **Loaded and Sudden Death retire** ("extra balls covers loaded", "sudden death is replaced by one life"), and **Extra Balls reaches every mode**: two more than the mode's own rack, which is a rack of two where there was none and two on top of the level's in Classic. **The workbook's Daily column becomes the overarching rule**, starting with the three that matter: Lose A Ball, Lock and Key never fall on a daily, because losing a ball for free is luck deciding a leaderboard and a Lock only a Key can end is a run that can be frozen for as long as the day lasts. Fog of War takes Show Bricks and Hide Bricks out of the table with it. **And retiring a twist is now a date, not a deletion.** Adding one was already safe through `activationKey`; taking one away was not, because a shorter pool changes what every past day rolled against. `retirementKey` closes that: before it the twist is in the pool exactly as it was, from it the pool is one shorter, so a change made after 1.3 ships cannot reach a day anybody has played. That is James's own requirement answered ("any changes to Daily Challenge mode cannot corrupt previous days"). **The golden record was re-pinned once, deliberately**: retiring Loaded moved two days that had drawn it, which is precisely what that test exists to catch, and it was allowed only because nothing has shipped yet. |
| **The mirror answers to the paddle's power-ups, and fires with it** | Round 225, on the matrix's Mirror Paddle row. **Inert and Flipped reach it now**: its bounce used an influence of a hard 1, which made the twin the one surface in the mode an Inert Paddle could not touch, and a power-up that switches off half the paddles reads as broken rather than as half-working. Auto-Aim and Ball Spin follow in the paddle's own order ("the ball is directed towards the aimed brick regardless of the paddle it bounces off"), and Random Bounce was already there through `ballHorizontalControl`. **Magnetism picks the nearer paddle** ("the ball is magnetised to the closest paddle and not to the other"): two magnets pulling one ball is a ball pulled to the point between them, which is the one place neither paddle is. A tie goes to the real paddle, because an arbitrary choice should be the one the player can do something about. **The twin fires lasers on the same beat**, copied from the laser just built rather than built again - so its texture, its theme and whether a Giga-Ball has made it pass through bricks are all the paddle's laser's, and nothing has to be told when any of that changes. Its offset is mirrored too, so a shot leaving the paddle's left edge leaves the twin's right. Not counted twice in the statistics and no second sound: one beat is one shot as far as the player's ears are concerned. ~~**Still owed on the twin**: sticky, aimed sticky and portal, and the split.~~ **Portal was built in round 285** on James's answer ("yes - a mirrored paddle gets its own portal, and both send the ball to the top"); what is left of this row is sticky, aimed sticky and the split, which is what the round-285 entry says at the top of this table. Original note: All three of those mean a second surface that can *hold* a ball, and the held-ball machinery is the paddle's alone - `ballIsOnPaddle`, the relative position, the tap that launches. A mirror that wore the sticky face and did not catch would be exactly the kind of lie round 211 refused, so it is a round of its own rather than a half of one. |
| **The safety paddle becomes a paddle, and shapes stop meeting Inert Paddle** | James, round 224: "go with the latest definition", against a matrix that gives the safety paddle the paddle's shape, its Inert Paddle, its Flipped Bounce Angle, its Auto-Aim, its Random Bounce and its Ball Spin. Round 211 called it furniture and answered with the backstop's arithmetic *on purpose*: the ball's own angle, reflected, with nothing about where it landed - and every one of those five needs the landing position back to have anything to act on, because a Flipped Angle with no angle to flip does nothing at all on the surface it is standing on. Its bounce is the paddle's sequence now, in the paddle's own order, so the two cannot drift: the shaped body first, then the bend, then the control, then the aim, then the grip. **Random Bounce needed no line**: `ballHorizontalControl` has applied it to every corrected bounce in the game since round 125. Two fixes came with it, both the ones the mirror needed in round 216 - the bar took the paddle's picture once at birth, so a shape collected while it stood left it wearing the plain face, and it watched `size` where Expand animates `xScale`. **And the shapes stop being offered against an Inert Paddle**, in either order ("do not fall when other power-up is active - the paddle is already inert when shaped"). Round 213 said no conflict rule was needed, because Inert set the angle influence to zero and nothing the shape said was heard - true while a shape was a formula, and untrue from the moment in that same round when it became a traced body, since the engine reflects the ball off the silhouette before the contact is even reported. An Inert Paddle under a shape is a bad power-up that does nothing; refusing to offer either is more honest than having one end the other, which would be two bad power-ups where the player collected one. |
| **What ends what: the matrix's fifteen overrides and its six cancellations** | "Most recent power-up overrides" is fifteen cells, and it is **not** the group model `PowerUpCatalogue.conflict` uses - it could not be. Inert Ball ends a Giga-Ball, a Wrecking Ball and a Ball Aura, and those three combine happily with each other; Auto-Aim ends a Portal, and a Portal runs contentedly beside an Inert Paddle. A group would have to claim conflicts the game does not have. So `EndlessIIExclusions` is the list of *pairs*, once, in the matrix's own order so the two can be read side by side, and the scene ends whichever half it did not just collect. Symmetric by construction, which is what "most recent" means. Two of the pairs reach outside Mayhem's clocks - Giga-Ball and Inert Ball are the original twenty-eight's, run as actions - and they end the way round 222 taught the Wipe to end them, by running the block their own wait would have run. The old hand-written displacements in Inert Paddle and Flipped Angle are gone, replaced by the same list. **The six cancellations were nearly all already true**: Slow against Increase Ball Speed, Expand against Shrink for the paddle and for the ball, all step back toward normal and have since 2020, and Retreat against Quicksand fell out of round 219's single field shift. The one that was not is **Drift**: round 201 made the opposite direction reverse the field and extend the clock, a dial rather than two coats of the same paint, which was a good answer to a question the matrix has since answered differently. It cancels now, by resetting the clock and letting the tick put the half-slid column back on its grid - stopping it by hand would leave the field between columns, and a brick between columns is a brick on no row at all (§8.6). |
| **A Wipe reaches them too, by running their own endings** | The other half of the matrix's three big rows. Every one of the original twenty-eight's timed power-ups was written the same way - a wait, then a block that puts everything back, run on the scene under a name - and `runClassicPowerUpTimer` is now that, plus one thing: **the ending block is kept**, under the same name, so something else can run it early. Ending a power-up before its time is doing exactly what its own ending does, and the Wipe now runs the very `SKAction` the wait would have run. **This deletes two hand-written copies.** `wipeGravity` and `wipeInertBall` reached two of the eleven by writing out what those blocks do, in the two places the game is oldest, and the other nine were not wiped at all. The eighteen call sites - eleven collections and seven resumes - go through the one helper, which is also how the collect path and the resume path stopped being able to disagree about what ending a power-up means. The Lock's table and the Wipe's are the same table, so a power-up cannot be frozen by one and left standing by the other. |
| **A Lock reaches the original twenty-eight** | The matrix's second structural answer, and the larger half of its three biggest rows: Lock, Key and Wipe each name forty-three power-ups, and the eleven timed ones from the original twenty-eight were in none of them. A player who locked a field full of power-ups watched half of them expire anyway. **These do not run on `EndlessIIClock`** - each is an `SKAction` sequence on the scene, a wait and then a block that puts everything back, with two more actions animating the icon and its bar - so freezing them is setting the speed of those three to zero, which is exactly what an `SKAction` means by frozen: the wait stops counting, the bar stops draining, and the bar goes on saying how much would be left. `endlessIIClassicTimers` is that list written once. **The freeze is written every frame** rather than at each end of a Lock, and that is the point rather than laziness: a power-up collected *during* a Lock starts a fresh action at full speed, and a freeze applied only when the Lock landed would miss it. It also sits outside the tick's playing guard, because a freeze is a state rather than a step - a pause taken inside a Lock would otherwise hand every old timer its speed back. **The Lasers keep firing while frozen**, and should: they fire from a `Timer` rather than from the action, and a Lasers that never runs out is what a Lock is for. **A Lock is worth dropping for an old power-up alone now**, where a field with nothing but a Giga-Ball running used to be a field a Lock declined to land on. ~~**Still owed: the Wipe's half of it.**~~ **Built in round 222** - `runClassicPowerUpTimer` registers each ending when the timer is scheduled and `wipeClassicTimers` runs all eleven early, which is exactly what this note asked for. It went unstruck for nine rounds and round 292 found it while picking the item up - and found `WipeEndsTheOldTimersTests` beside it, which has been proving all eleven end (`testTheLockAndTheWipeReadTheSameList`) since the round that built them. The coverage was there the whole time; only the note said otherwise. Original note: ending these early means running each power-up's own ending block, which at the time only Gravity and Inert Ball did, both by hand-copying what that block does. |
| **The matrix's first structural answer: collections reset rather than extend** | "Duration is reset" is what the matrix says for every power-up collected on top of itself, thirty-seven cells of it, and it reverses §5.4's default. Extending is what let three Drifts in a row spend half a minute sliding the field sideways: the collections compounded, and a power-up whose *length* stacks is a different power-up from one whose strength does. Resetting keeps a collection worth having - the clock is full again - without letting the good ones run away or the bad ones become a sentence. It is one line in `EndlessIIClock` plus the two vision clocks that count by hand, and **it aligns the two halves of the game**: the original twenty-eight have always cancelled their own timer and scheduled a fresh one, which is a reset written out longhand. Deepening is untouched, so stacking is still expressed as depth - "further ball speed decrease, duration is reset" is exactly what the code now does. Five tests inverted rather than deleted, and §5.4 rewritten to say reset with the reason beside it. |
| **Quicksand's second version, and the halo travelling with the field** | **One slot, two behaviours** (James, round 218: "Quicksand as 2 different versions... It should show up as the same power-up in the information view and use the same icons"). Classic's Quicksand is unchanged - the bricks move down and stay - and in Endless Mayhem it is temporary: the field steps two rows toward the paddle, is held there, and steps back. A second slot was never needed, and one slot is what makes the reference page show a single entry without anything being told to hide the other. **It is Retreat run backwards, through Retreat's own machinery.** `endlessIIRetreatFloorLift` becomes `endlessIIFieldShift`, and what it wants is now the sum of the two clocks: plus two rows while a Retreat runs, minus two while a Quicksand does. That gives the matrix's "cancels out" for the pair for nothing, because they are one number counted in opposite directions rather than two effects that have to be told about each other. The lower limit travels with the bricks either way, so what a Quicksand takes is *room* - exactly the room a Retreat gives. **Wants a play-test**: two rows closer is a real squeeze and the number is Retreat's rather than one chosen for this. **The original Endless does not get it yet**, and that is a gap against the workbook's Yes: the field-shift machinery is Mayhem's, it moves a lower-limit line that mode does not have, and porting it is its own round rather than a flag. **The halo travels with the field too** ("I mean that the halo tracks the field's vertical shift"), so it eats the same rows whatever else is running - during a Quicksand its centre is two rows below the paddle, and since the glow is a semicircle drawn upward it still covers the same two rows of bricks, which is the point. **The laser turrets needed nothing**: the overlay has scaled with the paddle since Expand was written. |
| **The design workbook becomes the source: names, descriptions and durations** | James delivered `Giga-Ball 2026.xlsx` on 22 August 2026 with a Power-Up Details sheet and a sixty-six by sixty-six interaction matrix, and answered twelve questions about it. Its rows turn out to be the reference page's own display order minus Jagged plus a second Quicksand, so every row maps to a slot with no guessing. **Fifteen names change**, rendered in the app's Title Case on his call: Fast Ball becomes Increase Ball Speed, Trajectory Line becomes Ball Trajectory, Portal Paddle becomes Portal, Paddle Halo becomes Halo, Ball Steering becomes Ball Control, Flipped Angle becomes Flipped Bounce Angle, Reversed Controls becomes Reversed Paddle Control, and Cull, Retreat, Aura, Infill and Descent each take a Brick or Ball prefix. Randomised Bounce becomes Random Bounce, and Wave Paddle goes back to Wavy Paddle. **Thirty-seven descriptions are replaced** with his shorter ones ("shorter is better"); the three places where the sheet has a slip - two "removes points *to* your score" and one "friction ... cause" - keep the game's own grammar. **The durations become uniform**: ten seconds for anything timed, five paddle hits for anything spent by a bounce. Retreat was eight, the Lock and Random Bounce fifteen, Ghost Ball and the Safety Paddle twelve, each set by its own play-test round and none of them still readable as a decision. Four power-ups change unit with it: Magnetism and the Halo move from hits to seconds because they act between bounces, the way Ball Steering did in round 15; Ball Trajectory moves from seconds to hits to match the Landing Marker it sits beside; and **the Lock loses its timer entirely** ("it is only stopped by Key"), which makes the Key the answer rather than a convenience, and its ring a full circle that never moves. **The workbook found four things the page was telling players wrongly**: the Landing Marker printed as ten seconds when it has counted paddle hits since round 215, Ball Control printed as five hits when it has run on seconds since round 15, and Retreat and Backstop printed no duration at all. **And one the HUD was drawing wrongly**: the ring marked every clock with its total, so Ball Steering wore ten marks for ten seconds. The marks follow whether the clock counts turns now, which meant making that flag true wherever it is true - most of the paddle batch was collecting through the seconds overload with a turns constant - and putting it back on restore, where only Ball Spin was doing so and a resumed run would have worn smooth rings. ~~**Still to come from the workbook**: the 447-cell interaction matrix, the endless Quicksand as Retreat's opposite number, the halo tracking the field's vertical shift, and the laser turrets widening with the paddle.~~ **Three of the four were built in the round immediately after this note and it was never struck.** The endless Quicksand and the halo travelling with the field are round 218's own row, two rows up this table; the laser overlay has scaled with Expand and Shrink for as long as they have existed (`paddleLaser.run(SKAction.scaleX(to:))`, three call sites). **Only the 447-cell interaction matrix is still owed.** |
| **What the Retreat change left behind, and one more pile-up to watch** | Round 215 inverted Retreat - it lifts and no longer clears - and left three copies of the old decision standing: the file header, the collect function's whole doc comment (which contradicted the inline note directly beneath it), and `endlessIIRaiseTheLowestBrickLevel(by:)`, the row-by-row clear itself, now called from nowhere in the app or the tests. All three are gone or corrected. Dead code that destroys bricks is the worst kind to leave lying about, because the next person to want "take the lowest rows" will find it and call it. The drawn Retreat badge needed no redrawing, only renaming: the horizontal line reads as the lower limit the arrow is retreating from just as well as it read as a row going. **Still open, and deliberately not changed: Laser Beam.** Three stutter reports in a row turned out to be a whole row of bricks destroyed inside one frame - Retreat's clear, the Paddle Halo's burst, and the aim freeze before them - and Laser Beam destroys a *column per ball*, through the full destroy path, with Exploding bricks in it free to cascade. That is more bricks in one frame than any of the three. It is left alone because a hitch at the moment a beam burns the field reads as impact rather than as jank, where the halo's recurred every time a row landed - but if a stutter is ever reported with Laser Beam in the ring, this is the first place to look. **Priced in round 295, and the judgement holds comfortably**: 0.21ms for one ball and **5.96ms for four** on a full field - a third of a frame at its very worst, on one frame, which does not drop one. (Four balls costs twenty-eight times one, not four: four beams take most of the field, so the cascades are most of what is left.) The number is here because round 291 showed what an unpriced judgement is worth - the ring HUD was costing 185% of a frame while everyone was looking elsewhere - and because the next person to weigh "reads as impact" against a cost should be weighing it against a measurement. `testWhatALaserBeamCostsOnTheFrameItFires`. |
| **The board comes into the run, and three things the shapes had left behind** | **Rival lines on the height markers**, the last item in the backlog and unblocked the day the Mayhem boards were approved. The field already draws a line every hundred metres and one at the player's own best; this adds the people. Which people is the whole design question, and the answer is **the ones just above you**: the board's leader is either unreachable or, on a board a day old, already behind, and either way it is a line that is never passed, while the three players immediately above the local rank are by definition within reach of the run being played. `GameCenterHandler.loadEndlessIIRivals` asks the rank, then the places above it - two calls, because a board cannot be asked "who is just above me" in one - and answers with an empty list for every ordinary failure, because the lines decorate a run that plays identically without them and a run interrupted to explain their absence is the worse outcome. `EndlessIIRivals` holds the rules and is where they are tested: a rival below where the run has got to is dropped, two players on one height share a line, a rival standing on the player's own best does not take that row, and a long display name is trimmed rather than shrunk. A rival's row is generated empty, the same as a best's, and wears a cool blue that is neither the player's lime nor the plain white of a hundred. **The board itself cannot be tested and has not been seen**: it needs other people to have played. **A resumed run wears the face it was paused in.** Which shape was running was the one part of the paddle batch the save did not carry - it came back domed whatever it had been - on the grounds that the format is shared with a shipped version. It needs no migration: every clock already saves a magnitude and this clock had never had a use for one, so the shape rides in it. The numbers are spelled out in `Surface.savedCode` rather than taken from `allCases`, because `allCases` follows the declaration order and a tidy-up that grouped the two wedges would silently turn every saved Wave into a Jagged; zero is left meaning "no shape recorded", so an older save resumes convex rather than resuming faceless with a running clock. **The mirror follows the paddle's scale, and traces the shape it wears.** Two bugs, one surface. Expand and Shrink animate `xScale` and never touch `size`, so the twin of an expanded paddle stayed the width it was born at - copied as a *scale* rather than folded into the size, because the real paddle is scaled and its rounded ends stretch with it. And round 211 gave the mirror the shaped face through `PaddleBounce.shaped`, which was right until round 213 stopped the shapes being formulas: from then on the paddle's dome was a traced silhouette and the mirror was a rectangle wearing a picture of one, showing a shape and giving a flat bounce. It traces the silhouette now and takes the engine's reflection as the answer, exactly as the paddle does, and the rebuild watches the artwork rather than the size because the two wedges are the same height as each other. |
| **Play-test round 215: the list, and the stutter behind three of it** | **Convex and concave had each other's picture** since round 213 - the old convex file is byte-identical to the new concave - and James's corrected art re-imported fixes both. **The drawn profile over the paddle is gone**, asked for twice: it existed to show a face that could only be guessed at, and the art shows it now, so a line drawn over a picture of the same curve is two answers to one question. **Retreat clears nothing** - it is the lift alone, which is the whole power-up on its own because the lower limit and every brick rise together, and it is called Retreat because that is what it does. **The aim no longer stops the world**: round 33 asked for a freeze and three rounds of stutter reports came out of it, most recently a freeze that stopped the scene but not `didSimulatePhysics`. The hold is a flag now, the field keeps descending, and a drag below the paddle always carries it - round 172's "two decisions on one finger" was an argument about a *stopped* world. **The descent is re-asked on resume**: the field's cadence is `countBricks` and everything that calls it is an event, so a pause taken mid-step left the move's flag true with nobody to clear it, until the next brick hit called `countBricks` and the field remembered itself - which is exactly the "until" in the report. **The landing marker's ring is segmented**, because it counts paddle hits and was drawing one continuous arc fed a fraction that only moves in steps. **The invisible-brick flash narrows to the row** and widens to both endless modes. **And the Paddle Halo eats a few bricks a frame, lowest first.** It destroyed every brick it touched on the same frame: one or two while it erodes a field it already overlaps, and eleven at once the moment a row descends into it, each running its role's reaction, its removal action, its scoring and its counters, with an Exploding brick in the burst taking its neighbours too. The same one-frame pile-up as Retreat's clear, and the third report in this list that was one. At 120fps two a frame still clears a row in well under a tenth of a second, so nothing about how it feels changes. |
| **The shaped paddle faces, rebuilt on the artwork (rounds 213 and 214)** | Supersedes "The shaped paddle faces, as built" further down this table, which described four faces drawn by formula and no artwork at all. James delivered forty-two imagesets - five shaped paddles, each with its own laser and sticky art, eleven HUD icon pairs and five badges - and **the body is the picture**: the paddle's body has always been `SKPhysicsBody(texture:size:)`, which traces the artwork's silhouette, so swapping the texture and rebuilding is all the engine needs to bounce off a dome or a dish. **With a shape running the formula stands down** - the engine has already reflected the ball off the silhouette by the time the contact is reported (§8.6), and that reflection *is* the answer, so `PaddleBounce.angleDegrees` and `ballHorizontalControl` are both skipped rather than layered on top. Three things still hold, because they are true of every bounce in the game: the run's own speed, never flat enough to run sideways for seconds, and never downward - a dome can reflect a ball down its own side, and a paddle that returns the ball into the floor is a shape that loses the run rather than one that makes it harder. **The bottom stays on its line**: each shape is drawn at the paddle's width and its own height, so the node rises by half the growth and the underside does not move, and the lift is given back before the next shape takes its own. The laser and sticky overlays needed no arithmetic at all - both are anchored at (0.5, 0) on the paddle's underside, so they grow upward from that line however tall they are, and a test pins those anchors because nothing else would notice them changing. **A resize retraces the body**: round 213 rebuilt only when the shape changed, so a resized paddle grew the sprite around a body that stayed the old width and the ball passed through the ends. The nine-slice went with it - `paddleCapRect` is in the plain art's unit coordinates and protects the wrong strips of a shaped picture, and a wider dome is the honest reading anyway. **Chasing that turned up something not established**: Expand and Shrink animate `paddle.xScale` and never touch `paddle.size`, so the retrace watches a number only Split Paddle and the setup move, and the shaped body is *not* retraced when a paddle expands. Whether that matters is unknown - a test measuring `SKPhysicsBody.area` across a scale proves nothing, because `area` is a construction-time value, and it was deleted rather than left to read as evidence. What is known is that the plain paddle's body is traced once at setup and Expand has scaled it for six years, so the whole game already rests on a traced body following its node's scale and the shapes are left resting on the same thing. What would show it failing, in play: an expanded paddle that does not bounce at its extreme ends - which would fail for every paddle, not only the shaped ones. **Wedge Left and Wedge Right are new**, taking the catalogue to sixty-six, and they break a rule on purpose: every face had to be odd, and a wedge is nothing but a bias.
